Wedge domains in compactly causal symmetric spaces
Karl-Hermann Neeb and Gestur Ólafsson

Abstract
Motivated by construction in Algebraic Quantum Field Theory we introduce wedge domains in compactly causal symmetric spaces M = G / H M=G/H , which includes in particular anti de Sitter space in all dimensions and its coverings. Our wedge domains generalize Rindler wedges in Minkowski space. The key geometric structure we use is the modular flow on M M defined by an Euler element in the Lie algebra of G G . Our main geometric result asserts that three seemingly different characterizations of these domains coincide: the positivity domain of the modular vector field; the domain specified by a KMS like analytic extension condition for the modular flow; and the domain specified by a polar decomposition in terms of certain cones. In the second half of the article we show that our wedge domains share important properties with wedge domains in Minkowski space. If G G is semisimple, there exist unitary representations ( U , ℋ ) (U,\mathcal{H}) of G G and isotone covariant nets of real subspaces 𝖧 ⁡ ( 𝒪 ) ⊆ ℋ {\sf H}(\mathcal{O})\subseteq\mathcal{H} , defined for any open subset 𝒪 ⊆ M \mathcal{O}\subseteq M , which assign to connected components of the wedge domains a standard subspace whose modular group corresponds to the modular flow on M M . This corresponds to the Bisognano–Wichmann property in Quantum Field Theory. We also show that the set of G G -translates of the connected components of the wedge domain provides a geometric realization of the abstract wedge space introduced by the first author and V. Morinelli.



Dedicated to the memory of Ottmar Loos

MSC 2010: Primary 22E45; Secondary 81R05, 81T05

1
Introduction

Let
M=G/H
be a homogeneous space of the Lie group
G

and
(U,\mathcal{H})
a unitary representation of
G
. Motivated by
their occurrence in Algebraic Quantum Field Theory (AQFT),
in the sense of Haag–Kastler (
[
Ha96
]
), one considers
nets of real subspaces on
M
, i.e., to each open subset
\mathcal{O}\subseteq M
, we assign a closed real subspace
{\sf H}(\mathcal{O})\subseteq\mathcal{H}

satisfying

•

Isotony:
\mathcal{O}_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{O}_{2}\Rightarrow{\sf H}(\mathcal{O}_{1})\subseteq{\sf H}(\mathcal{O}_{2})
,

•

Equivariance:
{\sf H}(g\mathcal{O})=U(g){\sf H}(\mathcal{O})
for
g\in G
.

If
(W(v))_{v\in\mathcal{H}}
are the Weyl operators on bosonic Fock space

\mathcal{F}_{+}(\mathcal{H})
, then we assign to each closed real subspace

{\tt E}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
the von Neumann algebra
\mathcal{R}({\tt E}):=W({\tt E})^{\prime\prime}

(
[
Si74
]
).
We thus obtain on
M
the isotone covariant net

(\mathcal{R}({\sf H}(\mathcal{O})))_{\mathcal{O}\subseteq M}
of von Neumann algebras, where
covariance refers to the canonical representation of
G
on
\mathcal{F}_{+}(\mathcal{H})
.
This method has been developed by Araki and Woods in the context of
free bosonic quantum fields (
[
Ar63
,
Ar64
,
AW63
,
AW68
]
),
but it can be modified to deal with fermionic Fock spaces
(cf.
[
EO73
]
,
[
BJL02
]
)
and there are variants for other statistics (anyons)
(
[
Schr97
]
,
[
Le15
, §3]
).

We are interested in structures on homogeneous spaces which
resemble space-times in AQFT. In this context the von Neumann
algebra associated to an open subset
\mathcal{O}\subseteq M
corresponds
to observables measurable in the “laboratory”
\mathcal{O}

(
[
Ha96
]
). One often focuses on those
von Neumann algebras
\mathcal{R}({\tt V})
for which the “vacuum vector”

\Omega\in\mathcal{F}_{+}(\mathcal{H})
is cyclic and separating, so that
the Tomita–Takesaki Theorem
applies to
(\mathcal{R}({\tt V}),\Omega)
(
[
BR87
, Thm. 2.5.14]
).
It is not hard to see that
\Omega
is

•

cyclic for
\mathcal{R}({\tt V})
if and only if

{\tt V}+i{\tt V}
is dense in
\mathcal{H}
,

•

separating for
\mathcal{R}({\tt V})
if and only if

{\tt V}\cap i{\tt V}=\{0\}
.

A closed real subspace
{\tt V}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
with both properties is called

standard
(cf.
[
Lo08
]
for the basic theory of standard subspaces).
It is therefore of particular interest to understand how to construct
nets for which the subspaces
{\sf H}(\mathcal{O})
are standard.
The main feature of Tomita–Takesaki theory is that it
provides a modular conjugation and a modular automorphism group,
and these can already be associated to a standard subspaces
{\tt V}\subseteq\mathcal{H}

(and pass through second quantization to the von Neumann context).
Concretely, we associate to
{\tt V}
a
pair of modular
objects

(\Delta_{\tt V},J_{\tt V})
:

•

the
modular operator

\Delta_{\tt V}
is a positive
selfadjoint operator,

•

J_{\tt V}
is a
conjugation
(an antiunitary
involution),

and these two operators satisfy the modular relation

J_{\tt V}\Delta_{\tt V}J_{\tt V}=\Delta_{\tt V}^{-1}.

The pair
(\Delta_{\tt V},J_{\tt V})
is obtained
by the polar decomposition
\sigma_{\tt V}=J_{\tt V}\Delta_{\tt V}^{1/2}
of the closed operator

\sigma_{\tt V}\colon{\tt V}+i{\tt V}\to\mathcal{H},\hskip 10.00002ptx+iy\mapsto x-iy

with
{\tt V}=\mathop{{\rm Fix}}\nolimits(\sigma_{\tt V})
. Key properties of these operators are that

J_{\tt V}{\tt V}={\tt V}^{\prime}:=\{w\in\mathcal{H}\colon(\forall v\in{\tt V})\ \mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits\langle v,w\rangle=0\}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ and }\hskip 10.00002pt\Delta_{\tt V}^{it}{\tt V}={\tt V}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ for }\hskip 10.00002ptt\in{\mathbb{R}}.

So we obtain a one-parameter group of automorphisms of
{\tt V}

(the modular group)
and a symmetry between
{\tt V}
and its commutant
{\tt V}^{\prime}
, implemented by
J_{\tt V}
.

The current interest in standard subspaces arose
in the 1990s from the work of Borchers
and Wiesbrock (
[
Bo92
,
Wi93
]
).
This led to the concept of modular localization
in Quantum Field Theory introduced by
Brunetti, Guido and Longo in
[
BGL02
,
BGL93
]
; see also
[
BDFS00
]

and
[
Le15
,
LL15
]
for important applications of this technique.

For a net on a homogeneous space
G/H
,
the domains
\mathcal{O}
for which
{\sf H}(\mathcal{O})
is standard
are of particular relevance. Here one would like to know when
the modular group
(\Delta_{\mathcal{O}}^{it})_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
is “geometric”
in the sense that it is implemented by a one-parameter subgroup of
G
, hence
corresponds to a one-parameter group of symmetries of
M

preserving the domain
\mathcal{O}
. For the modular
conjugation
J_{\mathcal{O}}
, we may likewise ask for the existence of an involution

\tau^{M}
on
M
satisfying
{\sf H}(\tau^{M}\mathcal{O})={\sf H}(\mathcal{O})^{\prime}
.
Building on
[
NÓ21a
]
, which deals with the case
H=\{e\}
,
i.e., left invariant nets on Lie groups,
we turn in this paper to nets on a class of symmetric spaces to
which the tools and methods developed in
[
NÓ21a
]
apply particularly well;
see also the companion paper
[
NÓ21b
]
.

First we explain how to find
natural standard subspaces for unitary representations.
So let
(U,\mathcal{H})
be a unitary representation of
G
which extends
to an
antiunitary representation
of the extended group

G_{\tau}=G\rtimes\{\mathop{{\rm id}}\nolimits_{G},\tau^{G}\},

where
\tau^{G}
is an involutive automorphism of
G
.
Then
J:=U(\tau^{G})
is a conjugation satisfying

JU(g)J=U(\tau^{G}(g))
for
g\in G
.
We then obtain for each pair

(h,\tau^{G})
, for which
h
is fixed by the Lie algebra
involution
\tau
induced by
\tau^{G}
, a standard subspace

{\tt V}:={\tt V}_{(h,\tau^{G},U)}=\mathop{{\rm Fix}}\nolimits(J_{\tt V}\Delta_{\tt V}^{1/2})
, specified by

J_{\tt V}=U(\tau^{G})\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ and }\hskip 10.00002pt\Delta_{{\tt V}}^{-it/2\pi}=U(\exp th)\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ for }\hskip 10.00002ptt\in{\mathbb{R}}.

(1)

This assignment is called the
Brunetti–Guido–Longo
(BGL) construction
(see
[
BGL02
]
).
As a consequence, standard subspaces can be associated to antiunitary
representations in abundance, but only a few of them
carry interesting geometric
information. In particular, we would like to understand
when a standard subspace of the form
{\tt V}_{(h,\tau^{G},U)}
arises from a
net on some homogeneous space
G/H
.

A structural property with strong impact in this context is the
spectrum condition

on the infinitesimal generators
\partial U(x)
of the one-parameter groups

(U(\exp tx))_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}
. This is the requirement that the closed convex cone

C_{U}:=\{x\in{\mathfrak{g}}\colon-i\partial U(x)\geq 0\}

(the
positive cone of
U
)
is “large” in the sense that the ideal
{\mathfrak{g}}_{C}:=C_{U}-C_{U}

satisfies
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{g}}_{C_{U}}+{\mathbb{R}}h
.
Let
{\tt V}={\tt V}_{(h,\tau^{G},U)}
be a standard subspace obtained from the BGL
construction as in (
1
). Then it is a natural requirement
that the semigroup

S_{\tt V}=\{g\in G\colon U(g){\tt V}\subseteq{\tt V}\}

(2)

of endomorphisms of
{\tt V}
is large in the sense that its Lie wedge

\mathop{\bf L{}}\nolimits(S_{\tt V})=\{x\in{\mathfrak{g}}\colon\exp({\mathbb{R}}_{+}x)\subseteq S_{\tt V}\}

(the set of infinitesimal generators of one-parameter subsemigroups of
S_{\tt V}
)
spans the Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
.
In
[
Ne21
]
it is shown that
if
\mathop{\bf L{}}\nolimits(S_{\tt V})
spans
{\mathfrak{g}}
, then

\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits h
defines a
3
-grading in the sense that

{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{g}}_{1}(h)\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}(h)\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}(h)\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ for }\hskip 10.00002pt{\mathfrak{g}}_{j}(h):=\ker(\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits h-j\mathop{{\rm id}}\nolimits_{\mathfrak{g}}),

(3)

and

\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(\tau^{G})=\tau_{h},

(4)

so that
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}(h)={\mathfrak{g}}^{\tau_{h}}
and
{\mathfrak{g}}_{1}(h)+{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}(h)={\mathfrak{g}}^{-\tau_{h}}
.
We call elements
h
with (
3
)
Euler elements

because they correspond to the Euler vector field on an open subset
of the homogeneous spaces with tangent space
{\mathfrak{g}}/({\mathfrak{g}}_{0}(h)+{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}(h))
.
Assuming that
h
is an Euler element and
\tau=\tau_{h}
,
the semigroup
S_{\tt V}
has been completely
determined in
[
Ne19
]
. To describe the structure of
S_{\tt V}
,
let

C_{\pm}:=\pm C_{U}\cap{\mathfrak{g}}_{\pm 1}(h)\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ and write }\hskip 10.00002ptG_{\tt V}=\{g\in G\colon U(g){\tt V}={\tt V}\}

for the stabilizer group of
{\tt V}
in
G
. Then

S_{\tt V}=\exp(C_{+})G_{\tt V}\exp(C_{-})=G_{\tt V}\exp(C_{+}+C_{-}).

(5)

Combining Euler elements with symmetric spaces
leads to the following concept:
A
modular causal symmetric Lie algebra
is a
quadruple
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C,h)
, where

•

({\mathfrak{g}},\tau)
is a
symmetric Lie algebra
, i.e.,

{\mathfrak{g}}
is a finite-dimensional real Lie algebra
and
\tau
an involutive automorphism of
{\mathfrak{g}}
.

•

({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C)
is a
causal symmetric Lie algebra
, i.e.,

C\subseteq{\mathfrak{g}}^{-\tau}
is a pointed generating
closed convex cone
invariant under the group
\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits_{\mathfrak{g}}({\mathfrak{h}})=\langle e^{\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits{\mathfrak{h}}}\rangle
.

•

h\in{\mathfrak{g}}^{\tau}
is an
Euler element
, i.e.,

\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits h
is diagonalizable with eigenvalues
\{-1,0,1\}
,
and the involution
\tau_{h}:=e^{\pi i\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits h}\in\mathop{{\rm Aut}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}})
satisfies

\tau_{h}(C)=-C
.

A causal symmetric Lie algebra
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C)
is called

compactly causal
(cc for short) if the cone
C
is
elliptic
, i.e.,
if its interior consists of elements
x
which are
elliptic

in the sense that
\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits x
is semisimple with purely imaginary spectrum.
Typical examples arise from invariant pointed generating cones
C_{\mathfrak{g}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{g}}
. Then

({\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}},\tau_{\rm flip},C_{\mathfrak{g}})\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ with }\hskip 10.00002pt\tau_{\rm flip}(x,y)=(y,x),

(6)

is a compactly causal symmetric Lie algebra; called
of group type (GT)
.
Another interesting class of compactly causal symmetric
Lie algebras arises from the involution
\tau_{h}=e^{\pi i\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits h}

defined by an Euler element. If
C_{\mathfrak{g}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{g}}
satisfies

-\tau_{h}(C_{\mathfrak{g}})=C_{\mathfrak{g}}
, then

({\mathfrak{g}},\tau_{h},C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{-\tau_{h}},h)
is a modular compactly causal
symmetric Lie algebra; called of
Cayley type (CT)
.

Irreducible compactly causal symmetric Lie algebras

({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C)
are either of group type with

{\mathfrak{g}}^{\tau}
simple hermitian, or
{\mathfrak{g}}
is simple hermitian, and

{\mathfrak{z}}({\mathfrak{k}})\subseteq{\mathfrak{g}}^{-\tau}
holds for a
\tau
-invariant
Cartan decomposition
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{k}}\oplus{\mathfrak{p}}
.
We refer to
[
HÓ97
]
for a classification.
The additional assumption that
{\mathfrak{h}}
contains an Euler element
leaves us with the following four types:

(GT)

Group type:
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{h}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}
,
{\mathfrak{h}}
simple hermitian
of tube type

{\mathfrak{h}}=\mathop{{\mathfrak{su}}}\nolimits_{r,r}({\mathbb{C}}),\ \ \mathop{{\mathfrak{sp}}}\nolimits_{2r}({\mathbb{R}}),\ \ \mathop{{\mathfrak{so}}}\nolimits_{2,d}({\mathbb{R}}),\ \ \mathop{{\mathfrak{so}}}\nolimits^{*}(4r),\ \ {\mathfrak{e}}_{7(-25)}.

(CT)

The Euler element
h
is central in
{\mathfrak{h}}
and
\tau=\tau_{h}
:

{\mathfrak{g}}

\mathop{{\mathfrak{su}}}\nolimits_{r,r}({\mathbb{C}})

\mathop{{\mathfrak{sp}}}\nolimits_{2r}({\mathbb{R}})

\mathop{{\mathfrak{so}}}\nolimits_{2,d}({\mathbb{R}}),d>2

\mathop{{\mathfrak{so}}}\nolimits^{*}(4r)

{\mathfrak{e}}_{7(-25)}

{\mathfrak{h}}

{\mathbb{R}}\oplus\mathop{{\mathfrak{sl}}}\nolimits_{r}({\mathbb{C}})

{\mathbb{R}}\oplus\mathop{{\mathfrak{sl}}}\nolimits_{r}({\mathbb{R}})

{\mathbb{R}}\oplus\mathop{{\mathfrak{so}}}\nolimits_{1,d-1}({\mathbb{R}})

{\mathbb{R}}\oplus\mathop{{\mathfrak{sl}}}\nolimits_{r}({\mathbb{H}})

{\mathbb{R}}\oplus{\mathfrak{e}}_{6(-26)}

(ST)

Split types:
\tau\not=\tau_{h}
and

{\rm rk}_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathfrak{h}}={\rm rk}_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathfrak{g}}
:
1
1

1

The real rank
\mathop{{\rm rank}}\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}}({\mathfrak{g}})

of a reductive Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
is the dimension
of a maximal abelian
\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits
-diagonalizable subspace.

{\mathfrak{g}}

\mathop{{\mathfrak{su}}}\nolimits_{r,r}({\mathbb{C}})

\mathop{{\mathfrak{so}}}\nolimits_{2,p+q}({\mathbb{R}})

\mathop{{\mathfrak{so}}}\nolimits^{*}(4r)

{\mathfrak{e}}_{7(-25)}

{\mathfrak{h}}

\mathop{{\mathfrak{so}}}\nolimits_{r,r}({\mathbb{R}})

\mathop{{\mathfrak{so}}}\nolimits_{1,p}({\mathbb{R}})\oplus\mathop{{\mathfrak{so}}}\nolimits_{1,q}({\mathbb{R}})

\mathop{{\mathfrak{so}}}\nolimits_{2r}({\mathbb{C}})

\mathop{{\mathfrak{sl}}}\nolimits_{4}({\mathbb{H}})

(NST)

Non-split types:
\tau\not=\tau_{h}
,
\mathop{{\rm rank}}\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathfrak{h}}=\frac{\mathop{{\rm rank}}\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}}{\mathfrak{g}}}{2}
:

{\mathfrak{g}}

\mathop{{\mathfrak{su}}}\nolimits_{2s,2s}({\mathbb{C}})

\mathop{{\mathfrak{sp}}}\nolimits_{4s}({\mathbb{R}})

\mathop{{\mathfrak{so}}}\nolimits_{2,d}({\mathbb{R}})

{\mathfrak{h}}

{\mathfrak{u}}_{s,s}({\mathbb{H}})

\mathop{{\mathfrak{sp}}}\nolimits_{2s}({\mathbb{C}})

\mathop{{\mathfrak{so}}}\nolimits_{1,d}({\mathbb{R}})

We introduce “wedge domains”
in compactly causal symmetric spaces
M=G/H
,
specified by a modular causal symmetric Lie algebra

({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C,h)
and a connected Lie group
G

with Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
on which
\tau

induces an involutive automorphism
\tau^{G}

for which
H\subseteq G^{\tau^{G}}
is an open subgroup.
We then obtain a one-parameter group

\alpha_{t}:=e^{t\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits h}
of automorphisms of
{\mathfrak{g}}
,
inducing automorphisms of
G
and
M
.

Let
V_{+}(gH):=g.C^{0}\subseteq T_{gH}(M)
denote the open cones
defining the causal structure on
M
and let

X_{h}^{M}\in\mathcal{V}(M)
be the
modular vector field
defined by

X_{h}^{M}(gH):=\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t=0}\alpha_{t}(g)H=\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t=0}\exp(th)gH.

(7)

We introduce the following domains associated to this data:

•

The
positivity domain of the vector filed
X_{h}^{M}
in
M
:

W_{M}^{+}(h):=\{m\in M\colon X_{h}^{M}(m)\in V_{+}(m)\}.

•

The
KMS wedge domain

W_{M}^{\rm KMS}(h):=\{m\in M\colon(\forall z\in\mathcal{S}_{\pi})\ \alpha_{z}(m)\in\mathcal{T}_{M}\},

where
(\alpha_{z})_{z\in{\mathbb{C}}}
denotes the holomorphic extension
of the modular flow to the complex symmetric space
M_{\mathbb{C}}

and

\mathcal{T}_{M}:=G\times_{H}iC^{0}

is the
tube domain of
M
, a complex manifold containing

M\cong G\times_{H}\{0\}
in its “boundary”.

•

The
polar wedge domain

W_{M}(h):=\bigcup_{m\in M^{\alpha}}\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{m}((C_{m}^{c})^{0}),

where
M^{\alpha}
is the submanifold of fixed points of the modular flow on
M
,

C_{m}:=\overline{V_{+}(m)}\subseteq T_{m}(M),

and

C_{m}^{c}:=C_{m}\cap T_{m}(M)_{1}-C_{m}\cap T_{m}(M)_{-1}\subseteq T_{m}(M),

where
T_{m}(M)_{j}
,
j=-1,0,1
denote the eigenspaces of the
generator of the modular flow on
T_{m}(M)
.

Our main geometric result, which is proved
after various preparations in Section
6

(Theorem
65
), asserts that
these three open subsets coincide:

W_{M}^{+}(h)=W_{M}^{\rm KMS}(h)=W_{M}(h).

For this result we assume
that the causal symmetric Lie algebra
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C)

is
extendable
, i.e., there exists a pointed closed convex invariant
cone
C_{\mathfrak{g}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{g}}
satisfying

C_{\mathfrak{g}}\cap{\mathfrak{q}}=C\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ and }\hskip 10.00002pt-\tau(C_{\mathfrak{g}})=C_{\mathfrak{g}}.

If
{\mathfrak{g}}
is reductive and
{\mathfrak{h}}
contains no non-zero ideals
(which is a very natural assumption as these ideals act trivially on
M
),
then
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C)
is extendable (Theorem
24
),
but there are also interesting non-reductive examples.
As we show in
[
NÓ21b
]
, the companion paper dealing with wedge domains
in non-compactly causal symmetric spaces,
the geometry of wedge domains in a non-compactly causal space
is more complicated because these domains
are generated from pieces of closed geodesics, the corresponding polar map
has singularities and it is defined only on a subset of an open cone.

To connect this geometric insight with nets of standard subspaces,
we show in our second main result (Theorem
75
)
that for any antiunitary representation

(U,\mathcal{H})
of
G_{\tau_{h}}=G\rtimes\{\mathbf{1},\tau_{h}^{G}\}

whose positive cone
C_{U}
is pointed and generating and satisfies

C=C_{U}\cap{\mathfrak{q}}
, every cyclic distribution vector

\eta\in\mathcal{H}^{-\infty}
fixed by
H
and the conjugation
J=U(\tau_{h}^{G})

leads to a natural covariant isotone net

{\sf H}^{M}
on
M
assigning to the connected component

W_{M}(h)_{eH}
of the wedge domain the standard subspace

{\tt V}_{(h,\tau^{G}_{h},U)}
. For classification results concerning this class
of representations, we refer to
[
KNÓ97
]
.

Our third main result (Theorem
812
) provides a concrete
construction of such representations and thus implies that
such nets exist on all reductive compactly causal modular symmetric spaces.
Starting with a
C_{\mathfrak{g}}
-positive antiunitary
representation
(\rho,\mathcal{K})
of
G_{\tau_{h}}
which is a
finite sum of irreducible ones, we obtain on
the space
B_{2}(\mathcal{K})
of Hilbert–Schmidt operators on
\mathcal{K}
a representation
by
U(g)A:=\rho(g)A\rho(\tau^{G}(g))^{-1}
and
the trace defines a distribution vector which generates a
subspace
\mathcal{H}_{\rho}
, so that Theorem
75
applies to

(U,\mathcal{H}_{\rho})
. We even obtain a rather direct description of

{\tt V}
as the closed real subspace generated by

\rho(C^{\infty}_{c}(S_{{\tt V},e}^{0},{\mathbb{R}}))\subseteq B_{1}(\mathcal{K})\subseteq B_{2}(\mathcal{K})
.
Here we use that
\rho
is a trace class representation
(see Section
8
for details).

Motivated by the analysis of abstract wedge spaces
in
[
MN21
]
, we prove in Section
9
that the wedge
space
\mathcal{W}(M,h)
of all
G
-translates of the connected component

W:=W_{M}(h)_{eH}
actually is a non-compactly causal parahermitian
symmetric space. It is a covering of the adjoint orbit

\mathcal{O}_{h}=\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(G)h\subseteq{\mathfrak{g}}
. The most difficult part of the proof
is to get hold of the stabilizer group
G_{W}=\{g\in G\colon gW=W\}
.
It is an interesting open problem to connect the covering theory
and the corresponding twisted duality developed in
[
MN21
]

to the geometry of coverings of compactly causal symmetric spaces
G/H
.

We conclude this paper with a section on the anti de Sitter space

\mathop{{\rm AdS}}\nolimits^{d}
, the prototypical Lorentzian example of a compactly causal symmetric space.
This section can be read independently.
For this example we evaluate all geometric data explicitly and show directly
that the three types of wedge domains coincide, without referring to the elaborate
structure theory used in the rest of the paper.

Structure of this paper:

We start in Section
2
by recalling basic facts on
causal symmetric spaces
on the Lie algebra level (Subsections
2.1
to

2.3
) and also on the global level
(Subsection
2.4
).

In Subsection
3.1
we classify for a
reductive compactly causal symmetric reductive Lie algebra
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau)

the orbits of
\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits_{\mathfrak{g}}({\mathfrak{h}})
in the set
of Euler elements in
{\mathfrak{h}}
. As this set describes the different choices
of the Euler element
h\in{\mathfrak{h}}
, this can be seen as a classification
of all “modular structures” on the associated symmetric spaces.
In Subsection
3.2
we show that the orbits
of the centralizer group
G^{h}
in
M^{\alpha}
are open and that this
leads to a natural bijection between the orbit spaces
M^{\alpha}/G^{h}

and
(\mathcal{O}^{h}\cap{\mathfrak{h}})/H
. Actually both sets correspond to the set of
double cosets
G^{h}gH\subseteq G
for which
\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)^{-1}h\in{\mathfrak{h}}
.

In Section
4
we introduce the three types of
wedge domains in a compactly causal symmetric space
M=G/H
.
In our analysis of wedge domains we
follow the strategy to first study spaces of group
type and then use embeddings into these spaces to derive corresponding
results in general. In Section
5
we start with
causal symmetric spaces of group type, i.e., pairs

(G,C_{\mathfrak{g}})
of a connected Lie group
G
and a
pointed generating invariant cone
C_{\mathfrak{g}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{g}}
.

In Section
6
we turn to wedge domains in compactly
causal symmetric spaces. In this context we
assume that
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C)
is extendable in the sense of
Subsection
2.1
, keeping in mind that this is always
the case if
{\mathfrak{g}}
is reductive. The main result of this section
is Theorem
65
, asserting that the three wedge domains in
M
coincide.
This is first proved for the special case

H=G^{\tau^{G}}
in Theorem
63
.
The proof of this theorem builds heavily on the group case
(Theorem
52
). We conclude this section with a brief
discussion of the assumption that the Euler element
h
is contained
in
{\mathfrak{h}}
, i.e., that the corresponding modular flow on
M
has a fixed point.
Note that the definition of
W_{M}(h)
makes no sense if
M^{\alpha}=\emptyset
.

In Section
7
we turn to representation theoretic aspects of
compactly causal symmetric spaces. We start with
introducing standard subspaces, the Brunetti–Guido–Longo construction
and recall the construction of nets of closed real subspaces
from distribution vectors, introduced in
[
NÓ21a
]
.
In Subsection
7.4
we describe in the general
Theorem
75
how the methods from
[
NÓ21a
]
can be used
to construct covariant nets of standard subspaces on
compactly causal symmetric spaces.
In Section
8
we construct such representations explicitly
in spaces of Hilbert–Schmidt operators
\mathcal{H}_{\rho}\subseteq B_{2}(\mathcal{K})
, where

(\rho,\mathcal{K})
is an antiunitary representation of
G_{\tau^{G}_{h}}
which is a
finite sum of irreducible representations. This is done in three steps:
First we recall from
[
Ne00
,
Ne19
]
some results on
the representations
(\rho,\mathcal{K})
of
G_{\tau_{h}}=G\rtimes\{\mathbf{1},\tau^{G}_{h}\}
,
then we use these representations to construct nets of standard subspaces
on the symmetric space of group type
G\cong(G\times G)/\Delta_{G}
,
and finally we use the twisted embedding

G\to G\times G,g\mapsto(g,\tau^{G}(g))
to obtain by
pullbacks representations of
G_{\tau_{h}}
that can be used to obtain with
Theorem
75
nets of standard subspaces on
M=G/H
.

In Section
9
we return to a geometric topic.
We show that, under rather natural assumptions,
the wedge space
\mathcal{W}(M,h)
of all
G
-translates
of the connected component
W_{M}(h)_{eH}
of the wedge domain in
M

carries the structure of an ordered symmetric space.
We conclude this paper with a short section discussing
some open problems and two appendices on some useful facts on
symmetric Lie groups and on distribution vectors of unitary representations.

Notation:

•

{\mathfrak{g}}
,
{\mathfrak{h}}
and
{\mathfrak{k}}
will denote Lie algebras
of the Lie groups
G
,
H
,
K
.

•

An element
h\in{\mathfrak{g}}
is called an
Euler element
if

\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits h
is non-zero and diagonalizable
with
{\rm Spec}(\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits h)\subseteq\{-1,0,1\}
. The set of Euler elements in
{\mathfrak{g}}
is
denoted by
\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}})
.

•

For
h\in\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}})
we write

\tau_{h}:=e^{\pi i\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits h}
for the corresponding involution on
{\mathfrak{g}}
and
note that
\kappa_{h}:=e^{-\frac{\pi i}{2}\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits h}
is an automorphism of

{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbb{C}}
of order
4
with
\kappa_{h}^{2}=\tau_{h}
which
implements the “Wick rotation” (partial Cayley transform).

•

For
h\in{\mathfrak{g}}
,
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
, and
{\mathfrak{l}}\subset{\mathfrak{g}}
an
\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits h
invariant
subspace we write

{\mathfrak{l}}_{\lambda}(h):=\ker(\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits h|_{{\mathfrak{l}}}-\lambda\mathbf{1})
for the corresponding eigenspace
in the adjoint representation.

•

We write
e\in G
for the identity element in the Lie group
G
,

G_{e}
for its identity component and
Z(G)
for its center.

•

For the left and right translations on the tangent bundle
T(G)
,
we write
g.v
and
v.g
for
g\in G,v\in T(G)
, respectively.

•

For
x\in{\mathfrak{g}}
, we write
G^{x}:=\{g\in G\colon\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)x=x\}

for the stabilizer of
x
in the adjoint representation
and
G^{x}_{e}:=(G^{x})_{e}
for its identity component.

•

If
\tau^{G}\in\mathop{{\rm Aut}}\nolimits(G)
is an involution, then
we write
G_{\tau}:=G\rtimes\{\mathop{{\rm id}}\nolimits_{G},\tau^{G}\}
,

g^{\sharp}:=\tau^{G}(g)^{-1}
, and
G^{\sharp}:=\{g\in G\colon g^{\sharp}=g\}
.

•

For a Lie subalgebra
{\mathfrak{h}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{g}}
, we write

\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits_{\mathfrak{g}}({\mathfrak{h}})=\langle e^{\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits{\mathfrak{h}}}\rangle\subseteq\mathop{{\rm Aut}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}})
for the corresponding group of inner
automorphism and put
\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}}):=\langle e^{\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits{\mathfrak{g}}}\rangle
.

•

For a (continuous) unitary representation
(U,\mathcal{H})
of a Lie group

G
with Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
, we write

\partial U(x)
for the skew-adjoint infinitesimal generator of
the unitary one-parameter group

U(\exp tx)
, so that we have

U(\exp tx)=e^{t\partial U(x)}
for
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
The
positive cone of
U
is the
\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(G)
-invariant closed convex cone

C_{U}:=\{x\in{\mathfrak{g}}\colon-i\partial U(x)\geq 0\}.

(8)

•

For a complex Hilbert space, we write
\mathop{{\rm AU}}\nolimits(\mathcal{H})
for the
group of unitary and antiunitary operators.

•

For a function
f\colon G\to{\mathbb{C}}
, we write

f^{\vee}(g):=f(g^{-1})
.

2
Causal symmetric Lie algebras

In this section we collect some basic facts and definitions concerning
causal symmetric spaces. Our main reference for these spaces is
[
HÓ97
]
for the
semisimple situation and
[
HN93
]
for the general case.

Definition 21
.

For a symmetric Lie algebra
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau)
, we write

{\mathfrak{h}}:={\mathfrak{g}}^{\tau}=\{x\in{\mathfrak{g}}\colon\tau(x)=x\}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ and }\hskip 10.00002pt{\mathfrak{q}}:={\mathfrak{g}}^{-\tau}=\{x\in{\mathfrak{g}}\colon\tau(x)=-x\}.

If
{\mathfrak{g}}
is semisimple, then
there exists a Cartan involution
\theta
commuting with
\tau
,
and all these Cartan involutions are mutually conjugate under

\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits_{\mathfrak{g}}({\mathfrak{h}})
(
[
KN96
, Prop. I.5(iii)]
).
If
\theta
is a Cartan involution commuting with
\tau
,
then we write
{\mathfrak{k}}:={\mathfrak{g}}^{\theta}
and
{\mathfrak{p}}:={\mathfrak{g}}^{-\theta}
for the

\theta
-eigenspaces. Now

{\mathfrak{g}}
decomposes as follows into simultaneous
(\tau,\theta)
-eigenspaces:

{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{h}}\oplus{\mathfrak{q}}={\mathfrak{k}}\oplus{\mathfrak{p}}={\mathfrak{h}}_{\mathfrak{k}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}_{\mathfrak{p}}\oplus{\mathfrak{q}}_{\mathfrak{k}}\oplus{\mathfrak{q}}_{\mathfrak{p}}

(9)

with

{\mathfrak{h}}_{\mathfrak{k}}:={\mathfrak{h}}\cap{\mathfrak{k}},\hskip 10.00002pt{\mathfrak{h}}_{\mathfrak{p}}:={\mathfrak{h}}\cap{\mathfrak{p}},\hskip 10.00002pt{\mathfrak{q}}_{\mathfrak{k}}:={\mathfrak{q}}\cap{\mathfrak{k}},\hskip 10.00002pt{\mathfrak{q}}_{\mathfrak{p}}:={\mathfrak{q}}\cap{\mathfrak{p}}.

We call

({\mathfrak{g}}^{c},\tau^{c})
with
{\mathfrak{g}}^{c}={\mathfrak{h}}+i{\mathfrak{q}}
and

\tau^{c}(x+iy)=x-iy
for
x\in{\mathfrak{h}},y\in{\mathfrak{q}}
, the
c
-dual
symmetric Lie algebra
.

Definition 22
.

Let
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau)
be a symmetric Lie algebra.

2
2

2

Here we extend concepts typically used for irreducible semisimple
symmetric Lie algebras to a more general context. This is motivated by
our heavy use of embeddings, which is most nicely done in a suitable
functorial framework.

(a)

A triple
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau_{h},h)
is called a
parahermitian
symmetric Lie algebra
if
h\in\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}})
is an Euler element and

\tau_{h}=e^{\pi i\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits h}
.

(b)

A triple
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C)
is called a
compactly causal (cc) symmetric Lie algebra
if

C\subseteq{\mathfrak{q}}
is a pointed generating elliptic
cone invariant under
\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits_{\mathfrak{g}}({\mathfrak{h}}):=\langle e^{\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits{\mathfrak{h}}}\rangle
.

(c)

A triple
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C)
is called a
non-compactly causal (ncc) symmetric Lie algebra
if

C\subseteq{\mathfrak{q}}
is a pointed generating hyperbolic
cone invariant under
\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits_{\mathfrak{g}}({\mathfrak{h}})
, i.e., the interior of
C
consists of

\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits
-diagonalizable elements.

(d)

A triple
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C)
is called a
causal symmetric Lie algebra
if it is either a cc or an ncc symmetric Lie algebra.

(e)

A
modular causal symmetric Lie algebra
is a
quadruple
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C,h)
, where

({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C)
is a causal symmetric Lie algebra,

h\in{\mathfrak{g}}^{\tau}
is an Euler element,
and the involution
\tau_{h}=e^{\pi i\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits h}\in\mathop{{\rm Aut}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}})
satisfies

\tau_{h}(C)=-C
.

Note that the
c
-dual of a cc symmetric Lie algebra
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C)
is
ncc and vice versa.

For the classification
of
\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{h}})
-invariant elliptic cones
C\subseteq{\mathfrak{q}}
, i.e., causal structures on the symmetric Lie algebra

({\mathfrak{g}},\tau)
,
see Sections 4.4 and 4.5 in
[
HÓ97
]
and
[
KN96
, Thm. X.3]
. Note that the
classification in the cc case and ncc case are the same via
c
-duality.

Remark 23
.

Suppose that
C\subseteq{\mathfrak{q}}
is a closed convex
\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits_{\mathfrak{g}}({\mathfrak{h}})
-invariant
cone with
-\tau_{h}(C)=C
. Since
\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits h
preserves

{\mathfrak{q}}
, the space
{\mathfrak{q}}
decomposes as

{\mathfrak{q}}={\mathfrak{q}}_{-1}(h)\oplus{\mathfrak{q}}_{0}(h)\oplus{\mathfrak{q}}_{1}(h).

As
C
is invariant under
e^{{\mathbb{R}}\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits h}
and
-\tau_{h}
, we see that

x=x_{1}+x_{0}+x_{-1}\in C
with
x_{j}\in{\mathfrak{q}}_{j}(h)
implies

e^{t\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits h}x=e^{t}x_{1}+x_{0}+e^{-t}x_{-1}\in C.

This shows that
x_{\pm 1}=\lim_{t\to\infty}e^{-t}e^{\pm t\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits h}x\in C
.
Therefore the two
closed convex cones

C_{\pm}:=\pm C\cap{\mathfrak{q}}_{\pm 1}(h)

(10)

satisfy

p_{{\mathfrak{q}}^{-\tau_{h}}}(C)=C^{-\tau_{h}}=C_{+}-C_{-}.

(11)

Furthermore, the automorphism
\kappa_{h}=e^{-\frac{\pi i}{2}\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits h}\in\mathop{{\rm Aut}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbb{C}})

takes the form

\kappa_{h}(z_{-1}+z_{0}+z_{1})=iz_{-1}+z_{0}-iz_{1}

with respect to the
3
-grading defined by
\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits h
and
we have

i\kappa_{h}(C^{-\tau_{h}})=C_{+}+C_{-}=:C^{c}

(12)

2.1
Embedding into group type

In this section we discuss the embedding of compactly causal triples

({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C)
into a natural causal triple of group
type
(({\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}})_{C},\tau_{\rm flip},C_{\mathfrak{g}})
. This embedding will play
a crucial role in the following.

We call a compactly causal symmetric Lie algebra

({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C)

extendable
if there exists a
pointed
closed convex invariant
cone
C_{\mathfrak{g}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{g}}
satisfying

C_{\mathfrak{g}}\cap{\mathfrak{q}}=C\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ and }\hskip 10.00002pt-\tau(C_{\mathfrak{g}})=C_{\mathfrak{g}}.

We then call
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C_{\mathfrak{g}})
an
extension of
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C
)
.
The following theorem (
[
KN96
, Thm. X.7]
,
[
HÓ97
, Thm. 4.5.8]
,

[
Ó91
, Thm. 7.8]
) shows that, for reductive Lie algebras,
extensions always exist, even with generating cones
C_{\mathfrak{g}}
.

Theorem 24
.

(Extension Theorem)

Let
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C)
be a reductive compactly causal symmetric Lie algebra
for which
{\mathfrak{h}}
contains no non-zero ideals of
{\mathfrak{g}}
.
Then there exists a pointed generating invariant closed
convex cone
C_{\mathfrak{g}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{g}}
with

-\tau(C_{\mathfrak{g}})=C_{\mathfrak{g}}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ and }\hskip 10.00002ptC_{\mathfrak{g}}\cap{\mathfrak{q}}=C.

(13)

Suppose that
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C_{\mathfrak{g}})
is an extension
of the compactly causal symmetric Lie algebra
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C)
.
If
C_{\mathfrak{g}}
is not generating, then

{\mathfrak{g}}_{C}:=C_{\mathfrak{g}}-C_{\mathfrak{g}}\trianglelefteq{\mathfrak{g}}

is a
\tau
-invariant ideal containing
C-C={\mathfrak{q}}
, so that

{\mathfrak{g}}_{C}={\mathfrak{h}}_{C}\oplus{\mathfrak{q}}
for
{\mathfrak{h}}_{C}={\mathfrak{h}}\cap{\mathfrak{g}}_{C}
.
Then

({\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}})_{C}=\{(x,y)\in{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}\colon x-y\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{C}\}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ with }\hskip 10.00002pt\tau_{e}(x,y)=(y,x)

is a symmetric Lie algebra with

({\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}})_{C}^{\tau_{e}}=\{(x,x)\colon x\in{\mathfrak{g}}\}\cong{\mathfrak{g}}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt({\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}})_{C}^{-\tau_{e}}=\{(x,-x)\colon x\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{C}\}\cong{\mathfrak{g}}_{C}.

We thus obtain an embedding of causal symmetric Lie algebras

({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C)\hookrightarrow(({\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}})_{C},\tau_{e},C_{\mathfrak{g}}),\hskip 10.00002ptx\mapsto(x,\tau x)\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ for }\hskip 10.00002ptx\in{\mathfrak{g}}.

(14)

This embedding is also compatible with the corresponding Euler elements.
Any Euler element
h\in{\mathfrak{h}}={\mathfrak{g}}^{\tau}
is mapped
to the Euler element
(h,h)\in({\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}})_{C}^{\tau_{e}}
.

On the global level, the embedding
(
14
) corresponds to the
quadratic representation of the symmetric space
G/H
in the group

G_{C}
with Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}_{C}
(see (
24
) below).
We shall use such embeddings to derive
properties of compactly causal symmetric spaces from those of
spaces of group type.

Since
\tau
commutes with
\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits h
on
{\mathfrak{g}}
, it preserves all eigenspaces

{\mathfrak{g}}_{j}(h)
,
j=-1,0,1
.
We conclude from (
13
) that the cones

C_{{\mathfrak{g}},\pm}:=\pm C_{\mathfrak{g}}\cap{\mathfrak{g}}_{\pm 1}(h)

satisfy
-\tau(C_{{\mathfrak{g}},\pm})=C_{{\mathfrak{g}},\pm}
, which shows in particular that
the cone

C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{c}:=C_{{\mathfrak{g}},+}+C_{{\mathfrak{g}},-}

(15)

is
-\tau
-invariant with

(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{c})^{-\tau}=(C_{{\mathfrak{g}},+}+C_{{\mathfrak{g}},-})^{-\tau}=C_{+}+C_{-}=C^{c}.

(16)

Remark 25
.

In
[
KN96
, Ex. X.4]
one finds examples of
compactly causal symmetric Lie algebras
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C)

which are not extendable, but
[
KN96
, Thm. X.7]
describes sufficient
conditions for general Lie algebras that cover all cases where

C_{\mathfrak{g}}
is generating.

We also note that, if
{\mathfrak{g}}
is not reductive
and
C_{\mathfrak{g}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{g}}
is a pointed generating invariant cone,
then
C_{\mathfrak{g}}\cap{\mathfrak{z}}({\mathfrak{g}})\not=\{0\}
. As
\tau_{h}
fixes the center
pointwise, this condition is incompatible with

-\tau_{h}(C_{\mathfrak{g}})=C_{\mathfrak{g}}
. Therefore it would be too much to hope
for extensions of modular compactly causal symmetric Lie algebras

({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C,h)
for which
C_{\mathfrak{g}}
is also generating.
We refer to
[
Oeh20
,
Oeh20b
]

for more details on the structure of non-reductive modular
compactly causal symmetric Lie algebras
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C,h)

and corresponding classification results.

Example 26
.

(A non-reductive example; cf.
[
Ne19
, Ex. 3.7]
)
We consider the Lie algebra

{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{hcsp}}(V,\omega):=\mathfrak{heis}(V,\omega)\rtimes{\mathfrak{csp}}(V,\omega),

where

(V,\omega)
is a symplectic vector space,

\mathfrak{heis}(V,\omega)={\mathbb{R}}\oplus V
is the corresponding Heisenberg algebra
with the bracket
[(z,v),(z^{\prime},v^{\prime})]=(\omega(v,v^{\prime}),0)
, and

{\mathfrak{csp}}(V,\omega):=\mathfrak{sp}(V,\omega)\oplus{\mathbb{R}}\mathop{{\rm id}}\nolimits_{V}

is the
conformal symplectic Lie algebra
of
(V,\omega)
.

The hyperplane ideal
{\mathfrak{j}}:=\mathfrak{heis}(V,\omega)\rtimes\mathfrak{sp}(V,\omega)

(the
Jacobi algebra
)
can be identified by the linear isomorphism

\varphi\colon{\mathfrak{j}}\to\mathop{{\rm Pol}}\nolimits_{\leq 2}(V),\hskip 20.00003pt\varphi(z,v,x)(\xi):=z+\omega(v,\xi)+\frac{1}{2}\omega(x\xi,\xi),\hskip 10.00002pt\xi\in V

with the Lie algebra of real polynomials

\mathop{{\rm Pol}}\nolimits_{\leq 2}(V)
of degree
\leq 2
on
V
,
endowed with the Poisson bracket (
[
Ne00
, Prop. A.IV.15]
).
The set

C:=\{f\in\mathop{{\rm Pol}}\nolimits_{\leq 2}(V)\colon f\geq 0\}

is a pointed invariant cone in
{\mathfrak{g}}
generating the ideal
{\mathfrak{g}}_{C}={\mathfrak{j}}
.
The element
h_{0}:=\mathop{{\rm id}}\nolimits_{V}
defines a derivation on
{\mathfrak{j}}
by

(\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits h_{0})(z,v,x)=(2z,v,0)
for
z\in{\mathbb{R}},v\in V,x\in\mathfrak{sp}(V,\omega)
.
Any involution
\tau_{V}
on
V
satisfying
\tau_{V}^{*}\omega=-\omega

defines by

\widetilde{\tau}_{V}(z,v,x):=(-z,\tau_{V}(v),\tau_{V}x\tau_{V})

(17)

an involution on
{\mathfrak{g}}
with
\widetilde{\tau}_{V}(h_{0})=h_{0}
,
and
-\widetilde{\tau}_{V}(C)=C
follows from

\varphi(\widetilde{\tau}_{V}(z,v,x))=-\varphi(z,v,x)\circ\tau_{V}.

Considering
\tau_{V}
as an element of
\mathfrak{sp}(V,\omega)
,
we obtain an Euler element

h:={\textstyle{\frac{1}{2}}}(\mathop{{\rm id}}\nolimits_{V}+\tau_{V})\in{\mathfrak{csp}}(V,\omega).

Writing
V=V_{1}\oplus V_{-1}
for
the
\tau_{V}
-eigenspace decomposition, the
\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits h
-eigenspaces in
{\mathfrak{g}}
are

{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}=0\oplus 0\oplus\mathfrak{sp}(V,\omega)_{-1},\hskip 10.00002pt{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}=0\oplus V_{-1}\oplus\mathfrak{sp}(V,\omega)_{0}\cong V_{-1}\rtimes\mathop{{\mathfrak{gl}}}\nolimits(V_{-1}),\hskip 10.00002pt{\mathfrak{g}}_{1}={\mathbb{R}}\oplus V_{1}\oplus\mathfrak{sp}(V,\omega)_{1}.

The eigenspace
{\mathfrak{g}}_{1}
can be identified with the space

\mathop{{\rm Pol}}\nolimits_{\leq 2}(V_{-1})
of polynomials of
degree
\leq 2
on
V_{-1}
and

C_{+}=C\cap{\mathfrak{g}}_{1}=\{f\in\mathop{{\rm Pol}}\nolimits_{\leq 2}(V_{-1})\colon f\geq 0\}.

This cone is invariant
under the natural action of the
affine group

G^{0}\cong\mathop{{\rm Aff}}\nolimits(V_{-1})_{e}\cong V_{-1}\rtimes\mathop{{\rm GL}}\nolimits(V_{-1})_{e}

whose Lie algebra is
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
. We also note that

{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}=\mathfrak{sp}(V,\omega)_{-1}\cong\mathop{{\rm Pol}}\nolimits_{2}(V_{1})\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ and }\hskip 10.00002ptC_{-}=-C\cap{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}=\{f\in\mathop{{\rm Pol}}\nolimits_{2}(V_{1})\colon f\leq 0\}.

Finally, we observe that
\tau_{h}=e^{\pi i\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits h}=(-\tau_{V})\,\widetilde{}
implies in particular
-\tau_{h}(C)=C
, so that

({\mathfrak{g}},\tau_{h},C^{c},h)\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ with }\hskip 10.00002ptC^{c}=C_{+}-C_{-}=C^{-\tau_{h}}

is a modular causal symmetric Lie algebra for which

({\mathfrak{g}},\tau_{h},C^{c})
is compactly causal and extendable to
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau_{h},C)
.

2.2
Hermitian simple Lie algebras

A real simple Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
contains a
pointed generating invariant cone
C_{\mathfrak{g}}
if and only if

{\mathfrak{g}}
is hermitian (
[
Vi80
]
). If this is the case, then
there exist two such cones
C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\rm min}\subseteq C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\rm max}
with the property
that
any other pointed generating invariant cone
C_{\mathfrak{g}}
satisfies

C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\rm min}\subseteq C_{\mathfrak{g}}\subseteq C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\rm max}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ or }\hskip 10.00002ptC_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\rm min}\subseteq-C_{\mathfrak{g}}\subseteq C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\rm max}.

(18)

We start by collecting some information on the relevant class of simple
Lie algebras.

Proposition 27
.

Let
{\mathfrak{g}}
be a simple hermitian Lie algebra. Then

{\mathfrak{g}}
contains an Euler element if and only if
{\mathfrak{g}}
is of tube type.
If this is the case, then the following assertions hold:

(a)

\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}})
acts transitively on the set
\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}})
of Euler elements.

(b)

If
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C)
is compactly causal , then
\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}})\cap{\mathfrak{h}}\not=\emptyset
.

(c)

For every Euler element
h\in{\mathfrak{g}}
,
the cone
C^{\rm max}_{\mathfrak{g}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{g}}
satisfies

\tau_{h}(C^{\rm max}_{\mathfrak{g}})=-C^{\rm max}_{\mathfrak{g}}
. In particular
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau_{h})
is
compactly causal.

(d)

For any pointed generating invariant cone
C_{\mathfrak{g}}\subseteq C^{\rm max}_{\mathfrak{g}}
,
we have

C_{\mathfrak{g}}\cap{\mathfrak{g}}^{-\tau_{h}}=C^{\rm max}_{\mathfrak{g}}\cap{\mathfrak{g}}^{-\tau_{h}}
.

Proof.

(a) follows from
[
MN21
, Prop. 3.11]
.

(b) Assume that
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau)
is compactly causal.
In
[
NÓ21b
, App. D]
we show that

\tau
preserves a
3
-grading of
{\mathfrak{g}}
, and this means that the
corresponding Euler element
h
is fixed by
\tau
, i.e.,
h\in{\mathfrak{h}}
.

(c) Since
\tau_{h}(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\rm max})
is one of the two maximal
invariant cones
\pm C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\rm max}
,
(c) follows from
[
Oeh20
, Lemma 2.28]
.

(d) Clearly,
C^{\rm max}_{\mathfrak{g}}\cap{\mathfrak{g}}^{-\tau_{h}}\supseteq C_{\mathfrak{g}}\cap{\mathfrak{g}}^{-\tau_{h}}

by (
18
).
Writing
{\mathfrak{g}}^{-\tau_{h}}={\mathfrak{g}}_{1}(h)\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}(h)
and using the invariance
under
e^{{\mathbb{R}}\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits h}
, it follows as in Remark
23
that

C_{\mathfrak{g}}\cap{\mathfrak{g}}^{-\tau_{h}}=C_{{\mathfrak{g}},+}+-C_{{\mathfrak{g}},-},\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ where }\hskip 10.00002ptC_{{\mathfrak{g}},\pm}=\pm C_{\mathfrak{g}}\cap{\mathfrak{g}}_{\pm 1}(h).

(19)

The cones
C_{{\mathfrak{g}},\pm}
and
C^{\rm max}_{{\mathfrak{g}},\pm}

coincide by
[
Oeh20
, Lemma 2.28]
, so that
(d) follows from (
19
).
∎

Proposition 28
.

Let
{\mathfrak{g}}
be simple and
h\in\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}})
.
Then
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau_{h})
is causal if and only if
it is of Cayley type, i.e.,
{\mathfrak{g}}
is simple hermitian of tube type.

In this case any causal structure represented
by a cone
C\subseteq{\mathfrak{q}}={\mathfrak{g}}^{-\tau_{h}}

leads to two cones
C=C_{+}-C_{-}
and
C^{c}=C_{+}+C_{-}
.
One is hyperbolic and the other is elliptic.

Proof.

We choose a Cartan involution
\theta
with

\theta(h)=-h
.

If
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau_{h})
is causal and

C\subseteq{\mathfrak{q}}
is a pointed generating

\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits_{\mathfrak{g}}({\mathfrak{h}})
-invariant cone, then

C^{0}
contains fixed points of the compact group

\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits_{\mathfrak{g}}({\mathfrak{h}}_{\mathfrak{k}})
, so that
{\mathfrak{q}}^{{\mathfrak{h}}_{\mathfrak{k}}}\not=\{0\}
.
Hence the conclusion follows from
[
HÓ97
, Thm. 1.3.11]
.

If, conversely,
{\mathfrak{g}}
is simple hermitian and
h\in\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}})
, then
Proposition
27
(c) implies that
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau_{h})
is causal.
∎

2.3
Reductive Lie algebras

The last section was devoted to the semisimple case. In this section
we discuss the reductive case.
We assume that
{\mathfrak{h}}
does not contain any
non-zero ideal.
This is no restriction because the integral subgroup corresponding to
such an ideal would act trivially on
G/H
and thus
can be factorized out. This in particular implies
that
{\mathfrak{z}}({\mathfrak{g}})\cap{\mathfrak{h}}=\{0\}
because
{\mathfrak{z}}({\mathfrak{g}})\cap{\mathfrak{h}}
is an ideal of
{\mathfrak{g}}
. Thus

H\cap Z(G)
is discrete.

The following proposition extends Proposition
27
(c) to
the larger class of reductive Lie algebras.

Proposition 29
.

If
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau)
is a
reductive compactly causal symmetric Lie algebra
containing an Euler element and
{\mathfrak{h}}
contains no non-zero ideal,
then
\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}})\cap{\mathfrak{h}}\not=\emptyset
.

Proof.

If
h\in{\mathfrak{g}}
is an Euler element and

h=h_{0}+h_{1}
with
h_{0}\in{\mathfrak{z}}({\mathfrak{g}})
and
h_{1}\in[{\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{g}}]
,
then
h_{1}\in\mathcal{E}([{\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{g}}])
. As
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau)
is compactly causal
and
{\mathfrak{h}}
contains no non-zero ideal, all simple
ideals of
{\mathfrak{g}}
are either hermitian or compact.
In fact, the Extension Theorem
24
implies the
existence of a pointed generating invariant cone in
{\mathfrak{g}}
,
hence
{\mathfrak{g}}
is quasihermitian by
[
Ne00
, Cor. VII.3.9]
,
i.e.,
{\mathfrak{k}}={\mathfrak{z}}_{\mathfrak{g}}({\mathfrak{z}}({\mathfrak{k}}))
holds for a maximal compactly embedded subalgebra.
Since
{\mathfrak{k}}
is adapted to the decomposition into simple ideals
{\mathfrak{g}}_{j}
,
the ideal
{\mathfrak{g}}_{j}
is compact if
{\mathfrak{z}}({\mathfrak{k}}_{j})=\{0\}
and
it is hermitian if
{\mathfrak{z}}({\mathfrak{k}}_{j})\not=\{0\}
(by definition).

As
\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits h_{1}
is diagonalizable,
h_{1}
is contained in the sum of all
hermitian simple ideals. Any such ideal is either
\tau
-invariant,
or corresponds to an irreducible summand of group type.
For group type Lie algebras
({\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}},\tau_{\rm flip})

(cf. (
6
)), the assertion
is trivial because
h=(h_{1},h_{2})\in\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}})

implies
(h_{j},h_{j})\in{\mathfrak{h}}
for
j=1,2
, and at least one of these two
elements is non-central, hence an Euler element.
For the
\tau
-invariant ideals, the assertion follows from
Proposition
27
(b).
∎

Let
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C)
be a reductive compactly causal symmetric Lie algebra
with
{\mathfrak{z}}({\mathfrak{g}})\subseteq{\mathfrak{q}}
(which follows from
{\mathfrak{h}}\cap{\mathfrak{z}}({\mathfrak{g}})=\{0\}
).
Then
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{z}}({\mathfrak{g}})\oplus\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}{\mathfrak{g}}_{j}
,
where the
{\mathfrak{g}}_{j}
are simple ideals that are either compact or simple hermitian.
If
{\mathfrak{g}}_{j}
is compact, we put
C_{{\mathfrak{g}}_{j}}^{\rm min}=\{0\}
and

C_{{\mathfrak{g}}_{j}}^{\rm max}={\mathfrak{g}}_{j}
. With (
18
)
this leads to invariant cones

C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\rm min}:=\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}C_{{\mathfrak{g}}_{j}}^{\rm min}\subseteq C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\rm max}:={\mathfrak{z}}({\mathfrak{g}})\oplus\sum_{j=1}^{n}C_{{\mathfrak{g}}_{j}}^{\rm max}.

(20)

Every Euler element
h\in{\mathfrak{g}}
decomposes as

h=h_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}h_{j}
with

h_{0}\in{\mathfrak{z}}({\mathfrak{g}})
and
h_{j}=0
if
{\mathfrak{g}}_{j}
is compact,
and if
h_{j}\not=0
, then it is an Euler element in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{j}
.
From Proposition
27
we now obtain:

Corollary 210
.

Let
{\mathfrak{g}}
be a reductive quasihermitian Lie algebra,
i.e., all simple ideals are compact or hermitian,
and
h\in\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}})
.Then the following assertions hold:

(a)

If
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\tau_{h}}=\ker(\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits h)
contains no simple hermitian ideal,
then
\tau_{h}(C^{\rm max}_{\mathfrak{g}})=-C^{\rm max}_{\mathfrak{g}}
.

(b)

For any pointed generating invariant cone

C_{\mathfrak{g}}\subseteq C^{\rm max}_{\mathfrak{g}}
, we have

C_{\mathfrak{g}}\cap{\mathfrak{g}}^{-\tau_{h}}=C^{\rm max}_{\mathfrak{g}}\cap{\mathfrak{g}}^{-\tau_{h}}
.

Proof.

(a) Our assumption on
h
means that

h_{j}\in\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}}_{j})
for every simple hermitian ideal
{\mathfrak{g}}_{j}
because

h_{j}\not=0
.
Hence (a) follows immediately from (
20
)
and the corresponding assertion for hermitian
simple Lie algebras (Proposition
27
).

(b) As
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\tau_{h}}
contains the center and all compact ideals,

{\mathfrak{g}}^{-\tau_{h}}
is contained in the sum

{\mathfrak{g}}_{\rm herm}:={\mathfrak{g}}_{j_{1}}\oplus\cdots\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{j_{k}}

of all hermitian simple ideals. Further, the invariance of all ideals
under
\tau_{h}
shows that

{\mathfrak{g}}^{-\tau_{h}}={\mathfrak{g}}_{j_{1}}^{-\tau_{h}}\oplus\cdots\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}_{j_{k}}^{-\tau_{h}}.

The classification of invariant cones
(see
[
Ne00
, Thm. VIII.3.21]
for more details) implies that

C_{{\mathfrak{g}},{\rm herm}}:=C_{\mathfrak{g}}\cap{\mathfrak{g}}_{\rm herm}
is a
pointed generating invariant cone in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\rm herm}

with

\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}C_{{\mathfrak{g}}_{j_{\ell}}}^{\rm min}\subseteq C_{{\mathfrak{g}},{\rm herm}}\subseteq C_{{\mathfrak{g}},{\rm herm}}^{\rm max}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}C_{{\mathfrak{g}}_{j_{\ell}}}^{\rm max}.

Therefore Proposition
27
(d) leads to

C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\rm max}\cap{\mathfrak{g}}^{-\tau_{h}}\supseteq C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{-\tau_{h}}\supseteq\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}C_{{\mathfrak{g}}_{j_{\ell}}}^{\rm min}\cap{\mathfrak{g}}_{j_{\ell}}^{-\tau_{h}}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}C_{{\mathfrak{g}}_{j_{\ell}}}^{\rm max}\cap{\mathfrak{g}}_{j_{\ell}}^{-\tau_{h}}=C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{\rm max}\cap{\mathfrak{g}}^{-\tau_{h}}.\qed

2.4
The global setting for symmetric spaces

In the following
G
denotes a connected real Lie group
and
\eta_{G}\colon G\to G_{\mathbb{C}}
its universal
complexification.
We assume that
\ker(\eta_{G})
is discrete,

which is always the case if
{\mathfrak{g}}
is semisimple
(because
\ker\eta_{G}\subseteq Z(G)
), if
G
is simply connected,
or if
G
is a matrix group.
The groups
G
and
G_{\mathbb{C}}
carry natural structures
of Loos symmetric spaces (
[
Lo69
]
), defined by

g\bullet h:=s_{g}(h):=gh^{-1}g,

(21)

Then automorphisms, antiautomorphisms and left and right translations define
automorphisms of the symmetric space
(G,\bullet)
. In particular, we
obtain a transitive action of the product group
G\times G
on
G

by automorphisms of
(G,\bullet)
via

(g_{1},g_{2}).g:=g_{1}gg_{2}^{-1}.

Then
T_{e}(G)\cong\{(x,-x)\colon x\in{\mathfrak{g}}\}\subseteq{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}
, and
the exponential function of the pointed symmetric space

(G,\bullet,e)
is given by

\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{e}\colon T_{e}(G)\to G,\hskip 10.00002pt(x,-x)\mapsto\exp(2x)=(\exp x,\exp-x).e.

(22)

If
\tau^{G}
is an involutive automorphism of
G
and

H\subseteq G^{\tau^{G}}
an open subgroup, then

M:=G/H,\hskip 20.00003ptg_{1}H\bullet g_{2}H:=g_{1}\tau^{G}(g_{2})^{-1}g_{1}H

(23)

is the corresponding
symmetric space
.
Its
quadratic representation
is the map

Q\colon M\to G,\hskip 10.00002ptQ(gH):=gg^{\sharp},\hskip 20.00003ptg^{\sharp}:=\tau^{G}(g)^{-1}.

(24)

It is a covering of the identity component
G^{\sharp}_{e}\cong G/G^{\tau^{G}}

of the symmetric subspace

G^{\sharp}:=\{g\in G\colon g^{\sharp}=g\}\subseteq G

(see Lemma
A1
in Appendix
A
).

We write the natural action of
G
on the tangent bundle

T(M)
by
(g,v)\mapsto g.v
and identify the tangent space

T_{eH}(M)
with
{\mathfrak{q}}
.
If
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C)
is a causal symmetric Lie algebra for which

\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(H)C=C
, then

V_{+}(gH)=g.C^{0}\subseteq T_{gH}(M)

defines on
M=G/H
a
G
-invariant cone field, i.e., a causal structure.

•

We call the pair
(M,C)
a
causal symmetric space
.
Note that
H
need not be connected, so that the
requirement
\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(H)C=C
is stronger than the invariance
under
\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(H_{e})=\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits_{\mathfrak{g}}({\mathfrak{h}})
.

•

We call
(M,C)

compactly causal
if
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C)
is
compactly causal.

Example 211
.

The following example shows that the causality of
G/H
can depends
on
\pi_{0}(H)
. For that let
G=\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(\mathop{{\rm SL}}\nolimits_{2}({\mathbb{R}}))\cong\mathop{{\rm SO}}\nolimits_{2,1}({\mathbb{R}})_{e}
. Let

h=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}\in\mathfrak{sl}(2,{\mathbb{R}})
. Then

h
is an Euler element and the involution
\tau_{h}
is given by

\tau_{h}\begin{pmatrix}x&y\\
z&-x\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}x&-y\\
-z&-x\end{pmatrix}.

Hence

{\mathfrak{h}}={\mathbb{R}}h\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002pt{\mathfrak{q}}=\left\{\begin{pmatrix}0&y\\
z&0\end{pmatrix}\colon y,z\in{\mathbb{R}}\right\}\cong{\mathbb{R}}x_{+}+{\mathbb{R}}x_{-}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ with }\hskip 10.00002ptx_{+}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix},x_{-}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}.

We
also have
{\mathfrak{g}}_{+1}(h)={\mathbb{R}}x_{+}
,
{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}(h)={\mathbb{R}}x_{-}
and
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}(h)={\mathbb{R}}h
. The map

\theta(x)=-x^{\top}
is
a Cartan involution commuting with
\tau_{h}
. Let

C_{+}:=[0,\infty)x_{+}\subset{\mathfrak{g}}_{1}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ and }\hskip 10.00002ptC_{-}:=[0,\infty)x_{-}\subset{\mathfrak{g}}_{-}.

Then
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau_{h},C_{+}-C_{-})
is
compactly causal and
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau_{h},C^{c}=C_{+}+C_{-})
is non-compactly
causal. Let
z=x_{+}-x_{-}\in{\mathfrak{k}}=\mathop{{\mathfrak{so}}}\nolimits_{2}({\mathbb{R}})
. Then
\theta=e^{\pi\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits z}\in G^{\tau_{h}}=:H

as
\theta
commutes with
\tau_{h}
, but
\theta(C^{c})=-C^{c}
. As
H=(H\cap K)H_{e}=\{\mathbf{1},\theta\}H_{e}
it follows that
C
is
H
-invariant but

\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(H)C^{c}\supseteq-C^{c}
, so that
C^{c}
is not. It is only
invariant under the connected group
H_{e}
.

In general, if
{\mathfrak{g}}
is simple hermitian of tube type
with Euler element
h

and involution
\tau=\tau_{h}
, then if
C=C_{+}-C_{-}
defines a compactly
causal structure, then the cone
C^{c}=C_{+}+C_{-}
defines a non-compactly causal
structure on
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau_{h})
. As above
we see that
C^{c}
is invariant under
\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(G)^{\tau_{h}}_{e}
but not under

\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(G)^{\tau_{h}}
. On the other hand
C
is invariant under the full group
\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(G)^{\tau_{h}}
.

If
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C,h)
is a modular compactly causal symmetric Lie algebra,
then we further assume that the involution
\tau_{h}\in\mathop{{\rm Aut}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}})
integrates
to an involution
\tau_{h}^{G}
of
G

satisfying

\tau_{h}^{G}(H)=H,

(25)

so that it induces an involution
\tau_{h}^{M}
on
M
.

Remark 212
.

(Integrability of
\tau_{h}
)
If
G
is a connected Lie group with Lie algebra

{\mathfrak{g}}
and
h\in\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}})
an Euler element, then

\tau_{h}
does not always integrate to an automorphism of
G
.

Here is an example where
{\mathfrak{g}}
is simple.
Let
G:=\mathop{{\rm SU}}\nolimits_{p,p}({\mathbb{C}})
and
K:={\rm S}(\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits_{p}({\mathbb{C}})\times\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits_{p}({\mathbb{C}}))

be the canonical maximal compact subgroup.
For the Euler element
h:=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}0&\mathbf{1}_{p}\\
\mathbf{1}_{p}&0\end{pmatrix}
,
the corresponding involution
\tau_{h}
acts on
{\mathfrak{g}}
by

\tau_{h}\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}d&c\\
b&a\end{pmatrix},

so that we have on the group level
\tau_{h}^{G}(k_{1},k_{2})=(k_{2},k_{1})
on

K\subseteq\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits_{p}({\mathbb{C}})\times\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits_{p}({\mathbb{C}})
. On

Z(\widetilde{G})=\{(n,z_{1},z_{2})\in{\mathbb{Z}}\times C_{p}\times C_{p}\colon e^{\frac{2\pi in}{p}}z_{1}=z_{2}\}\cong{\mathbb{Z}}\times C_{p}

(see
[
Ti67
, p. 28]
), this leads to the non-trivial involution on

{\mathbb{Z}}\times C_{p}
, given by

\tau_{h}^{\widetilde{G}}(n,z_{1})=(-n,\zeta_{p}^{n}z_{1}),\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ where }\hskip 10.00002pt\zeta_{p}=e^{\frac{2\pi i}{p}}.

It fixes the subgroup
\{0\}\times C_{p}
pointwise
and maps
(-2,\zeta_{p})
to its inverse.
In particular, the cyclic subgroup
\Gamma=\langle(1,1)\rangle

is not invariant, so that
\tau_{h}
does not integrate to
the group
\widetilde{G}/\Gamma
.

Remark 213
.

(Symmetric space structures)
Let
G
be a connected Lie group and
H\subseteq G
be a closed subgroup
with
\mathop{\bf L{}}\nolimits(H)={\mathfrak{h}}
, where
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau)
is a symmetric Lie algebra.
Let
N\subseteq H
be the largest normal subgroup of
G
, contained in
H
, i.e.,
the kernel of the
G
-action on
M
. We put

G_{1}:=G/N\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ and }\hskip 10.00002ptH_{1}:=H/N.

(a)
M=G/H\cong G_{1}/H_{1}
carries a symmetric space structure
if and only if
\tau
integrates to an involution
\tau^{G_{1}}
on
G_{1}

leaving
H_{1}
invariant. Then

H_{1}\subseteq G_{1}^{\tau^{G_{1}}}
is an open subgroup because it
preserves the base point and the corresponding involution

\tau^{M}
on
M
is determined by its differential in the base point.
If
\tau
integrates to an involution

\tau^{G}
on
G
, then we need not have
H\subseteq G^{\tau^{G}}
, we
only have the weaker condition

H\subseteq\{g\in G\colon g\tau^{G}(g)^{-1}\in N\}.

(b) If
{\mathfrak{g}}
is semisimple, then
Z(G)\cap H
acts trivially on
M
,
hence is contained in
N
. It follows that, for
N=\{e\}
,
the adjoint action restricts to an injective representation of
H
.

If, in addition,
{\mathfrak{h}}
contains no non-zero ideal of
{\mathfrak{g}}
,
then
N
is a discrete
normal subgroup of the connected group
G
, hence central, and thus

N=H\cap Z(G).

(26)

(c) From
\mathop{\bf L{}}\nolimits(H)={\mathfrak{h}}
we obtain
\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(H){\mathfrak{h}}={\mathfrak{h}}
.
If
{\mathfrak{g}}
is semisimple, so that
{\mathfrak{q}}={\mathfrak{h}}^{\bot}
with respect to the
Cartan–Killing form implies
\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(H){\mathfrak{q}}={\mathfrak{q}}
. This already implies

H\subseteq\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits^{-1}(\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(G)^{\tau})=\{g\in G\colon gg^{\sharp}\in Z(G)\}.

(27)

(d) If
\tau^{G}(H)=H
and
\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits
is faithful on
H
,
then
\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(H)\subseteq\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(G)^{\tau}
implies
H\subseteq G^{\tau^{G}}
.

3
Fixed points of the modular flow in
M

In Subsection
3.1
we classify for a
reductive compactly causal symmetric reductive Lie algebra
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau)

the orbits of
\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits_{\mathfrak{g}}({\mathfrak{h}})
in the set
\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}})\cap{\mathfrak{h}}

of Euler elements in
{\mathfrak{h}}
. As this set describes the choices
of the Euler element
h\in{\mathfrak{h}}
, this provides a classification
of all modular structures on the associated symmetric spaces.
The classification is done by reduction to the simple case.

On
M=G/H
, an element
m=gH
is fixed by the modular flow

\alpha_{t}^{M}(gH)=\exp(th)gH=g\exp(t\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)^{-1}h)H

(28)

if and only if

\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)^{-1}h\in{\mathfrak{h}}
. Therefore elements of the fixed point set
M^{\alpha}

correspond to Euler elements in
\mathcal{O}_{h}\cap{\mathfrak{h}}
, where

\mathcal{O}_{h}=\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(G)h
is the adjoint orbit of
h
.

In Subsection
3.2
we show that the orbits
of the group
G^{h}
in
M^{\alpha}
are open and that this
leads to a natural bijection between the orbit spaces
M^{\alpha}/G^{h}

and
(\mathcal{O}^{h}\cap{\mathfrak{h}})/H
. Both sets correspond to the set of
double cosets
G^{h}gH\subseteq G
for which
\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)^{-1}h\in{\mathfrak{h}}
.

3.1
\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits_{\mathfrak{g}}({\mathfrak{h}})
-orbits in
\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}})\cap{\mathfrak{h}}

Let
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C)
be a reductive compactly causal symmetric Lie algebra.
Then
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau)
decomposes as

({\mathfrak{g}},\tau)\cong({\mathfrak{g}}_{0},\tau_{0})\oplus\bigoplus_{j=1}^{N}({\mathfrak{g}}_{j},\tau_{j}),

where
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
contains the center and all compact ideals
and the
\tau
-invariant ideals
{\mathfrak{g}}_{j}
,
j\geq 1
, are
either simple hermitian or irreducible of group type.
This decomposition leads to a corresponding decomposition for
{\mathfrak{h}}
:

{\mathfrak{h}}={\mathfrak{h}}_{0}\oplus\bigoplus_{j=1}^{N}{\mathfrak{h}}_{j},

and an element
h\in{\mathfrak{g}}\setminus{\mathfrak{z}}({\mathfrak{g}})

is an Euler element in
{\mathfrak{g}}
if and only if
all its components
h_{0},h_{1},\ldots,h_{N}
either vanish or are Euler elements in
{\mathfrak{g}}_{j}
.
In particular, if
h_{j}\not=0
, then
{\mathfrak{g}}_{j}
is simple hermitian
of tube type (Proposition
28
).
Furthermore,
h_{0}\in{\mathfrak{z}}({\mathfrak{g}})
.

The group

\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits_{\mathfrak{g}}({\mathfrak{h}})
is a product of the subgroups

\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits_{\mathfrak{g}}({\mathfrak{h}}_{j})
,
j=0,\ldots,N
, acting on the ideals
{\mathfrak{g}}_{j}
.
This reduces the problem to classify
\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits_{\mathfrak{g}}({\mathfrak{h}})
-orbits in the
subset
\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}})\cap{\mathfrak{h}}
to the case where

({\mathfrak{g}},\tau)
is irreducible. In the group case

{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{h}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}
, and
\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{h}})
acts transitively on

\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}})\cap{\mathfrak{h}}=\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{h}})
(Proposition
27
).
Therefore it suffices
to analyze the case where
{\mathfrak{g}}
is simple hermitian.

We assume for the moment that
{\mathfrak{g}}
is simple hermitian and
that
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C)
is compactly causal. We do not assume that

\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}})\not=\emptyset
. Let
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{k}}\oplus{\mathfrak{p}}
a
Cartan decomposition invariant under
\tau
.
Then the compact causality of
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau)
is equivalent to

{\mathfrak{z}}({\mathfrak{k}})\subseteq{\mathfrak{q}}
. Let

{\mathfrak{c}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{h}}_{\mathfrak{p}}
be maximal abelian
and
{\mathfrak{a}}\supseteq{\mathfrak{c}}
maximal abelian in
{\mathfrak{p}}
, so that

s:=\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits{\mathfrak{c}}=\mathop{{\rm rank}}\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}}({\mathfrak{h}})\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ and }\hskip 10.00002ptr:=\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits{\mathfrak{a}}=\mathop{{\rm rank}}\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}}({\mathfrak{g}}).

If
h\in\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}})\cap{\mathfrak{h}}
, then

\tau
preserves the
3
-grading defined by
h
.
On the euclidean simple Jordan algebra

E:={\mathfrak{g}}_{1}(h)
(
[
Oeh20
, Rem. 2.24]
),

\gamma:=-\tau|_{E}
is a Jordan automorphism
(
[
Oeh20
, Prop. 3.12]
).
If
\gamma=\mathop{{\rm id}}\nolimits_{E}
, then
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau)
is of
Cayley type
(CT) and

\tau=\tau_{h}
,
and if this is not the case, then
\gamma
is non-trivial
and there are two cases. For split type (ST)
we have
r=s
, and for non-split type (NST) we have

r=2s

(cf.
[
Ó91
, §3]
,
[
NÓ21b
, §D.2.2, Lemma D.5]
,
[
BH98
]
).
If
r=s
, then
\Delta({\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{c}})\cong C_{r}
by Moore’s Theorem
as
{\mathfrak{g}}
is of tube type.

To understand the
\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits_{\mathfrak{g}}({\mathfrak{h}})
-orbits in

\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}})\cap{\mathfrak{h}}
, we have to compare the root systems

\Delta({\mathfrak{h}},{\mathfrak{c}})\subseteq\Delta({\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{c}})

and the inclusions
\mathcal{W}({\mathfrak{h}},{\mathfrak{c}})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{W}({\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{c}})
of the corresponding
Weyl groups.
Any Euler element
h\in\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}})\cap{\mathfrak{h}}
is conjugate under

\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits_{\mathfrak{g}}({\mathfrak{h}})
to one in
{\mathfrak{c}}
. The space

\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}})
consists of a single
\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}})
-orbit
(Proposition
27
(a)), but

\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits_{\mathfrak{g}}({\mathfrak{h}})
may not act transitively on
\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}})\cap{\mathfrak{h}}

(see Theorem
32
).

For the table below, we need the following two examples:

Example 31
.

(a) Assume that
\tau=\tau_{h}
and that

({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C)
is compactly causal of Cayley type.
Then
{\mathfrak{a}}={\mathfrak{c}}
, and the root system
\Delta({\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{a}})
is of type
C_{r}
:

\Delta({\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{a}})\cong C_{r}=\{\pm 2\varepsilon_{j},\pm\varepsilon_{i}\pm\varepsilon_{j}\colon 1\leq i\not=j\leq r\}

and

\Delta({\mathfrak{h}},{\mathfrak{a}})\cong A_{r-1}=\{\pm(\varepsilon_{i}-\varepsilon_{j})\colon 1\leq i\not=j\leq r\}.

The Weyl group
\mathcal{W}(C_{r})
consists of all products of
permutations of
\{1,\ldots,r\}
with
sign changes. Its contains the Weyl group
\mathcal{W}(A_{r-1})
as the subgroup of
permutations. Let
w_{0}=\mathbf{1}
and
w_{j}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r})=(-x_{1},\cdots,-x_{j},x_{j+1},\ldots,x_{r})
. Then

\mathcal{W}(A_{r-1})\backslash\mathcal{W}(C_{r})/\mathcal{W}(A_{r-1})\cong\{w_{0},\ldots,w_{r}\}.

It follows that, up the conjugation by
\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits_{\mathfrak{g}}({\mathfrak{h}})
,
the Euler elements are given by

h_{k}=\frac{1}{2}(\underbrace{1,\ldots,1}_{k},-1,\ldots,-1)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{for}\ 0\leq k\leq r.

To see this, we first note that
h=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r})\in{\mathfrak{a}}
is an Euler element if and only if

x_{j}\in\{\frac{1}{2},-\frac{1}{2}\}
. Applying a suitable
permutation we can always order the
x_{j}
so that

x_{1},\ldots,x_{k}=1/2
and
x_{k+1},\ldots,x_{r}=-1/2
. Note that all
of those elements are conjugate under
\mathcal{W}(C_{r})
as this group
also contains the sign changes.

(b) For
{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathop{{\mathfrak{su}}}\nolimits_{2s,2s}({\mathbb{C}})
and
{\mathfrak{h}}={\mathfrak{u}}_{s,s}({\mathbb{H}})
we have

\Delta({\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{a}})\cong C_{2s}
and
\Delta({\mathfrak{h}},{\mathfrak{c}})\cong C_{s}
.
To determine
\Delta({\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{c}})
, we realize
{\mathfrak{g}}
with respect to the form
defined by the hermitian
2\times 2
-block matrix

B=\begin{pmatrix}0&\mathbf{1}_{2s}\\
\mathbf{1}_{2s}&0\end{pmatrix}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ as }\hskip 10.00002pt{\mathfrak{g}}=\{X\in\mathop{{\mathfrak{sl}}}\nolimits_{2r}({\mathbb{C}})\colon X^{*}B=-BX\}\cong\mathop{{\mathfrak{su}}}\nolimits_{r,r}({\mathbb{C}}).

Then
{\mathfrak{a}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{g}}
can be realized by diagonal matrices of the form

\mathop{{\rm diag}}\nolimits(x_{1},\ldots,x_{2s},-x_{1},\ldots,-x_{2s}),

and
{\mathfrak{c}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{a}}
corresponds to diagonal matrices
satisfying
x_{1}=x_{2},x_{3}=x_{4},\ldots,x_{2s-1}=x_{2s}
.
In these coordinates,

\Delta({\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{a}})\cong C_{2s}=\{\pm 2\varepsilon_{j},\pm\varepsilon_{i}\pm\varepsilon_{j}\colon 1\leq i\not=j\leq 2s\}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ and }\hskip 10.00002pt\Delta({\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{c}})\cong C_{s}.

Restricting from
{\mathfrak{a}}
to
{\mathfrak{c}}
, maps

\varepsilon_{2j-1}
and
\varepsilon_{2j}
(in
C_{2s}
) to
\varepsilon_{j}
(in
C_{s}
) for
j=1,\ldots,s
.
Now it is easy to see that

\Delta({\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{c}})=\Delta({\mathfrak{h}},{\mathfrak{c}})\cup\{0\}.

(c) For
{\mathfrak{g}}=\mathfrak{sp}_{4s}({\mathbb{R}})
and
{\mathfrak{h}}=\mathfrak{sp}_{2s}({\mathbb{C}})
we have exactly
the same pattern and therefore

\Delta({\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{c}})=\Delta({\mathfrak{h}},{\mathfrak{c}})\cup\{0\}.

{\mathfrak{g}}

{\mathfrak{g}}^{c}={\mathfrak{h}}+i{\mathfrak{q}}

{\mathfrak{h}}

Table 1: Irreducible compactly causal symmetric Lie algebras

({\mathfrak{g}},\tau)
with
\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}})\cap{\mathfrak{h}}\not=\emptyset

From Table 1 we get the following classification theorem.
Here we use that, as in
[
MN21
, Thm. 3.10]
, orbits of
Euler elements can be classified by
representatives in a positive Weyl chamber
in terms of a root basis. They correspond to
3
-gradings
of the root system
\Delta({\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{a}})
for
h\in{\mathfrak{a}}\cap\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}})
and to

3
-gradings of
\Delta({\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{c}})
for
h\in{\mathfrak{c}}\cap\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}})
.
We also recall from Proposition
27
that
the existence of an Euler element in a simple hermitian Lie algebra

{\mathfrak{g}}
implies that
{\mathfrak{g}}
is of tube type.

Theorem 32
.

(Classification of
\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits_{\mathfrak{g}}({\mathfrak{h}})
-orbits in
\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}})\cap{\mathfrak{h}}
)

Let
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau)
be an irreducible compactly causal symmetric Lie algebra.
Then we have the following situations:

(GT)

For group type

\Delta({\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{c}})=\Delta({\mathfrak{h}},{\mathfrak{c}})\cong C_{r}
, and

\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits_{\mathfrak{g}}({\mathfrak{h}})
is transitive on
\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}})\cap{\mathfrak{h}}
.

(CT)

For Cayley type

\Delta({\mathfrak{h}},{\mathfrak{c}})\cong A_{r-1}\subseteq C_{r}=\Delta({\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{c}}).

In the canonical identification of
{\mathfrak{c}}
with
{\mathbb{R}}^{r}
, we have

\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}})\cap{\mathfrak{c}}=\{{\textstyle{\frac{1}{2}}}(\pm 1,\cdots,\pm 1)\}

and the orbits of
\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits_{\mathfrak{g}}({\mathfrak{h}})

in
\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}})\cap{\mathfrak{h}}
are represented by the elements

h_{k}={\textstyle{\frac{1}{2}}}(\underbrace{1,\ldots,1}_{k},-1,\ldots,-1),\hskip 10.00002ptk=0,1,\ldots,r.

(29)

(ST)

For split type

\Delta({\mathfrak{h}},{\mathfrak{c}})\cong D_{r}\subseteq C_{r}=\Delta({\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{c}}),

where we identify
D_{3}\cong A_{3}
and
D_{2}\cong A_{1}\oplus A_{1}
.
Then
\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}})^{\tau}
acts transitively on

\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}})\cap{\mathfrak{h}}
and its identity component

\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits_{\mathfrak{g}}({\mathfrak{h}})
has two orbits represented by
h_{r-1}
and
h_{r}
.

(NST)

For non-split type

\Delta({\mathfrak{h}},{\mathfrak{c}})=\Delta({\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{c}})=C_{r}

and
\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits_{\mathfrak{g}}({\mathfrak{h}})
acts transitively on
\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}})\cap{\mathfrak{h}}
.

Proof.

(GT) Here
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau)\cong({\mathfrak{h}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}},\tau_{\rm flip})
, where

{\mathfrak{h}}
is simple hermitian of tube type.
Accordingly
{\mathfrak{a}}={\mathfrak{c}}\oplus{\mathfrak{c}}
, and the assertion follows.

(CT) The first assertion follows from Table 1 and the remainder
from the discussion in Example
31
(a).

(ST) For split type a look at Table 1 shows that

\Delta({\mathfrak{h}},{\mathfrak{c}})\cong D_{r}\subseteq C_{r}=\Delta({\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{c}}),

where we identify
D_{3}\cong A_{3}
and
D_{2}\cong A_{1}\oplus A_{1}
.
Then
\mathcal{W}(D_{r})\trianglelefteq\mathcal{W}(C_{r})
is an index
2
subgroup
and
\mathcal{W}(C_{r})
acts by automorphisms on
D_{r}
. Therefore

\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}})^{\tau}
induces on
{\mathfrak{c}}
the full group
\mathcal{W}(C_{r})
.
Hence the
\mathcal{W}(D_{r})
-orbits in
\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}})\cap{\mathfrak{c}}
are represented by

h_{r-1}
and
h_{r}
, and both are conjugate under
\mathcal{W}(C_{r})
.

(NST) Table 1 shows that, for non-split type, we have

\Delta({\mathfrak{h}},{\mathfrak{c}})=\Delta({\mathfrak{g}},{\mathfrak{c}})=C_{r}.

As
\mathcal{W}(C_{r})
acts transitively on
\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}})\cap{\mathfrak{c}}
,
it follows that
\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits_{\mathfrak{g}}({\mathfrak{h}})
acts transitively on
\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}})\cap{\mathfrak{h}}
.
∎

Corollary 33
.

If
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau)
is irreducible and not of Cayley type,
then
\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}})^{\tau}
acts transitively on
\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}})\cap{\mathfrak{h}}
.

3.2
Connected components of
M^{\alpha}

Recall our global context from Subsection
2.4
. Our goal in
this section is to prove the following proposition:

Proposition 34
.

The orbits of the identity component
G^{h}_{e}
on the
fixed point set

M^{\alpha}:=\{gH\in M\colon\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)^{-1}h\in{\mathfrak{h}}\}=\{m\in M\colon X_{h}^{M}(m)=0\}

of the modular flow coincide with its connected components.

Example
36
below shows
that, for Cayley type involutions on hermitian
simple Lie algebras, the connected components of
M^{\alpha}
may have
different dimensions. Our discussion of de Sitter space
in Subsection
11.1
reveals a situation where

G^{h}
does not preserve the connected components of
M^{\alpha}
.

Proof.

Clearly,
M^{\alpha}
is invariant
under the action of the group
G^{h}
which commutes with
\alpha
.
Therefore
G^{h}_{e}
preserves all connected components of
M^{\alpha}
.
We now show that all
G^{h}_{e}
-orbits in
M^{\alpha}
are open, hence coincide
with its connected components.

First we consider the base point
eH
which is contained in
M^{\alpha}

because
h\in{\mathfrak{h}}
.
Using the exponential function
\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{eH}\colon{\mathfrak{q}}\to M
as a chart around
eH
,
its equivariance with respect to
\alpha
and the one-parameter group

e^{t\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits h}
on
{\mathfrak{q}}
, it follows that
M^{\alpha}
is a symmetric subspace of
M

with
T_{eH}(M^{\alpha})={\mathfrak{q}}_{0}(h)\subseteq{\mathfrak{g}}^{h}
. Therefore

G^{h}_{e}.eH\supseteq\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{eH}({\mathfrak{q}}_{0}(h))
contains a neighborhood of
eH
in
M^{\alpha}
,
and this implies that the
G^{h}_{e}
-orbit of
eH
in
M^{\alpha}
is open.

Now let
m=gH\in M^{\alpha}
be arbitrary.
Then

\alpha_{t}^{M}(gH)=\exp(th)gH=g\exp(t\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)^{-1}h)H

shows that

m\in M^{\alpha}
is equivalent to
\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)^{-1}h\in{\mathfrak{h}}
and

\Phi\colon{\mathfrak{q}}\to M,\hskip 10.00002pt\Phi(x):=g\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{eH}(x)

defines a chart around
m
satisfying

\alpha_{t}\Phi(x)=\exp(th)g\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{eH}(x)=\Phi(e^{t\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)^{-1}h}x).

(30)

Hence
\Phi
maps

\{x\in{\mathfrak{q}}\colon[\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)^{-1}h,x]=0\}={\mathfrak{q}}\cap\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)^{-1}{\mathfrak{g}}^{h}

(31)

onto a neighborhood of
m
in
M^{\alpha}
. Finally

\Phi({\mathfrak{q}}\cap\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)^{-1}{\mathfrak{g}}^{h})\subseteq g\exp({\mathfrak{q}}\cap\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)^{-1}{\mathfrak{g}}^{h})H\subseteq\exp({\mathfrak{g}}^{h})gH\subseteq G^{h}_{e}.m

shows that
G^{h}_{e}.m
is open in
M^{\alpha}
.
∎

Example 35
.

(The group case) In the group case the set
M^{\alpha}=G^{\alpha}

is rather simple to describe.
Here
M=G
,
h=(h_{0},h_{0})
and

\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g_{1},g_{2})h=(\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g_{1})h_{0},\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g_{2})h_{0})\in{\mathfrak{h}}
is equivalent to

\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g_{1})h_{0}=\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g_{2})h_{0}
, i.e.,
g_{1}^{-1}g_{2}\in G^{h_{0}}
.
If this is the case, then
\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g_{1},g_{2})h=\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g_{1},g_{1})h\in\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(\Delta_{G})h
.

We also note that
(G\times G)^{h}=G^{h_{0}}\times G^{h_{0}}

acts transitively on the submanifold
M^{\alpha}=G^{h_{0}}
.

Example 36
.

(An example of Cayley type)
As in Example
31
, we
consider the hermitian form on
{\mathbb{C}}^{2r}
defined by the block
diagonal matrix
B=\begin{pmatrix}0&\mathbf{1}\\
\mathbf{1}&0\end{pmatrix}
, so that

{\mathfrak{g}}:=\{X\in\mathop{{\mathfrak{sl}}}\nolimits_{2r}({\mathbb{C}})\colon X^{*}B=-BX\}\cong\mathop{{\mathfrak{su}}}\nolimits_{r,r}({\mathbb{C}})\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ and }\hskip 10.00002pt\tau\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}a&-b\\
-c&d\end{pmatrix}.

Then

{\mathfrak{h}}=\Big\{\begin{pmatrix}X&0\\
0&-X^{*}\end{pmatrix}\colon\mathop{{\rm tr}}\nolimits(X)\in{\mathbb{R}}\Big\}\cong{\mathbb{R}}\oplus\mathop{{\mathfrak{sl}}}\nolimits_{r}({\mathbb{C}}).

The corresponding groups are

G\cong\mathop{{\rm SU}}\nolimits_{r,r}({\mathbb{C}})\supseteq H\cong\{g\in\mathop{{\rm GL}}\nolimits_{r}({\mathbb{C}})\colon\det(g)\in{\mathbb{R}}\}.

Then

{\mathfrak{a}}=\{\mathop{{\rm diag}}\nolimits(x_{1},\ldots,x_{r},-x_{1},\ldots,-x_{r}\}\colon x_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}\}\cong{\mathbb{R}}^{r},

and in these coordinates, we obtain Euler elements

h_{k}=\frac{1}{2}(\mathbf{1}_{k},-\mathbf{1}_{r-k},-\mathbf{1}_{k},\mathbf{1}_{r-k}),\hskip 10.00002ptk=0,\ldots,r

(Theorem
32
).
Then

\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits\mathcal{O}^{H}_{h_{k}}=\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits[h_{k},{\mathfrak{h}}]=\mathop{{\rm dim}}\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}}(M_{k,r-k}({\mathbb{C}})\oplus M_{r-k,k}({\mathbb{C}}))=2k(r-k)

depends on
k
. For
k=0,r
, the orbit is trivial.
This corresponds to the fact that the base point
eH
in

M=G/H\cong\mathcal{O}^{G}_{h}
is an isolated fixed point of the modular
flow. The components

M_{k}^{\alpha}:=\{gH\colon\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)^{-1}h\in\mathcal{O}^{H}_{h_{k}}\},\hskip 10.00002ptk=0,\ldots,r,

are of different dimensions (for
r>1
).

Lemma 37
.

We have a bijection

\Gamma\colon M^{\alpha}/G^{h}\to(\mathcal{O}_{h}\cap{\mathfrak{h}})/H,\hskip 10.00002ptG^{h}gH\mapsto\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(H)\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)^{-1}h.

Proof.

As
gH\in M^{\alpha}
is equivalent to
\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)^{-1}h\in{\mathfrak{h}}
,
we obtain a well-defined surjective map sending the
G^{h}
-orbit
G^{h}gH
to the

H
-orbit
\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(H)\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)^{-1}h\subseteq\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}})\cap{\mathfrak{h}}
.

If
g_{1}H,g_{2}H\in M^{\alpha}
map to the same
H
-orbit in
\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}})\cap{\mathfrak{h}}
,
then there exists an element
k\in H
with

\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g_{1})^{-1}h=\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(k^{-1})\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g_{2})^{-1}h
, i.e.,

g_{2}kg_{1}^{-1}\in G^{h}
. This implies that

g_{2}H=g_{2}kH\in G^{h}g_{1}H.

As the surjectivity of
\Gamma
follows from the fact that

gH\in M^{\alpha}
is equivalent to
\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)^{-1}h\in{\mathfrak{h}}
,

\Gamma
is bijective.
∎

Remark 38
.

(a) If
H
is connected, then the preceding lemma shows that the
orbits of
\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(H)=\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits_{\mathfrak{g}}({\mathfrak{h}})
in
\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}})\cap{\mathfrak{h}}

are in one-to-one correspondence with the

G^{h}
-orbits in
M^{\alpha}
for
M=G/H
.
Therefore Theorem
32

provides a classification of
G^{h}
-orbits in
M^{\alpha}
whenever

H
is connected.

(b) As
G^{h}
-orbits in
M^{\alpha}
are open by
Proposition
34
, we may also think of

\pi_{0}(G^{h})
-orbits in
\pi_{0}(M^{\alpha})
being classified by

(\mathcal{O}_{h}\cap{\mathfrak{h}})/H
.

Remark 39
.

The involution
\tau_{h}^{M}
acts on the fixed point manifold
M^{\alpha}
, but not
necessarily trivially. A typical example is the group type space

M=\widetilde{\mathop{{\rm SL}}}\nolimits_{2}({\mathbb{R}})
,
where
Z(G)\subseteq M^{\alpha}
and
Z(G)\cap M^{\tau_{h}^{G}}=\{e\}
.

Remark 310
.

(
G^{h}
and
G^{\tau^{G}}
for simple Lie algebras)
As
G^{h}=\{g\in G\colon\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)h=h\}
, we have

\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(G^{h})=\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(G)^{h}=\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(G)^{\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits h}\subseteq\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(G)^{\tau_{h}}.

We claim that, if
{\mathfrak{g}}
is simple, then

\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(G^{h})
is of index
2
in
\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(G)^{\tau_{h}}
.
In fact, in this case
