{\mathfrak{g}}^{-\tau_{h}}
is a direct sum of two irreducible

{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}(h)
-modules
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\pm 1}(h)
(
[
HÓ97
, Cor. 1.3.13]
), and any element

\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)
commuting with
\tau_{h}
either
preserves both subspaces or exchanges them. If it preserves both,
then
\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)
commutes with
\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits h
, i.e.,
g\in G^{h}
.
If not, then
\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)h=-h
, and as such an element
exists (Proposition
27
(a)),
the index of
\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(G^{h})
in
\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(G)^{\tau^{G}}
is
two.

4
Wedge domains in compactly causal symmetric spaces

In this section we introduce the wedge domains

W_{M}^{+}(h)
,
W_{M}^{\rm KMS}(h)
and
W_{M}(h)

in a compactly causal symmetric
space
M=G/H
with the infinitesimal data
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C,h)

(under the global assumption from
Subsection
2.4
that the universal complexification

\eta_{G}
has discrete kernel).
One of our main results asserts that
these three subsets coincide, which is far from obvious from
their definitions. Moreover, their connected components are
orbits of an open real Olshanski subsemigroup of
G
.
We therefore start recalling the construction of Olshanski subsemigroups.

4.1
Tube domains of compactly causal symmetric spaces

Definition 41
.

(Complex Olshanski semigroups)
Let
G
be a connected Lie group with
simply connected covering group
q_{G}\colon\widetilde{G}\to G
.
For a pointed closed convex
\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(G)
-invariant cone
{C_{\mathfrak{g}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{g}}}
,
Lawson’s Theorem (
[
La94
]
,
[
Ne00
, Thm. IX.1.10]
) implies the existence
of a semigroup
\Gamma_{\widetilde{G}}(C_{\mathfrak{g}})
which is a covering of the subsemigroup

\eta_{\widetilde{G}}(\widetilde{G})\exp(iC_{\mathfrak{g}})
of the universal complexification

\widetilde{G}_{\mathbb{C}}
(the simply connected group with Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbb{C}}
).
Then the exponential function
\exp\colon{\mathfrak{g}}+iC_{\mathfrak{g}}\to\widetilde{G}_{\mathbb{C}}

lifts to an exponential function
\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits\colon{\mathfrak{g}}+iC_{\mathfrak{g}}\to\Gamma_{\widetilde{G}}(C_{\mathfrak{g}})
,
and the polar map

\widetilde{G}\times C_{\mathfrak{g}}\to\Gamma_{\widetilde{G}}(C_{\mathfrak{g}}),\hskip 10.00002pt(g,x)\mapsto g\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits(ix)

is a homeomorphism.
We now define the
closed complex Olshanski semigroup

corresponding to the pair
(G,C_{\mathfrak{g}})
by

\Gamma_{G}(C_{\mathfrak{g}}):=\Gamma_{\widetilde{G}}(C_{\mathfrak{g}})/(\ker q_{G}),

(32)

where
q_{G}\colon\widetilde{G}\to G
is the universal covering map
(cf.
[
Ne00
, Thm. XI.1.12]
).
Then the polar map
G\times C_{\mathfrak{g}}\to\Gamma_{G}(C_{\mathfrak{g}})
is a
homeomorphism, and if

C_{\mathfrak{g}}
has interior points, then it restricts to a
diffeomorphism from

G\times C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{0}
onto the open subsemigroup

\Gamma_{G}(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{0}):=\Gamma_{\widetilde{G}}(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{0})/(\ker q_{G})\subseteq\Gamma_{G}(C_{\mathfrak{g}}),

(33)

Complex Olshanski semigroups are non-abelian generalizations
of complex tube domains defined by open cones in real vector spaces;
the domains
{\mathfrak{g}}+iC_{\mathfrak{g}}^{0}
which are the tangent objects of
the open complex Olshanski semigroups
\Gamma_{G}(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{0})

are typical examples. We now turn to similar
objects for symmetric spaces, which on the tangent level correspond to the
tube domain
{\mathfrak{q}}+iC^{0}\subseteq{\mathfrak{q}}_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Definition 42
.

(The complex tube domain of a compactly causal symmetric space)
Let
(G,\tau,H,C)
be a
compactly causal symmetric Lie group
, i.e.,

G
is a connected Lie group,
\tau^{G}
an involutive automorphism of
G
,

H\subseteq G^{\tau^{G}}
an open subgroup, and
C\subseteq{\mathfrak{q}}
a pointed
generating
\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(H)
-invariant closed convex cone such that

C^{0}
consists of elliptic elements.
We assume that the universal complexification
\eta_{G}\colon G\to G_{\mathbb{C}}
has
discrete kernel (which is the case if
G
is simply connected or semisimple).
The involution
\tau^{G}\in\mathop{{\rm Aut}}\nolimits(G)
induces by the universal property
a holomorphic automorphism
\tau^{G_{\mathbb{C}}}
of
G_{\mathbb{C}}
with

\eta_{G}(H)\subseteq G_{\mathbb{C}}^{\tau^{G_{\mathbb{C}}}}
,
and

G_{\mathbb{C}}^{\sharp}=\{g\in G_{\mathbb{C}}\colon\tau^{G_{\mathbb{C}}}(g)=g^{-1}\}

is a complex symmetric subspace of
G_{\mathbb{C}}
.

We call the fiber product

\mathcal{T}_{M}(C):=G\times_{H}iC^{0}

the
tube domain of the pair
(M,C)
.
To obtain a complex manifold structure on this domain,
we observe that the quadratic representation

Q\colon M=G/H\to G
extends to a map

Q_{\mathcal{T}}\colon\mathcal{T}_{M}(C)=G\times_{H}iC^{0}\to G_{\mathbb{C}}^{\sharp},\hskip 10.00002pt[g,ix]\mapsto g.\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits(ix)=g\exp(2ix)g^{\sharp}

which is a covering of an open subset.
Therefore
\mathcal{T}_{M}(C)
carries a unique complex manifold
structure for which
q_{\mathcal{T}}
is holomorphic.
Clearly,
\mathcal{T}_{M}(C)\subseteq G\times_{H}iC
contains
M
as

G\times_{H}\{0\}
in its “boundary” (cf.
[
HÓØ91
]
,
[
KNÓ97
]
,
[
Ne99
]
).

Remark 43
.

(a) Suppose that
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C)
is extendable
and that
C_{\mathfrak{g}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{g}}
is a
-\tau
-invariant
\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(G)
-invariant
pointed cone
with
C=C_{\mathfrak{g}}\cap{\mathfrak{q}}
(see Subsection
2.1
).
Let
G_{C}\trianglelefteq G
be the normal integral subgroup
with Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}_{C}=C_{\mathfrak{g}}-C_{\mathfrak{g}}
.
Then we have the corresponding open
complex Olshanski semigroup

\mathcal{T}_{G_{C}}(C_{\mathfrak{g}}):=G_{C}\exp(iC_{\mathfrak{g}}^{0})

and the quadratic representation

Q\colon M\to G_{C}
extends to a map

Q\colon\mathcal{T}_{M}(C)=G_{C}\times_{H}iC^{0}\to\mathcal{T}_{G_{C}}(C_{\mathfrak{g}}),\hskip 10.00002pt(g,ix)\mapsto g\exp(2ix)g^{\sharp}.

By Lemma
A2
and Remark
A3
, its range
is the identity component of the complex submanifold

\mathcal{T}_{G_{C}}(C_{\mathfrak{g}})^{\sharp}
:

Q(\mathcal{T}_{M}(C))=\mathcal{T}_{G_{C}}(C_{\mathfrak{g}})_{e}^{\sharp}=\{ss^{\sharp}\colon s\in\mathcal{T}_{G_{C}}(C_{\mathfrak{g}})\}.

Since
Q\colon\mathcal{T}_{M}(C)\to Q(\mathcal{T}_{M}(C))
is a covering map
and the
G_{C}
-action on its range lifts to the
G_{C}
-action on
M
,
the holomorphic action of the semigroup
\mathcal{T}_{G_{C}}(C_{\mathfrak{g}})

on
Q(\mathcal{T}_{M}(C))
by
s.m=sms^{\sharp}
lifts to a holomorphic action

\mathcal{T}_{G_{C}}(C_{\mathfrak{g}})\times\mathcal{T}_{M}(C)\to\mathcal{T}_{M}(C),

(34)

extending the
G
-action.

(b) Let
\tau^{G_{\mathbb{C}}}\in\mathop{{\rm Aut}}\nolimits(G_{\mathbb{C}})
be the holomorphic involution
with
\tau^{G_{\mathbb{C}}}\circ\eta_{G}=\eta_{G}\circ\tau^{G}

and
H_{\mathbb{C}}\subseteq G_{\mathbb{C}}^{\tau^{G_{\mathbb{C}}}}
be an open subgroup with

\eta_{G}^{-1}(H_{\mathbb{C}})=H
. Then we have a natural embedding
of
G/H
as the
G
-orbit of the base point
eH_{\mathbb{C}}\in M_{\mathbb{C}}:=G_{\mathbb{C}}/H_{\mathbb{C}}
,
and

\mathcal{T}_{M}(C)=G.\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits(iC^{0})

(35)

can be identified with the orbit

\Gamma_{G}(C_{G}^{0}).eH_{\mathbb{C}}
of the base point under the action of the
open complex Olshanski semigroup
\mathcal{T}_{G}(C_{G})
, which is an open subset
of
M_{\mathbb{C}}
(cf.
[
HÓØ91
, Lemma 1.3]
).

Example 44
.

(a) Let
(G,\tau)
be a connected symmetric Lie group
and
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C)
be a corresponding compactly causal
symmetric Lie algebra.
Suppose that

C_{\mathfrak{g}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{g}}
is a pointed
\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(G)
-invariant
generating cone satisfying

-\tau(C_{\mathfrak{g}})=C_{\mathfrak{g}}
and
C:=C_{\mathfrak{g}}\cap{\mathfrak{q}}

(cf. Theorem
24
).
Then we have a corresponding open complex Olshanski semigroup

S=\mathcal{T}_{G}(C_{\mathfrak{g}})
(Definition
41
) and thus an embedding

\mathcal{T}_{G/G^{\tau^{G}}}(C)=G\times_{G^{\tau^{G}}}iC^{0}\cong\bigcup_{g\in G}g\exp(iC^{0})g^{\sharp}\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{!}}}\ S^{\sharp}_{e}\hookrightarrow S

(Lemma
A2
(4)).

(b) Consider a symmetric space
M=(G,\bullet)
of group
type and an
\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(G)
-invariant cone
C_{\mathfrak{g}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{g}}
.
The corresponding
symmetric Lie group is
G\times G
with

\tau^{G\times G}(g_{1},g_{2})=(g_{2},g_{1}),\hskip 10.00002pt(G\times G)^{\tau^{G\times G}}=\Delta_{G},\hskip 10.00002pt{\mathfrak{q}}=\{(x,-x)\colon x\in{\mathfrak{g}}\},

and the cone

C=\{(x,-x)\colon x\in C_{\mathfrak{g}}\}=(C_{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus-C_{\mathfrak{g}})\cap{\mathfrak{q}}

is
\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(\Delta_{G})
-invariant.
We have the open complex Olshanski semigroup

\Gamma_{G\times G}(C_{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus-C_{\mathfrak{g}})=\Gamma_{G}(C_{\mathfrak{g}})\times\Gamma_{G}(-C_{\mathfrak{g}}).

Here
(g_{1},g_{2})^{\sharp}=(g_{2}^{-1},g_{1}^{-1})
is the

\sharp
-operation in
G\times G
, so that the

\sharp
-fixed points in the open complex Olshanski semigroup

S:=\Gamma_{G\times G}(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{0}\oplus-C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{0})

are the pairs
(s,s^{-1})
,
s\in\Gamma_{G}(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{0})
. We thus obtain

\mathcal{T}_{G}(C)=(G\times G)\times_{\Delta_{G}}iC^{0}\cong G\times iC^{0}\cong\Gamma_{G}(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{0})=\{(s,s^{-1})\colon s\in\Gamma_{G}(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{0})\}\cong S^{\sharp}.

As a complex manifold, this is a copy of the open complex Olshanski semigroup

\Gamma_{G}(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{0})
.

4.2
The modular flow and three types of wedge domains

We are now ready to introduce the three different type of wedge domains
in
M
. We already introduced the modular flow in (
28
) but
repeat the definition here:

Definition 45
.

(The modular flow) The Euler element

h\in{\mathfrak{h}}

defines an
{\mathbb{R}}
-action by automorphisms on
G
via

\alpha_{t}(g)=\exp(th)g\exp(-th),\hskip 20.00003ptg\in G.

Then
\alpha
preserves all connected components of
the subgroup
G^{\tau^{G}}
, hence in particular
H
.
Therefore
\alpha_{t}
induces a flow

\alpha_{t}^{M}(gH)=\exp(th)gH=g\exp(t\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)^{-1}h)H

(36)

on
M=G/H
. This flow is generated by the
modular vector field

X_{h}^{M}\in\mathcal{V}(M),\hskip 10.00002ptX_{h}^{M}(m)=\frac{d}{dt}\Big|_{t=0}\alpha_{t}(m).

(37)

The modular flow extends to
{\mathbb{R}}
-actions by holomorphic maps
on the complex tube domains
\mathcal{T}_{G}(C_{\mathfrak{g}})
and on
\mathcal{T}_{M}(C)
via

\alpha_{t}(g\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits(ix))=\alpha_{t}(g)\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits(ie^{t\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits h}x)\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ and }\hskip 10.00002pt\alpha_{t}^{M}([g,ix]):=[\alpha_{t}(g),ie^{t\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits h}x].

Their infinitesimal generators are denoted
X^{\mathcal{T}_{G}}_{h}
and

X^{\mathcal{T}_{M}}_{h}
, respectively.

On
G_{\mathbb{C}}
we even obtain a holomorphic flow
by
\alpha_{z}(g)=\exp(zh)g\exp(-zh)
,
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
,
but on
\mathcal{T}_{G}(C_{\mathfrak{g}})
and
\mathcal{T}_{M}(C)
, the real flow does not extend to
all of
{\mathbb{C}}
. However, for

s
in the closed complex semigroup
\Gamma_{G}(C_{\mathfrak{g}})
, we consider the orbit map

\alpha^{s}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to\Gamma_{G}(C_{\mathfrak{g}}),\hskip 10.00002ptt\mapsto\alpha_{t}(s)

and define

\alpha_{x+iy}(s):=\alpha^{s}(x+iy)\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ for }\hskip 10.00002ptx+iy\in{\mathbb{C}},

whenever the maximal local flow of the vector field

iX^{\mathcal{T}_{M}}
with initial value
\alpha_{x}(s)|_{s=0}
is defined in
y\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
This implies that

\alpha^{s}
extends to a continuous map on the closed strip
between
{\mathbb{R}}
and
{\mathbb{R}}+iy
which is holomorphic on the interior
in the sense that its composition with the natural map

\Gamma_{G}(C_{\mathfrak{g}})\to G_{\mathbb{C}},g\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits(ix)\mapsto\eta_{G}(g)\exp(ix)

is a holomorphic
G_{\mathbb{C}}
-valued map (cf. Definition
41
).

Recall that
we assume that
\tau
integrates to an involution

\tau_{h}^{G}
on
G
which leaves
H
invariant,
so that
it induces an involution
\tau_{h}^{M}
on
M=G/H

(see the end of Subsection
2.4
).

Definition 46
.

We consider the following types of
wedge domains
:

3
3

3

In
complex analysis “domains” are assumed connected. Here we use
the term “domain” for an open subset.

•

The
positivity domain of the modular vector field
X_{h}^{M}
in
M
is

W_{M}^{+}(h):=\{m\in M\colon X_{h}^{M}(m)\in V_{+}(m)\},

where
V_{+}(m)\subseteq T_{m}(M)
is the open cone corresponding to the

G
-invariant cone field with
V_{+}(eH)=C^{0}
in
T_{eH}(M)\cong{\mathfrak{q}}
.
If
M
is Lorentzian and
V_{+}(m)
is the future light cone in
T_{m}(M)
,
then this is the domain where the modular vector field is future oriented
timelike.

•

The
KMS wedge domain
is

\displaystyle W_{M}^{\rm KMS}(h):=

\displaystyle\{m\in M\colon(\forall z\in\mathcal{S}_{\pi})\ \alpha_{z}(m)\in\mathcal{T}_{M}(C)\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{m\in M\colon(\forall y\in(0,\pi))\ \alpha_{iy}(m)\in\mathcal{T}_{M}(C)\},

where
\alpha_{z}(m)
is assumed to be defined in
m

in as in Definition
45
,
and

\mathcal{S}_{\pi}=\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}\colon 0<\mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits z<\pi\}.

This is the set of all points
m\in M
whose
\alpha
-orbit map extends
analytically to a map
\mathcal{S}_{\pi}\to\mathcal{T}_{M}(C)
. Comparing boundary values on

{\mathbb{R}}
and
\pi i+{\mathbb{R}}
resembles KMS conditions; hence the name
(see also
[
NÓØ21
, App. A.2]
).

•

Let
m\in M^{\alpha}
be a fixed point of the modular flow
and
C_{m}=\overline{V_{+}(m)}\subseteq T_{m}(M)
. Then
\alpha
induces a

1
-parameter group of linear automorphisms on
T_{m}(M)
whose infinitesimal
generator has the eigenvalues
-1,0,1
. We write

T_{m}(M)_{j}
,
j=-1,0,1
, for the corresponding eigenspaces and consider the pointed cone

C_{m}^{c}:=C_{m}\cap T_{m}(M)_{1}-C_{m}\cap T_{m}(M)_{-1}\subseteq T_{m}(M).

Note that

C_{m}^{c}-C_{m}^{c}\subseteq T_{m}(M)
is a
is a vector space complement of
T_{m}(M^{\alpha})=T_{m}(M)_{0}
.

Then the
wedge domain in
m
is defined as

W_{M}(h)_{m}:=G^{h}_{e}.\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{m}(C_{m}^{c,0}),

where
C_{m}^{c,0}:=(C_{m}^{c})^{0}

is the relative interior of
C_{m}^{c}
in
T_{m}(M)_{1}+T_{m}(M)_{-1}

(cf. (
10
)) and

G^{h}_{e}=(G^{h})_{e}
is the connected subgroup
with Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}^{\tau_{h}}={\mathfrak{g}}_{0}(h)
.
On the infinitesimal level, this domain corresponds to the wedge

T_{m}(M)_{0}+C_{m}^{c}\subseteq T_{m}(M)
.

Identifying
C
with
C_{eH}\subseteq T_{eH}(M)\cong{\mathfrak{q}}
,
the
wedge domain in the base point is

W_{M}(H)_{eH}:=G^{h}_{e}.\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{eH}((C_{+}+C_{-})^{0}),\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ where }\hskip 10.00002ptC_{\pm}=\pm C\cap{\mathfrak{q}}_{\pm 1}(h).

We define the
polar wedge domain of
(M,h)
as

W_{M}(h):=\bigcup_{m\in M^{\alpha}}W_{M}(h)_{m}=\bigcup_{m\in M^{\alpha}}\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{m}(C_{m}^{c,0}).

(38)

Remark 47
.

For
g\in G
and
v\in T_{m}(M)
, the fact that
G
acts by automorphisms
on the symmetric space
M
implies that

g.\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{m}(v)=\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{gm}(g.v)\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ for }\hskip 10.00002ptg\in G,m\in M,v\in T_{m}(M).

(39)

Now let
m=gH\in M^{\alpha}
and identify
T_{eH}(M)
with
{\mathfrak{q}}
.
For
h^{\prime}:=\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)^{-1}h\in{\mathfrak{h}}
, we then have

C_{m}=g.C\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ and }\hskip 10.00002ptT(\alpha_{t})(g.x)=g.(e^{t\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits h^{\prime}}x)\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ for }\hskip 10.00002ptt\in{\mathbb{R}},x\in{\mathfrak{q}},

so that

C_{m}^{c}=g.C^{c}(h^{\prime})\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ for }\hskip 10.00002ptC^{c}(h^{\prime}):=C\cap{\mathfrak{q}}_{1}(h^{\prime})-C\cap{\mathfrak{q}}_{-1}(h^{\prime}).

(40)

This leads to the relation

\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{m}(C_{m}^{c,0})=\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{gH}(g.C^{c}(h^{\prime})^{0})=g.\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{eH}(C^{c}(h^{\prime})^{0}),

(41)

which in turn entails with
G^{h}g=gG^{h^{\prime}}

\displaystyle W_{M}(h)_{gH}

\displaystyle=G^{h}_{e}.\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{gH}(C_{m}^{c,0})=G^{h}_{e}g.\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{eH}(C^{c}(h^{\prime})^{0})=gG^{h^{\prime}}_{e}.\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{eH}(C^{c}(h^{\prime})^{0})

\displaystyle=g.W_{M}(h^{\prime})_{eh}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ for }\hskip 10.00002ptgH\in M^{\alpha}.

(42)

Example
36
shows that the dimensions of the eigenspaces of

\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits h^{\prime}
on
{\mathfrak{q}}
are not always the same.

Remark 48
.

(On the assumption
h\in{\mathfrak{h}}
)

(a) The domains
W_{M}^{+}(h)
and
W_{M}^{\rm KMS}(h)
are defined for any
Euler element
h\in\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}})
. They only require the corresponding
flows in
M
and
\mathcal{T}_{M}(C)
. However, these domains may be trivial
if
h\not\in{\mathfrak{h}}
(cf. Proposition
66
).

(b) If the vector field
X_{h}^{M}
has a zero, then we may choose
the base point accordingly to obtain
h\in{\mathfrak{h}}
. An Euler element

h\in\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}})
has this property if and only if its adjoint orbit

\mathcal{O}_{h}
intersects
{\mathfrak{h}}
(cf. (
36
) in
Definition
45
).

For a compactly causal symmetric Lie algebra
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C)
,
there may be many different cones
C^{\prime}
for which

({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C^{\prime})
is compactly causal,
but there is a rather explicit classification of all these
cones which is described in
[
NÓ21a
, Thm. 3.6]
in some
detail in the
c
-dual context of non-compactly causal spaces.
It implies in particular that

C
is contained in a uniquely determined
maximal
\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits_{\mathfrak{g}}({\mathfrak{h}})
-invariant elliptic
cone
C_{\mathfrak{q}}^{\rm max}
(which need not be pointed;
see also (
20
) and the
discussion in Subsection
2.3
).

Recall the definition of
C_{\pm}:=(\pm C)\cap{\mathfrak{g}}_{\pm 1}
from Remark
23
. The following
proposition follows immediately by
c
-duality from

[
NÓ21b
, Prop. 3.8]
. It has the interesting consequence that,
if
{\mathfrak{g}}
is reductive, then the cones
C_{\pm}
remain the same when we replace
C
by the maximal cone
C^{\rm max}\subseteq{\mathfrak{q}}
containing
C

(see also Corollary
210
(b) for spaces of group type).

Proposition 49
.

Let
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C,h)
be a reductive modular compactly causal
symmetric Lie algebra.
Then

C_{+}-C_{-}=C\cap{\mathfrak{q}}^{-\tau_{h}}=C^{\rm max}\cap{\mathfrak{q}}^{-\tau_{h}}.

(43)

This proposition implies in particular that the
wedge domain
W_{M}(h)
remains the same if we replace
C
by
C^{\rm max}
.
So it only depends on the “direction” of the cone
C
, not on its specific
shape. This has interesting consequences for the global structure of
W_{M}(h)
.

Remark 410
.

(Factorization of wedge domains) Let

({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C)
be a reductive compactly causal symmetric Lie algebra.
Then
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau)
decomposes as

({\mathfrak{g}},\tau)\cong({\mathfrak{g}}_{0},\tau_{0})\oplus\bigoplus_{j=1}^{N}({\mathfrak{g}}_{j},\tau_{j}),

where
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}
contains the center and all compact ideals
and the
\tau
-invariant ideals
{\mathfrak{g}}_{j}
,
j\geq 1
, are
either simple hermitian or irreducible of group type
(cf.
[
NÓ21a
, Prop. 2.5]
and Subsection
2.3
).
Decomposing
{\mathfrak{q}}={\mathfrak{q}}_{0}\oplus\bigoplus_{j=1}^{N}{\mathfrak{q}}_{j}
accordingly,

C^{\rm max}={\mathfrak{q}}_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{N}C^{\rm max}_{j}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ with }\hskip 10.00002ptC_{j}^{\rm max}=C^{\rm max}\cap{\mathfrak{q}}_{j},

where the cones
C_{j}^{\rm max}
are pointed.
If
M
is the simply connected symmetric space corresponding to
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau)
,
it follows that

M\cong M_{0}\times M_{1}\times\cdots\times M_{N},

(44)

and

W_{M}(h)=M_{0}\times\prod_{j=1}^{N}W_{M_{j}}(h_{j}),

(45)

where
h=h_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{N}h_{j}
with
h_{j}\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{j}
and
h_{0}\in{\mathfrak{z}}({\mathfrak{g}})
.

We have a similar global decomposition if
G
is semisimple with

Z(G)=\{e\}
.
Then

G=\mathop{{\rm Aut}}\nolimits(G)_{e}\cong\prod_{j=0}^{N}\mathop{{\rm Aut}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}}_{j}),\hskip 10.00002ptH=G^{\tau}\cong\prod_{j=0}^{N}\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}}_{j})^{\tau_{j}},\hskip 10.00002ptM\cong\prod_{j=0}^{N}M_{j}.

Example 411
.

(The case of compact Lie algebras)
If
{\mathfrak{g}}
is a compact Lie algebra, then

\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits x
has purely imaginary spectrum for every
x\in{\mathfrak{g}}
,
so that
\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}})=\emptyset
. However, we may consider central elements

h\in{\mathfrak{z}}({\mathfrak{g}})
as a degenerate kind of Euler elements with
\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits h=0

and
{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{g}}_{0}(h)
.

There are many compactly
causal symmetric spaces for which
{\mathfrak{g}}
is a compact Lie algebra.
For instance, a compact Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
contains pointed generating invariant
cones
C
if and only if
{\mathfrak{z}}({\mathfrak{g}})\not=\{0\}
, and then

{\mathfrak{z}}({\mathfrak{g}})\cap C^{0}\not=\emptyset
holds for any such cone.
The Lie algebra

{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{u}}_{n}({\mathbb{C}})\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ with }\hskip 10.00002ptC:=\{X\in{\mathfrak{g}}\colon-iX\geq 0\}

is an important example.
The corresponding causal group
(\mathop{\rm U{}}\nolimits_{n}({\mathbb{C}}),C)
is the conformal compactification
of the euclidean Jordan algebra
\mathop{{\rm Herm}}\nolimits_{n}({\mathbb{C}})
, which, for
n=2
, is
isomorphic to the
4
-dimensional Minkowski space
(
[
FK94
]
). Involutions
\tau
of
{\mathfrak{u}}_{n}({\mathbb{C}})

with
\tau(C)=-C
are obtained from involutive
automorphisms of
\mathop{{\mathfrak{su}}}\nolimits_{n}({\mathbb{C}})
, extending by
\tau(i\mathbf{1})=-i\mathbf{1}
.

For any compactly causal symmetric Lie algebra
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C)

and
h\in{\mathfrak{z}}({\mathfrak{g}})
, we have
C_{\pm}=\{0\}
, so that

W_{M}(h)=\emptyset
. Further, the triviality of the modular flow
implies
W_{M}^{\rm KMS}(h)=\emptyset
, but if

h\in{\mathfrak{z}}({\mathfrak{g}})^{-\tau}\cap C^{0}
, then
W_{M}^{+}(h)=M
.

5
Wedge domains in spaces of group type

For our analysis of wedge domains in compactly causal symmetric spaces,
we shall follow the strategy to first study spaces of group
type
G\cong(G\times G)/\Delta_{G}
,
and then use embeddings into these spaces implemented by
the quadratic representation to derive corresponding
results in general.

Let
(G,C)
be a causal symmetric spaces of group type, i.e.,

G
is a connected Lie group and
C\subseteq{\mathfrak{g}}
a
pointed generating invariant cone. We further assume that the universal
complexification
\eta_{G}\colon G\to G_{\mathbb{C}}
has discrete kernel.
We consider

G
as the symmetric space
(G\times G)/\Delta_{G}
with
\tau^{G\times G}(g_{1},g_{2})=(g_{2},g_{1})
and
G\times G
acting by
(g_{1},g_{2}).g=g_{1}gg_{2}^{-1}
.
The associated causal symmetric Lie algebra is

({\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C),\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ where }\hskip 10.00002pt\tau(x,y)=(y,x),

and

C\subseteq{\mathfrak{g}}\cong{\mathfrak{q}}=\{(x,-x)\colon x\in{\mathfrak{g}}\}

is an
\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(G)
-invariant pointed generating invariant cone.
The Euler element
h\in\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}})
defines the Euler element

(h,h)\in{\mathfrak{g}}\times{\mathfrak{g}}
which generates the flow

\alpha_{t}(g)=\exp(th)g\exp(-th)
on
G
.
If
\tau_{h}^{G}
is an involution on
G
integrating

\tau_{h}=e^{\pi i\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits h}
(cf. Remark
212
), then
this involution extends to the involution

\tau^{G}_{h}\times\tau_{h}^{G}
on
G\times G
.

We recall the closed/open complex Olshanski semigroup

\Gamma_{G}(C)
from Definition
41
.
We start with the preparation of the
analysis of wedge domains in
modular symmetric spaces of group type.

Proposition 51
.

Let
G
be a connected Lie group with Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
,

h\in\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}})
an Euler element and
C\subseteq{\mathfrak{g}}

an
\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(G)
-invariant pointed closed convex cone such that:

(i)

-\tau_{h}(C)=C
.

4
4

4

If
{\mathfrak{g}}
is not reductive and
C
is pointed
and
generating,
then
C\cap{\mathfrak{z}}({\mathfrak{g}})\not=\{0\}

(
[
Ne00
, Thm. VII.3.10]
).

As
\tau_{h}|_{{\mathfrak{z}}({\mathfrak{g}})}=\mathop{{\rm id}}\nolimits_{{\mathfrak{z}}({\mathfrak{g}})}
,
the condition
-\tau_{h}(C)=C
can only be satisfied if

C
is
not
generating (see Example
26
).

(ii)

\tau_{h}
integrates to an automorphism
\tau_{h}^{G}
of
G
.

(iii)

The kernel of the universal complexification

\eta_{G}\colon G\to G_{\mathbb{C}}
is discrete.

Let
g^{\sharp}=\tau(s)^{-1}
,
g\in G
.
Then the following assertions hold:

(a)

Let
C^{c}:=C_{+}+C_{-}
with
C_{\pm}:=\pm C\cap{\mathfrak{g}}_{\pm 1}(h)
. Then the convex cones

C^{c},C_{+}
and
C_{-}
are
G^{h}
-invariant and
the subset

\Gamma_{G^{h}}(C^{c}):=G^{h}\exp(C^{c})

(46)

is a real Olshanski semigroup, associated to the symmetric
Lie group
(G,\tau_{h}^{G})
. In particular,
the polar map
G^{h}\times C^{c}\to\Gamma_{G^{h}}(C^{c})
is a homeomorphism and

\Gamma_{G^{h}}(C^{c})
is invariant under
s\mapsto s^{\sharp}
.

(b)

The semigroup

S(C,h):=\{g\in G\colon h-\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)h\in C\}

is invariant under
s\mapsto s^{\sharp}
and
satisfies

\displaystyle S(C,h)

\displaystyle=\exp(C_{+})G^{h}\exp(C_{-})=G^{h}\exp(C_{+})\exp(C_{-})

\displaystyle=\exp(C_{-})\exp(C_{+})G^{h}=\exp(C_{-})G^{h}\exp(C_{+})=\Gamma_{G^{h}}(C^{c}).

(47)

If
C
is generating,

S(C,h)^{0}=\Gamma_{G^{h}}(C^{c,0})=S(C^{0},h):=\{g\in G\colon h-\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)h\in C^{0}\}.

(48)

It is invariant under

g\mapsto g^{\sharp}
.

(c)

The subset of those elements
g\in G
for which the orbit map

\alpha^{g}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to G,\hskip 10.00002pt\alpha^{g}(t)=\exp(th)g\exp(-th)

extends to a continuous map

\alpha^{g}\colon\overline{\mathcal{S}_{\pi}}\to\Gamma_{G}(C)
, which is
holomorphic on
\mathcal{S}_{\pi}
when composed with the natural map

\Gamma_{G}(C)\to G_{\mathbb{C}}
, coincides with
the closed semigroup
\Gamma_{G^{h}}(C^{c})
.
If
C
has interior points, then

W_{G}^{\rm KMS}(h)=\Gamma_{G^{h}}(C^{c,0}).

(49)

(d)

Let
X_{h}^{G}\in\mathcal{V}(G)
be the vector field defined by

X_{h}^{G}(g):=\frac{d}{dt}\big|_{t=0}\alpha_{t}(g).
We write

G\times TG\to TG,\hskip 10.00002pt(g,x)\mapsto g.x

for left translation of vectors on
G
. Then

S(C,h)=\{g\in G\colon X_{h}^{G}(g)\in g.C\},\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ and }\hskip 10.00002ptS(C,h)^{0}=\{g\in G\colon X_{h}^{G}(g)\in g.C^{0}\}\

(50)

if
C
is generating.

(e)

On

\Gamma_{G}(C)
we consider the antiholomorphic involution defined by

\overline{\tau}_{h}(g\exp(ix))=\tau_{h}^{G}(g)\exp(-i\tau_{h}(x)).

(51)

Its set of fixed points coincides with

\Gamma_{G}(C)^{\overline{\tau}_{h}}=G^{\tau_{h}^{G}}\exp(iC^{-\tau_{h}})

(52)

and, if
C
has interior points, then the interior
of the identity component
\Gamma_{G}(C)^{\overline{\tau}_{h}}_{e}

satisfies

S(C,h)_{e}^{0}=\alpha_{-\pi i/2}\big((\Gamma_{G}(C)^{\overline{\tau}_{h}})_{e}^{0}\big).

Proof.

Similar results are stated in
[
Ne19
]
for
simply connected Lie groups
G
. If

q_{G}\colon\widetilde{G}\to G
is the universal covering group of
G
,
they apply to the group
\widetilde{G}
. It therefore suffices
to derive everything from
[
Ne19
]
.

(a) That
C^{c},C_{+}
and
C_{-}
are
G^{h}
invariant follows from

\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(G^{c}){\mathfrak{g}}_{j}(h)={\mathfrak{g}}_{j}(h)
and the invariance of
C
. The
remainder of (a) follows by applying
[
Ne19
, Prop. 2.6]
to
\widetilde{G}

because
\ker(q_{G})\subseteq\widetilde{G}^{h}
and

G^{h}=q_{G}(\widetilde{G}^{h})
.

(b) First we note that for
g\in G
we have

-\tau(h-\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g^{\sharp})h)=\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g^{-1})h-h=\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g^{-1})(h-\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)h)\in C

As
C
is
G
-invariant.
Thus
h-\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g^{\sharp})h\in-\tau(C)=C
.
That the four middle sets in (
(b)
) agree now
follows
now from the
\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(G^{h})
invariance of
C_{\pm}
.

For
\widetilde{S}(C,h):=\{g\in\widetilde{G}\colon h-\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)h\in C\}

we have
\widetilde{S}(C,h)=q_{G}^{-1}(S(C,h))
, so that the
corresponding result for
\widetilde{G}
(
[
Ne19
, Thm. 2.16]
)
and the invariance of
G^{h},\widetilde{S}(C,h)
and
\widetilde{\Gamma}_{G^{h}}(C^{c}):=\widetilde{G}^{h}\exp(C^{c})

under multiplication with the central subgroup
\ker(q_{G})

show that it also holds for
G
.

Assume, in addition, that
C
is generating, so that
C^{0}\not=\emptyset
.
Then
S(C^{0},h)\subseteq S(C,h)
is an open subsemigroup,
hence contained in

S(C,h)^{0}=G^{h}\exp(C^{c,0})=G^{h}\exp(C_{+}^{0})\exp(C_{-}^{0})=\exp(C_{-}^{0})\exp(C_{+}^{0})G^{h}.

To show equality, write an element
g\in S(C,h)^{0}
as

g=\exp(x_{-})\exp(x_{+})g^{\prime}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ with }\hskip 10.00002ptg^{\prime}\in G^{h}_{C},x_{\pm}\in C_{\pm}^{0}.

Then

\displaystyle\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)h

\displaystyle=e^{\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits x_{-}}e^{\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits x_{+}}h=e^{\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits x_{-}}(h+[x_{+},h])=e^{\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits x_{-}}(h-x_{+})

\displaystyle=h-x_{+}+[x_{-},h]-[x_{-},x_{+}]-[x_{-},[x_{-},x_{+}]]

\displaystyle=h-\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(\exp x_{-})(x_{+}-x_{-})\in h-C^{0}

as
C^{0}
is
G
-invariant. It follows that
h-\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)h\in C^{0}
.
and hence
g\in S(C^{0},h)
.

(c) For
\widetilde{G}
and the closed cone,
this is
[
Ne19
, Thm. 2.21]
. As

\widetilde{G}^{h}
and the specified subset of
G

are
\ker(q_{G})
-invariant, the assertion also holds for
G
.

For
z=x+iy\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbb{C}}
with
x,y\in{\mathfrak{g}}
, we write
x=\mathop{{\rm Re}}\nolimits z
and
y=\mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits z
.
To verify (
49
), for
g=g_{0}\exp(x_{-}+x_{+})
with
g_{0}\in G^{h}
,

x_{\pm}\in C_{\pm}=C\cap{\mathfrak{g}}_{\pm 1}(h)
, and

0<t<\pi
, we note that
\sin(t)>0
and

\displaystyle\alpha_{it}(g_{0}\exp(x_{+}+x_{-}))

\displaystyle=g_{0}\exp(e^{it}x_{+}+e^{-it}x_{-})

\displaystyle=g_{0}\exp((\cos t+i\sin t)x_{+}+(\cos t-i\sin t)x_{-}).

Thus

\mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits(e^{it}x_{+}e^{-it}x_{-})=\sin(t)(x_{+}-x_{-}).

This element is contained in the interior
C^{0}
if and only if

x_{\pm}\in C_{\pm}^{0}
, and in this case

\exp(e^{it}x_{+}+e^{-it}x_{-})\in\exp({\mathfrak{g}}+iC^{0})\subseteq\Gamma_{G}(C^{0}).

We therefore obtain (
49
).

(d) With the notation for left and right translations by
G

on
TG
(see the notation introduced in the introduction), we have

X_{h}^{G}(g)=h.g-g.h=g.(\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)^{-1}h-h).

Hence (
50
) follows immediately from

\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)C=C
, (
(b)
) and (
48
).

(e) Equation (
52
) follows from
(
51
). We observe that

\alpha_{\pi i/2}(S(C,h)_{e})=(G^{h})_{e}\exp\big(e^{\frac{\pi i}{2}\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits h}(C_{+}+C_{-})\big)=(G^{h})_{e}\exp(i(C_{+}-C_{-})),

so that
\alpha_{\pi i/2}(S(C,h)_{e})
is the identity component
of the fixed point set
\Gamma_{G}(C)^{\overline{\tau}_{h}}

in the closed Olshanski semigroup
\Gamma_{G}(C)
.
If
C
has interior points, this argument shows that

\alpha_{\pi i/2}(S(C,h)_{e}^{0})
is the connected component
of the fixed point submanifold in the open semigroup

\Gamma_{G}(C^{0})
whose closure contains the identity.
This proves (e).
∎

To provide a good context to deal also with group
type spaces that are not reductive, we have to deal
with invariant cones
C\subseteq{\mathfrak{g}}
which are not generating.
This is mainly due to the fact that the requirement of
C
being generating
is not compatible with
-\tau_{h}(C)=C
.
As in Subsection
2.1
, this problem can be overcome by observing that

{\mathfrak{g}}_{C}=C-C\trianglelefteq{\mathfrak{g}}

is an ideal of the Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
. We consider the corresponding
integral subgroup
G_{C}\trianglelefteq G
, endowed with its natural
Lie group structure (it is closed if
G
is simply connected).
Then

(G\times G)_{C}:=\{(g_{1},g_{2})\in G\colon g_{1}g_{2}^{-1}\in G_{C}\}

is a Lie group containing the diagonal
\Delta_{G}
and isomorphic to

G_{C}\rtimes G
, where
G
acts by conjugation on
G_{C}
.
Then

G_{C}\cong(G\times G)_{C}/\Delta_{G}

is a symmetric space on which

V_{+}(g):=g.C^{0}

defines a
(G\times G)_{C}
-invariant field of pointed open cones
(a causal structure). The corresponding symmetric Lie algebra is

({\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}})_{C}=\{(x,y)\in{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}\colon x-y\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{C}\}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ with }\hskip 10.00002pt\tau(x,y)=(y,x).

Here
{\mathfrak{q}}=\{(x,-x)\colon x\in{\mathfrak{g}}_{C}\}\cong{\mathfrak{g}}_{C}
contains the pointed generating cone

\{(x,-x)\colon x\in C\}\cong C
.
We now determine the wedge domains in the causal symmetric space
M=G_{C}

for the structure defined by
(({\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}})_{C},\tau,C)
.

We do not assume that
C
is generating in
{\mathfrak{g}}
, but it is natural
to assume that the two cones
C_{\pm}
generate
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\pm 1}(h)
to
ensure that the semigroup
S(C,h)
has interior points.

Theorem 52
.

(Wedge domains in spaces of group type)

Suppose that
C_{\pm}
generate
{\mathfrak{g}}_{\pm 1}(h)
.
Then the wedge domains in the compactly causal symmetric space

(G_{C},C)
corresponding to the modular compactly causal symmetric Lie algebra

(({\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}})_{C},\tau,C,(h,h))
are

W_{G_{C}}^{+}(h)=W_{G_{C}}^{\rm KMS}(h)=W_{G_{C}}(h)=G_{C}^{h}\exp(C_{+}^{0}+C_{-}^{0}).

Proof.

The fixed point set of the modular flow in
M=G_{C}
is
the centralizer
G_{C}^{h}
of
G
. For a pair
(g_{1},g_{2})\in(G\times G)_{C}
,
we have
m:=(g_{1},g_{2}).e=g_{1}g_{2}^{-1}\in G_{C}^{h}
if and only if

\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g_{1})^{-1}h=\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g_{2})^{-1}h
, i.e.,
\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g_{1},g_{2})^{-1}(h,h)\in{\mathfrak{h}}=\Delta_{\mathfrak{g}}
.
The invariance of
C
under
\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(G)
implies the invariance
of the cones
C_{\pm}=\pm C\cap{\mathfrak{g}}_{\pm 1}(h)
under

\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(G^{h})
. Therefore
C^{c}=C_{+}+C_{-}\subseteq{\mathfrak{g}}^{-\tau_{h}}
is invariant under

\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(G^{h})
.

For
m=g_{1}g_{2}^{-1}\in G_{C}^{h}
, the modular flow
acts on
T_{m}(G_{C})=g_{1}.{\mathfrak{g}}_{C}.g_{2}^{-1}=m.{\mathfrak{g}}_{C}

by

\alpha_{t}(m.x)=m.e^{t\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits h}x,

so that
C_{m}^{c}:=m.C^{c}
(here we use the dot notation for left and right translations on
T(G)
).
Therefore the polar wedge domain in
M=G_{C}
is

W_{G_{C}}(h)=\bigcup_{m\in G_{C}^{h}}m\exp(C^{c,0})=G_{C}^{h}\exp(C^{c,0}).

(53)

Next we derive from Proposition
51
(c) that

W_{G_{C}}^{\rm KMS}(h)=G^{h}_{C}\exp(C_{-}^{0}+C_{+}^{0})=W_{G_{C}}(h).

(54)

Finally, we observe that Proposition
51
(a),(d) imply that

W_{G_{C}}^{+}(h)=G_{C}^{h}\exp(C_{+}^{0}+C_{-}^{0})=W_{G_{C}}(h).

(55)

The assertion now follows from (
54
) and (
55
).
∎

The open subset
W_{G_{C}}(h)
neet not be connected, but its connected
components are parametrized by
the group
\pi_{0}(G_{C}^{h})
of connected components of
G_{C}^{h}
.

Remark 53
.

(Some information on
\pi_{0}(G^{h})
)

(a) As the example
G=\widetilde{\mathop{{\rm SL}}}\nolimits_{2}({\mathbb{R}})
with the Cayley type
involution

\tau\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}a&-b\\
-c&d\end{pmatrix}

shows,
G^{h}
is in general not connected. It contains
Z(G)\cong{\mathbb{Z}}

and

(G^{h})_{e}=\exp({\mathbb{R}}h)\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ for }\hskip 10.00002pth=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}.

This shows that
\pi_{0}(G^{h})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}
.
This example also shows that, in general

G^{h}\not\subseteq G^{\tau^{G}},

because here
\tau^{G}
acts on
Z(G)
by inversion
(cf.
[
MN21
, Ex. 2.10(d)]
).

(b) If
G
is contained in
G_{\mathbb{C}}
, then

(G_{\mathbb{C}})^{h}
coincides with the centralizer of the circle group

\overline{\exp(ih)}
, hence is connected (
[
HN12
, Cor. 14.3.10]
). Therefore

G^{h}=(G_{\mathbb{C}})^{h}\cap G
is the fixed point group of the complex conjugation
(with respect to
G
) on the connected complex group
(G_{\mathbb{C}})^{h}
. Thus

\pi_{0}(G^{h})
is a finite elementary abelian
2
-group, i.e., isomorphic to

{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{k}
for some
k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
(
[
Lo69
, Thm. IV.3.4]
).

6
Wedge domains in
extendable causal symmetric spaces

In this section we turn to the wedge domains in compactly
causal symmetric spaces.
We
assume that
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C)
is extendable in the sense of
Subsection

2.1
, i.e., there exists a
G
-invariant
convex cone
C_{\mathfrak{g}}\subset{\mathfrak{g}}
such that
-\tau C_{\mathfrak{g}}=C_{\mathfrak{g}}
and
C_{\mathfrak{g}}\cap{\mathfrak{q}}=C
.
This always holds if
{\mathfrak{g}}
is reductive by the Extension Theorem
24
.
We write
{\mathfrak{g}}_{C}=C_{\mathfrak{g}}-C_{\mathfrak{g}}\trianglelefteq{\mathfrak{g}}
for the ideal generated by
C_{\mathfrak{g}}
and

G_{C}\trianglelefteq G
for the connected normal subgroup whose Lie algebra is
{\mathfrak{g}}_{C}
(see (
24
)).

The main result of this section
is Theorem
65
, asserting that
all three wedge domains in
M=G/H
are the same.
This is first proved for the special case

H=G^{\tau^{G}}
in Theorem
63
.
The proof of this theorem builds heavily on the group case
(Theorem
52
). We conclude this section with a brief
discussion of the assumption that the Euler element
h
is contained
in
{\mathfrak{h}}
, i.e., that the corresponding modular flow on
M
has a fixed point.

The following proposition identifies the basic connected components
of the wedge domains in
G^{\sharp}_{e}
.

Proposition 61
.

Let
(G,\tau^{G})
be a connected symmetric Lie group corresponding
to the modular compactly causal symmetric Lie algebra

({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C,h)
,

M_{G}:=G/G^{\tau^{G}}\cong G^{\sharp}_{e}=(G_{C}^{\sharp})_{e},

and let
C_{\mathfrak{g}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{g}}
be
a pointed invariant closed convex cone with

C_{\mathfrak{g}}\cap{\mathfrak{q}}=C\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ and }\hskip 10.00002pt\hskip 10.00002pt-\tau(C_{\mathfrak{g}})=-\tau_{h}(C_{\mathfrak{g}})=C_{\mathfrak{g}}.

For the real Olshanski semigroup

S(C_{\mathfrak{g}},h)=G_{C}^{h}\exp(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{c})
, the following assertions hold:

(a)

The basic connected component of the
wedge domains in
M_{G}
coincide:

W_{M_{G}}(h)_{e}=W_{M_{G}}^{+}(h)_{e}=W_{M_{G}}^{\rm KMS}(h)_{e},

and this domain can be obtained from the semigroup

S(C_{\mathfrak{g}},h)=G_{C}^{h}\exp(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{c})
as

S(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{0},h)^{\sharp}_{e}=\bigcup_{g\in(G_{C}^{h})_{e}}g\exp(C_{+}^{0}+C_{-}^{0})g^{\sharp}\cong(G_{C}^{h})_{e}\times_{(G_{C}^{h})_{e}^{\tau}}(C_{+}^{0}+C_{-}^{0}).

(56)

(b)

W_{M_{G}}(h)_{e}
is the orbit
S(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{0},h)_{e}.e
of the base point under
the action of the open semigroup
S(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{0},h)_{e}
on
{M_{G}}=(G_{C}^{\sharp})_{e}

by
g.x:=gxg^{\sharp}
.

Proof.

The flow
\alpha_{t}

commutes with the involution
\tau
.
If
m\in{M_{G}}\cong(G_{C}^{\sharp})_{e}\subseteq G_{C}^{\sharp}

is contained in
W_{M_{G}}^{\rm KMS}(h)

(Definition
46
), then

\alpha_{z}(m)\in\mathcal{T}_{M_{G}}(C)\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{G_{C}}(C_{\mathfrak{g}})=\Gamma_{G_{C}}(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{0})\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ for }\hskip 10.00002ptz\in\mathcal{S}_{\pi}

(cf. Remark
43
), so that
Theorem
52
implies that

W_{M_{G}}^{\rm KMS}(h)\subseteq{M_{G}}\cap W_{G_{C}}^{\rm KMS}(h)={M_{G}}\cap S(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{0},h)\subseteq S(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{0},h)^{\sharp},

(57)

and thus

\displaystyle W_{M_{G}}^{\rm KMS}(h)_{e}

\displaystyle\subseteq S(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{0},h)^{\sharp}_{e}=(G_{C}^{h})_{e}.\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits((C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{c,0})^{-\tau})

\displaystyle{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\eqref{eq:doubledcone2}}}}\ (G_{C}^{h})_{e}.\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits((C_{+}+C_{-})^{0})=W_{M_{G}}(h)_{e}.

(58)

The invariance of both sides under

(G_{C}^{h})_{e}
reduces the verification of the converse inclusion to showing
that

\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits(C_{+}^{0}+C_{-}^{0})\subseteq W_{M_{G}}^{\rm KMS}(h).

(59)

For
x_{\pm}\in C_{\pm}^{0}
and
0<t<\pi
, we have
\sin t>0
and hence

\mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits(e^{it}x_{+}+e^{-it}x_{-})=\sin(t)(x_{+}-x_{-})\in C^{0}.

Now (
59
) follows
from

\alpha_{it}\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits(x_{+}+x_{-})=\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits(e^{it}x_{+}+e^{-it}x_{-})
,

\exp(e^{it}x_{+}+e^{-it}x_{-})\in\Gamma_{G_{C}}(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{0})

and

\mathcal{T}_{M_{G}}(C)=\{ss^{\sharp}\colon s\in\Gamma_{G_{C}}(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{0})\}\subseteq\Gamma_{G_{C}}(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{0})=\mathcal{T}_{G_{C}}(C_{\mathfrak{g}})

(Remark
43
(a)).
We thus obtain

W_{M_{G}}^{\rm KMS}(h)_{e}=(G_{C}^{h})_{e}.\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits(C_{-}^{0}+C_{+}^{0})=W_{M_{G}}(h)_{e}.

(60)

Next we observe that
C^{0}=C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{0}\cap{\mathfrak{q}}

implies that, for
m=gg^{\sharp}\in{M_{G}}=(G_{C}^{\sharp})_{e}\subseteq G_{C}
, we have

V^{M_{G}}_{+}(m)=g.C^{0}.g^{\sharp}=T_{m}({M_{G}})\cap g.C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{0}.g^{\sharp}=T_{m}({M_{G}})\cap V_{+}^{G_{C}}(gg^{\sharp}).

(61)

In view of Theorem
52
, this shows that

W_{M_{G}}^{+}(h)=W_{G_{C}}^{+}(h)\cap{M_{G}}=S(C_{\mathfrak{g}},h)^{0}\cap{M_{G}}.

(62)

The semigroup
S(C_{\mathfrak{g}},h)=G_{C}^{h}\exp(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{c})
is a real Olshanski semigroup
corresponding to the involution
\tau_{h}
on
{\mathfrak{g}}

(Proposition
51
).
Further,

\tau
and
\tau_{h}
commute and the cone
C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{c}
is
-\tau
-invariant
(cf. (
16
)),
so that Lemma
A2
(3) shows that

S(C_{\mathfrak{g}},h)^{\sharp}_{e}=(G_{C}^{h})_{e}.\exp((C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{c})^{-\tau})=(G_{C}^{h})_{e}.\exp(C^{c})=(G_{C}^{h})_{e}.\exp(C_{+}+C_{-}).

We thus conclude with (
60
)
and Lemma
A2
(3,4) that

W_{M_{G}}^{+}(h)_{e}\ \ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\rm\ref{lem:real-olsh}}}}\ \ S(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{0},h)^{\sharp}_{e}\ \ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\eqref{eq:wedge-eq2}}}}\ \ W_{M_{G}}^{\rm KMS}(h)_{e}.

Now (a) follows from (
60
),
so that (b) is a direct consequence of (
6
). ∎

The preceding proposition identifies the basic components
of the wedge domains in the compactly causal symmetric space
M_{G}
.
We now prepare the ground for the analysis of the
other connected components.
We recall the polar wedge domain

W_{M}(h)=\bigcup_{m\in M^{\alpha}}\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{m}(C^{c}_{m}(h)^{0})\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\eqref{eq:exp-cmc}}}}\ \bigcup_{gH\in M^{\alpha}}g.\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{eH}(C^{c}(\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)^{-1}h)^{0})\subseteq M.

(63)

Comparing with Definition
46
and using that

M^{\alpha}_{eH}=G^{h}_{e}.eH
(Proposition
34
), we now see that

W_{M}(h)_{eH}=G^{h}_{e}.\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{eH}(C^{c}(h)^{0})=\bigcup_{m\in M^{\alpha}_{eH}}\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{m}(C^{c}_{m}(h)^{0}).

(64)

Remark 62
.

As the connected components of
M^{\alpha}
may have different dimensions
(Example
36
)
and the cones
C^{c}_{m}\subseteq T_{m}(M)
span complements to
T_{m}(M^{\alpha})
, they
may also be of different dimension.

Theorem 63
.

(Wedge domains in extendable compactly causal symmetric spaces)

Let
(G,\tau^{G})
be a connected symmetric Lie group corresponding
to the modular compactly causal symmetric Lie algebra

({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C,h)
,

M=M_{G}=G^{\sharp}_{e}\cong G/G^{\tau^{G}}\cong(G_{C}^{\sharp})_{e},

and let
C_{\mathfrak{g}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{g}}
be
a pointed invariant closed convex cone with

C_{\mathfrak{g}}\cap{\mathfrak{q}}=C\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ and }\hskip 10.00002pt\hskip 10.00002pt-\tau(C_{\mathfrak{g}})=-\tau_{h}(C_{\mathfrak{g}})=C_{\mathfrak{g}}.

Then

W_{M}(h)=W_{M}^{+}(h)=W_{M}^{\rm KMS}(h).

Proof.

We start with the discussion of the positivity domain

W_{M}^{+}(h)
. From (
62
) in the proof of
Proposition
61
we know that, for

S_{C}:=S(C_{\mathfrak{g}},h)=G_{C}^{h}\exp(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{c})\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ and }\hskip 10.00002ptS_{C}^{0}=G_{C}^{h}\exp(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{c,0}),

we have

W_{M}^{+}(h)=S_{C}^{0}\cap M=S_{C}^{0}\cap G^{\sharp}_{e}\subseteq(S_{C}^{0})^{\sharp}

is the union of all connected components of
(S_{C}^{0})^{\sharp}

which are contained in
M=G^{\sharp}_{e}
.
Information on these connected components comes from
Lemma
A2
,
which shows that each connected component of

(S_{C})^{\sharp}
intersects
(G_{C}^{h})^{\sharp}
, the
\alpha
-fixed point set
in
G_{C}^{\sharp}
. So let
g_{0}\in M^{\alpha}\cap S_{C}

and consider the involution
\gamma^{G}(g):=g_{0}^{-1}\tau(g)g_{0}
of
G

and the Lie algebra involution

\gamma:=\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g_{0})^{-1}\tau=\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(\tau(g))\tau\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(\tau(g))^{-1}\in\mathop{{\rm Aut}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}}).

By Lemma
A2
(5)(c), the connected component
of
S^{\sharp}_{C}
containing
g_{0}
is

g_{0}\{s\gamma^{G}(s)^{\sharp}\colon s\in S_{C,e}\}=g_{0}\bigcup_{g_{1}\in(G^{h}_{C})_{e}}g_{1}\exp(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{c,-\gamma})g_{0}^{-1}g_{1}^{\sharp}g_{0}.

(65)

We write
g_{0}=gg^{\sharp}
with
g\in G

and observe that
h^{\prime}=\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)^{-1}h\in{\mathfrak{h}}
, so that we also have

h^{\prime}=\tau(h^{\prime})=\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(\tau(g))^{-1}h
. The Lie algebra involution

\gamma
satisfies

\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g^{\sharp}){\mathfrak{g}}^{-\gamma}=\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(\tau(g))^{-1}{\mathfrak{g}}^{-\gamma}={\mathfrak{g}}^{-\tau}={\mathfrak{q}}.

(66)

Therefore the cone

C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{c,-\gamma}=C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{c}\cap{\mathfrak{g}}^{-\gamma}=\big(C_{\mathfrak{g}}\cap{\mathfrak{g}}_{1}(h)-C_{\mathfrak{g}}\cap{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}(h)\big)\cap{\mathfrak{g}}^{-\gamma}

satisfies

\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g^{\sharp})C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{c,-\gamma}=\big(C_{\mathfrak{g}}\cap{\mathfrak{g}}_{1}(h^{\prime})-C_{\mathfrak{g}}\cap{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}(h^{\prime})\big)\cap{\mathfrak{q}}=C\cap{\mathfrak{g}}_{1}(h^{\prime})-C\cap{\mathfrak{g}}_{-1}(h^{\prime})=:C^{c}(h^{\prime}),

(67)

where we have used that the projection
p_{\mathfrak{q}}\colon{\mathfrak{g}}\to{\mathfrak{q}}
commutes
with
\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits h^{\prime}
and maps
C_{\mathfrak{g}}
into itself.

We now arrive with (
65
) at

\displaystyle(S_{C})_{g_{0}}^{\sharp}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{g_{1}\in(G_{C}^{h})_{e}}g_{0}g_{1}\exp(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{c,-\gamma})g_{0}^{-1}g_{1}^{\sharp}g_{0}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{g_{1}\in(G_{C}^{h})_{e}}(g_{0}g_{1}g_{0}^{-1})g_{0}\exp(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{c,-\gamma})(g_{0}g_{1}g_{0}^{-1})^{\sharp}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{g_{2}\in(G^{h}_{C})_{e}}g_{2}g_{0}\exp(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{c,-\gamma})g_{2}^{\sharp}=(G^{h}_{C})_{e}.(g_{0}\exp(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{c,-\gamma}))

\displaystyle=(G^{h}_{C})_{e}.\big(gg^{\sharp}\exp(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{c,-\gamma})\big)=(G^{h}_{C})_{e}.\Big(g\exp\big(\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g^{\sharp})C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{c,-\gamma}\big)g^{\sharp}\Big)

\displaystyle\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\eqref{eq:conetrafo}}}}\ (G^{h}_{C})_{e}g.\exp(C^{c}(h^{\prime})).

For the interior of this connected component, we obtain

(S_{C}^{0})_{g_{0}}^{\sharp}=(G^{h}_{C})_{e}g.\exp(C^{c}(h^{\prime})^{0}).

(68)

The boundary of this domain contains in particular the connected
component

M^{\alpha}_{g_{0}}=(G^{h}_{C})_{e}g.e=\{g_{1}gg^{\sharp}g_{1}^{\sharp}\colon g_{1}\in(G^{h}_{C})_{e}\}

(cf. Proposition
34
).

We are now ready to use this information to identify the positivity domain.
As
X^{M}_{h}(g.m)\in V_{+}(g.m)=g.V_{+}(m)
is equivalent to

g^{-1}.X^{M}_{h}(g.m)=X^{M}_{h^{\prime}}(m)\in V_{+}(m)
, it follows that

g^{-1}.W_{M}^{+}(h)=W_{M}^{+}(h^{\prime})=W_{M}^{+}(\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)^{-1}h).

(69)

Combining Remark
47
with
Proposition
61
, we conclude that

\displaystyle W_{M}(h)

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{m\in M^{\alpha}}W_{M}(h)_{m}\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\ref{rem:6.5}}}}\ \bigcup_{gH\in M^{\alpha}}g.W_{M}(\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)^{-1}h)_{eH}

\displaystyle\subseteq\bigcup_{gH\in M^{\alpha}}g.W_{M}^{+}(\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)^{-1}h)\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\eqref{eq:posdom-trafo}}}}\ W_{M}^{+}(h).

(70)

To obtain the converse inclusion, we use (
62
) to obtain

\displaystyle W_{M}^{+}(h)

\displaystyle\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\eqref{eq:conmg}}}}\ S_{C}^{0}\cap M=S_{C}^{0}\cap G^{\sharp}_{e}=\bigcup_{g\in G,gg^{\sharp}\in G^{h}}(S_{C}^{0})^{\sharp}_{gg^{\sharp}}

\displaystyle\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\eqref{eq:concompfib}}}}\ \bigcup_{g\in G,\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)^{-1}h\in{\mathfrak{h}}}(G^{h}_{C})_{e}g.\exp(C^{c}(\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)^{-1}h))=W_{M}(h).

(71)

This completes the proof of the equality of
W_{M}(h)
and
W_{M}^{+}(h)
.

We now turn to the KMS wedge domain
W_{M}^{\rm KMS}(h)
.
Since
G
acts naturally on the tube domain
\mathcal{T}_{M}(C)
, the modular
flow
\alpha^{\prime}
defined by
h^{\prime}=\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)^{-1}h
on
M
satisfies

\alpha_{z}(g.m)=g.\alpha_{z}^{\prime}(m)\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ for }\hskip 10.00002ptz\in\mathcal{S}_{\pi}.

This shows that

g^{-1}.W_{M}^{\rm KMS}(h)=W_{M}^{\rm KMS}(h^{\prime})=W_{M}^{\rm KMS}(\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)^{-1}h).

(72)

As for the positivity domain, we now obtain with
Proposition
61
,

\displaystyle W_{M}(h)

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{m\in M^{\alpha}}W_{M}(h)_{m}=\bigcup_{gH\in M^{\alpha}}g.W_{M}(\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)^{-1}h)_{eH}

\displaystyle\subseteq\bigcup_{gH\in M^{\alpha}}g.W_{M}^{\rm KMS}(\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)^{-1}h)\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\eqref{eq:kms-trafo}}}}\ W_{M}^{\rm KMS}(h).

(73)

Combining (
57
) with (
6
), we finally get

W_{M}^{\rm KMS}(h)\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\subseteq}\limits^{\eqref{eq:weq}}}}\ S(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{0},h)\cap M=S_{C}^{0}\cap G^{\sharp}_{e}=W_{M}(h),

and therefore the equality
W_{M}(h)=W_{M}^{\rm KMS}(h)
.
∎

Remark 64
.

The relation in (
69
) shows that
any
h^{\prime}=\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)^{-1}h\in\mathcal{O}_{h}
leads to a wedge domain
W_{M}(h^{\prime})=g^{-1}.W_{M}(h)

that is a translate of the wedge domain
W_{M}(h)
. In this sense
the geometric structure of the set
W_{M}(h)
does not depend
on the choice of the Euler element in
\mathcal{O}_{h}\cap{\mathfrak{h}}
.
Nevertheless the structure of the basic connected component

W_{M}(h)_{eH}
of the wedge domain
W_{M}(h)
does (Example
36
).

Eventually, we turn to the general case, where

M=G/H
does not necessarily embed into
G
.

Theorem 65
.

(Wedge domains in extendable compactly causal symmetric spaces)

Let
(G,\tau^{G})
be a connected symmetric Lie group corresponding
to the modular compactly causal symmetric Lie algebra

({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C,h)
, assume that
\eta_{G}\colon G\to G_{\mathbb{C}}
has discrete kernel,
let
H\subseteq G^{\tau^{G}}
be an open subgroup,

M:=G/H
, and let
C_{\mathfrak{g}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{g}}
be
a pointed invariant closed convex cone with

C_{\mathfrak{g}}\cap{\mathfrak{q}}=C\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ and }\hskip 10.00002pt\hskip 10.00002pt-\tau(C_{\mathfrak{g}})=-\tau_{h}(C_{\mathfrak{g}})=C_{\mathfrak{g}}.

Then

W_{M}(h)=W_{M}^{+}(h)=W_{M}^{\rm KMS}(h).

(74)

Proof.

Let
\widetilde{\tau}\in\mathop{{\rm Aut}}\nolimits(\widetilde{G})
be the involution
integrating
\tau
.
In the simply connected covering
\widetilde{G}
of
G
,
the subgroup
\widetilde{G}^{\widetilde{\tau}}
is connected
by
[
Lo69
, Thm. IV.3.4]
, so that

[
HN12
, Cor. 11.1.14]
implies that

M_{\widetilde{G}}:=\widetilde{G}^{\sharp}_{e}\cong\widetilde{G}/\widetilde{G}^{\widetilde{\tau}}

is simply connected, hence can be identified with the simply connected
covering
\widetilde{M}
of
M
. Accordingly, the universal covering map is

q_{M}\colon\widetilde{M}=\widetilde{G}/\widetilde{G}^{\widetilde{\tau}}\to M=G/H,\hskip 10.00002ptg\widetilde{G}^{\widetilde{\tau}}\mapsto q_{G}(g)H,

where
q_{G}\colon\widetilde{G}\to G
is the universal covering of
G
.
From the isomorphism
\widetilde{M}\cong M_{\widetilde{G}}
and
Theorem
63
, we obtain the equalities

W_{\widetilde{M}}^{+}(h)=W_{\widetilde{M}}^{\rm KMS}(h)=W_{\widetilde{M}}(h).

(75)

The equivariance of
q_{M}
with respect to the modular flow implies that

W_{\widetilde{M}}^{+}(h)=q_{M}^{-1}(W_{M}^{+}(h)),\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ hence }\hskip 10.00002ptW_{M}^{+}(h)=q_{M}(W_{\widetilde{M}}^{+}(h)).

(76)

We also have coverings of the tube domains

\mathcal{T}_{\widetilde{M}}(C)\to\mathcal{T}_{M}(C)

which are equivariant with respect to the modular flow. This
entails

q_{M}^{-1}(W_{M}^{\rm KMS}(h))=W_{\widetilde{M}}^{\rm KMS}(h),\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ and thus }\hskip 10.00002ptW_{M}^{\rm KMS}(h)=q_{M}(W_{\widetilde{M}}^{\rm KMS}(h)).

(77)

In fact, the inclusion
\subseteq
follows directly from the equivariance.
For the converse inclusions we use the existence of lifts of trajectories of
the imaginary vector field
i\cdot X_{h}^{\mathcal{T}_{M}}
in
\mathcal{T}_{M}(C)
to

\mathcal{T}_{\widetilde{M}}(C)
.

Next we observe that

\widetilde{M}^{\alpha}=\{m\in\widetilde{M}\colon X_{h}^{\widetilde{M}}(m)=0\}=q_{M}^{-1}(M^{\alpha}).

(78)

As
T_{m}(q_{M})V_{+}(m)=V_{+}(q_{M}(m))
and
q_{M}
is
\alpha
-equivariant,
we have

T_{m}(q_{M})C_{m}^{c}=C_{q_{M}(m)}^{c},

and thus, for
m\in\widetilde{M}^{\alpha}
,

\displaystyle q_{M}(W_{\widetilde{M}}(h)_{m})

\displaystyle=q_{M}(\widetilde{G}^{h}_{e}.\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{m}(C_{m}^{c,0}))=G^{h}_{e}.\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{q_{M}(m)}\big(T_{m}(q_{M})C_{m}^{c,0}\big)

\displaystyle=G^{h}_{e}.\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{q_{M}(m)}\big(C_{q_{M}(m)}^{c,0}\big)=W_{M}(h)_{q_{M}(m)}.

Taking the union over all
\alpha
-fixed points, we arrive with
(
78
) at

q_{M}(W_{\widetilde{M}}(h))=W_{M}(h).

(79)

Applying
q_{M}
to the equalities in (
75
) now leads
with (
76
), (
77
) and
(
79
) to (
74
).
∎

Assumptions on the Euler element

We assume throughout that the Euler element
h

generating the modular flow on
M=G/H

is contained in
{\mathfrak{h}}={\mathfrak{g}}^{\tau}

(see in particular Proposition
27
(b)).

For group type spaces
({\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}},\tau_{\rm flip})
,
Euler elements in
{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}

are of the form
h=(h_{1},h_{2})
, where

h_{j}\in\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}})
are Euler elements.

5
5

5

If
{\mathfrak{g}}
is simple hermitian, then
\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}})
acts transitively
on
\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}})
by Proposition
27
, so that there are only
3
orbits of

\mathop{{\rm Inn}}\nolimits({\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}})
on
\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}})
, represented
by elements of the form
(h,h)
,
(h,0)
,
(0,h)
with

h\in\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}})
.

The following proposition shows that, for
h\not\in{\mathfrak{h}}
, the wedge
domains may degenerate drastically.

Proposition 66
.

Let
({\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}},\tau_{\rm flix},C)
be a
causal symmetric Lie algebra of group type.
If
h_{0}\in\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}})
, then
h:=(h_{0},0)\in\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}})
and

W_{G}(h)=W_{G}^{+}(h)=W_{G}^{\rm KMS}(h)=\emptyset.

Proof.

We write

C=\{(x,-x)\colon x\in C_{\mathfrak{g}}\}

for an invariant cone
C_{\mathfrak{g}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{g}}
.
The modular flow acts on
G
by left translations

\alpha_{t}(g)=\exp(th_{0})g
.
For
g\in G
, the inclusion

\exp([0,\pi]ih_{0})g\subseteq G\exp(iC_{\mathfrak{g}})

implies
h_{0}\in C_{\mathfrak{g}}
, contradicting the fact that

\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits h_{0}
is diagonalizable and the
elements of
C_{\mathfrak{g}}
have purely imaginary spectrum. This shows that

W_{G}^{\rm KMS}(h)=\emptyset.

Moreover,

({\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}})_{\pm 1}(h)={\mathfrak{g}}_{\pm 1}(h_{0})\oplus\{0\}

implies that
C_{\pm}=C\cap({\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}})_{\pm 1}(h)=\{0\}
, so that

W_{G}(h)=\emptyset
.

Finally, we observe that

p_{\mathfrak{q}}(\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g_{1},g_{2})^{-1}(h_{0},0))=\frac{1}{2}(\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g_{1}^{-1})h_{0},-\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g_{1})^{-1}h_{0})\in C^{0}

is equivalent to
\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g_{1}^{-1})h\in C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{0}
, which is never the case
because
C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{0}
consists of elliptic elements. This shows that

W_{G}^{+}(h)=\emptyset
.
∎

For right translations one argues similarly.
On the other hand, the Euler elements of the form
(h,h)

correspond to non-trivial wedge domains, such as

W_{G}(h)=G^{h}\exp(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{c,0})

(Theorem
52
).

7
Nets of standard subspaces

In this section we turn to representation theoretic aspects of
compactly causal symmetric spaces. We start with
introducing standard subspaces, the Brunetti–Guido–Longo construction
and recall the construction of nets of closed real subspaces
based on distribution vectors from
[
NÓ21a
]
.
In Subsection
7.4
we show in the general
Theorem
75
how the methods from
[
NÓ21a
]
can be used
to construct covariant nets of standard subspaces on
compactly causal symmetric spaces.

7.1
Standard subspaces

In this section we recall the notion of standard subspaces and modular objects and how those
object are related to anti-unitary representation of
{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a complex Hilbert space.
A closed real subspace
{\tt V}\subseteq\mathcal{H}

is called
standard
if

{\tt V}\cap i{\tt V}=\{0\}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ and }\hskip 10.00002pt\mathcal{H}=\overline{{\tt V}+i{\tt V}}

(80)

(see
[
Lo08
]
or
[
NÓØ21
]
for basic facts on standard subspaces). Associated to
every standard subspace is the closed densely defined conjugate linear
operator

\sigma_{\tt V}\colon{\tt V}+i{\tt V}\to\mathcal{H},\hskip 10.00002ptx+iy\mapsto x-iy

with polar decomposition

\sigma_{\tt V}=J_{\tt V}\Delta_{\tt V}^{1/2}.

The operator
J_{\tt V}
is an everywhere defined conjugation (an antiunitary
involution), and
the
modular operator

\Delta_{\tt V}
is a positive selfadjoint operator. These two operators satisfy the
modular relation

J_{\tt V}\Delta_{\tt V}J_{\tt V}=\Delta_{\tt V}^{-1}
.

Conversely, every
pair
(\Delta,J)
, consisting of a positive selfadjoint operator
\Delta

and a conjugation satisfying the modular relation

J\Delta J=\Delta^{-1}
defines a standard subspace via

{\tt V}:=\mathop{{\rm Fix}}\nolimits(J\Delta^{1/2}).

In this sense standard subspaces are in
bijection with pairs of modular objects.

We also note that, for any pair
(\Delta,J)
of modular objects,

U(e^{t}):=\Delta^{-it/2\pi}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ and }\hskip 10.00002ptU(-1):=J

(81)

define a continuous homomorphism

U\colon{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}\to\mathop{{\rm AU}}\nolimits(\mathcal{H})
mapping negative
numbers to antiunitary operators. Conversely, every
such homomorphism is obtained for

\Delta:=e^{2\pi i\cdot\partial U(1)}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ and }\hskip 10.00002ptJ:=U(-1).

(82)

7.2
The BGL construction

In this section we generalize the above construction
for
{\mathbb{R}}^{\times}
to more general Lie groups. For an involutive automorphism
\tau^{G}
on a connected
Lie group
G
, we consider
the group

G_{\tau}=G\rtimes\{\mathop{{\rm id}}\nolimits_{G},\tau^{G}\}.

An
antiunitary representation
of
G_{\tau}
is a homomorphism

U\colon G_{\tau}\to\mathop{{\rm AU}}\nolimits(\mathcal{H})
(the group of unitary and antiunitary operators on

\mathcal{H}
) such that
U
is strongly continuous and

J:=U(\tau^{G})
is a conjugation.
We then have

JU(g)J=U(\tau^{G}(g))\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ for }\hskip 10.00002ptg\in G.

For every
h\in{\mathfrak{g}}
fixed by
\tau:=\mathop{\bf L{}}\nolimits(\tau^{G})
, we then obtain a
standard subspace

{\tt V}:={\tt V}_{(h,\tau^{G},U)}\subseteq\mathcal{H},

specified by

J_{\tt V}=U(\tau^{G})\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ and }\hskip 10.00002pt\Delta_{{\tt V}}^{-it/2\pi}=U(\exp th)\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ for }\hskip 10.00002ptt\in{\mathbb{R}}

(83)

as in (
81
). Here the fact that

U(\tau^{G})
commutes with
U(\exp{\mathbb{R}}h)
implies the modular relation,
so that

{\tt V}=\mathop{{\rm Fix}}\nolimits(J_{\tt V}\Delta_{\tt V}^{1/2})

is a standard subspace of
\mathcal{H}
.
This assignment is called the
Brunetti–Guido–Longo
(BGL) construction
(see
[
BGL02
]
).

7.3
Nets of real subspaces on homogeneous spaces

With the BGL construction, standard subspaces can be associated to antiunitary
representations in abundance, but only a few of them
carry interesting geometric
information. In particular, we would like to understand
when a standard subspace of the form
{\tt V}_{(h,\tau^{G},U)}
arises from a
natural family
{\tt V}(\mathcal{O})
of real subspaces associated to open
subsets of a homogeneous space
M=G/P
and for which domains
\mathcal{O}\subseteq M

the subspace
{\tt V}(\mathcal{O})
is standard.

Covariant families of real subspaces of
\mathcal{H}

which are not necessarily standard are easy to construct
in any unitary representation
(U,\mathcal{H})
of
G

from distribution vectors
(see Appendix
B
for definitions and basic properties).
To a real linear subspace
{\tt E}\subseteq\mathcal{H}^{-\infty}
and
an open subset
\mathcal{O}\subseteq G
, we associate a
closed real subspace of
\mathcal{H}
by

{\sf H}_{\tt E}(\mathcal{O}):=\overline{{\rm span}_{\mathbb{R}}\big(U^{-\infty}(C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathcal{O},{\mathbb{R}})){\tt E}\big)}\subseteq\mathcal{H}.

(84)

With the projection map
q_{P}\colon G\to G/P
, we then obtain
on
G/P
a net of real subspaces by

{\sf H}^{G/P}_{\tt E}(\mathcal{O}):={\sf H}_{\tt E}(q_{P}^{-1}(\mathcal{O})).

If
{\tt E}
is
P
-invariant, then we actually have

{\sf H}_{\tt E}(\mathcal{O})={\sf H}_{\tt E}(\mathcal{O}P)
(
[
NÓ21a
, Lemma 2.11]
), so that any subspace

{\sf H}_{\tt E}(\mathcal{O})
attached to an open subset of
G
also corresponds to
an open subset of
G/P
.

7.4
Connecting wedge domains and standard subspaces

We now connect the BGL construction with the construction
based on real subspaces of distribution vectors.
For an Euler element
h\in{\mathfrak{g}}
we consider the involution

\tau_{h}=e^{\pi i\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits h}
and assume that the
Lie algebra involution

\tau_{h}
integrates to an involutive automorphism

\tau_{h}^{G}
of the connected Lie group
G
with Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}

(cf. Remark
212
).
Suppose that

(U,\mathcal{H})
is an antiunitary representation of
G_{\tau_{h}}=G\rtimes\{\mathbf{1},\tau_{h}^{G}\}
with

C_{U}
pointed and that
{\tt V}\subseteq\mathcal{H}
is the standard subspace
specified by the triple
(h,\tau_{h}^{G},U)
as in
(
83
) by the BGL construction. We also assume that

{\mathfrak{g}}={\mathfrak{g}}_{C}+{\mathbb{R}}h
for
{\mathfrak{g}}_{C}=C_{U}-C_{U}
(cf.
[
NÓ21a
, §3]
).
Then
[
Ne19
, Thms. 2.16, 3.4]
imply that, for
C_{\mathfrak{g}}=C_{U}
,

S_{\tt V}=G_{{\tt V}}\exp(C_{{\mathfrak{g}},+}+C_{{\mathfrak{g}},-}),\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ where }\hskip 10.00002pt\mathop{\bf L{}}\nolimits(G_{\tt V})={\mathfrak{g}}^{\tau_{h}},\hskip 20.00003ptC_{{\mathfrak{g}},\pm}=\pm C_{U}\cap{\mathfrak{g}}_{\pm 1}(h).

(85)

Definition 71
.

For an antiunitary representation

(U,\mathcal{H})
of
G_{\tau_{h}}
and
J:=U(\tau_{h}^{G})
, we write

\mathcal{H}^{-\infty}_{{\rm ext},J}
for the real linear space of all
distribution vectors
\eta\in\mathcal{H}^{-\infty}
, for which the orbit map

\alpha^{\eta}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathcal{H}^{-\infty},\hskip 10.00002pt\alpha^{\eta}(t):=\eta\circ U(\exp-th)

extends to a map on the closed strip
\overline{\mathcal{S}_{\pi}}
,
which is continuous with respect to the weak-
*
-topology on
the space of distribution vectors, weak-
*
-holomorphic
on the open strip
\mathcal{S}_{\pi}
and satisfies

\alpha^{\eta}(\pi i)=J_{\tt V}\eta

(86)

(cf.
[
NÓ21a
, Def. 3.6]
).

Theorem 72
.

(
[
NÓ21a
, Thm. 3.5, Prop. 3.13]
)

Let
{\tt E}\subseteq\mathcal{H}^{-\infty}

be a real subspace invariant
under
U(\exp({\mathbb{R}}h))
such that
{\sf H}_{\tt E}(G)
is total in
\mathcal{H}
.
Then the following assertions hold:

(a)

If
\emptyset\not=\mathcal{O}\subseteq G
is open,
then
{\sf H}_{\tt E}(\mathcal{O})
is total in
\mathcal{H}

(Reeh–Schlieder property)
.

(b)

If
{\tt E}\subseteq\mathcal{H}^{-\infty}_{{\rm ext},J}
,
then
{\sf H}_{\tt E}(S_{\tt V}^{0})={\tt V}

is the standard subspace from (
83
).

We shall use the following consequence of this theorem:

Corollary 73
.

For any non-empty open subset

\emptyset\not=\mathcal{O}\subseteq S_{{\tt V}}
with
\exp({\mathbb{R}}h)\mathcal{O}=\mathcal{O}
, we have

{\sf H}_{\tt E}(\mathcal{O})={\tt V}.

In particular,
{\sf H}_{\tt E}(S_{{\tt V},e}^{0})={\tt V}
.

Proof.

From Theorem
72
we obtain
{\sf H}_{\tt E}(\mathcal{O})\subseteq{\sf H}_{\tt E}(S_{\tt V}^{0})={\tt V}
,
so that

{\sf H}_{\tt E}(\mathcal{O})\cap i{\sf H}_{\tt E}(\mathcal{O})\subseteq{\tt V}\cap i{\tt V}=\{0\}.

Further, Theorem
72
(a) implies that
{\sf H}_{\tt E}(\mathcal{O})
is total, hence
standard. Now the invariance of
{\sf H}_{\tt E}(\mathcal{O})
under the modular
group
U(\exp{\mathbb{R}}h)
of
{\tt V}
shows equality (
[
NÓ21a
, Lemma 3.4]
).
∎

Corollary 74
.

Let
P\subseteq G
be a closed subgroup with

h\in\mathop{\bf L{}}\nolimits(P)
and

\eta\in\mathcal{H}^{-\infty}
fixed by
U(P)
and
J
which is cyclic in the sense
that
{\sf H}_{{\mathbb{R}}\eta}(G)
is total in
\mathcal{H}
. Then

{\sf H}_{{\mathbb{R}}\eta}(S_{{\tt V},e}^{0})={\sf H}^{G/P}_{{\mathbb{R}}\eta}(S_{{\tt V},e}^{0}P/P)={\tt V}.

Proof.

We put
{\tt E}:={\mathbb{R}}\eta
. As
\eta
is fixed by
P
,
it is
U(P)
-invariant, hence in particular invariant under

U(\exp{\mathbb{R}}h)
. As
J\eta=\eta
, we have

{\tt E}\subseteq\mathcal{H}^{-\infty}_{{\rm ext},J}
, so that
Corollary
73

shows that
{\sf H}_{\tt E}(S_{{\tt V},e}^{0})={\tt V}
. For the second assertion we
use
[
NÓ21a
, Lemma 2.11]
to obtain

{\tt V}={\sf H}_{\tt E}(S_{{\tt V},e}^{0})={\sf H}_{\tt E}(S_{{\tt V},e}^{0}P)={\sf H}_{\tt E}^{G/P}(S_{{\tt V},e}^{0}P/P).\qed

The preceding corollary shows that the orbit

S_{{\tt V},e}^{0}P/P=S_{{\tt V},e}^{0}.eP
of the base point
eP\in G/P

under the open subsemigroup
S_{{\tt V},e}^{0}
is a natural domain
to which the standard subspace can be associated.

For compactly causal symmetric spaces, we thus obtain the following
concretization:

Theorem 75
.

Let
M=G/H
be a compactly causal modular symmetric space with
infinitesimal data
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C,h)
.
Further, let
(U,\mathcal{H})
be an anti-unitary representation of
G_{\tau_{h}}

whose positive cone
C_{U}
is pointed,

{\mathfrak{g}}=C_{U}-C_{U}+{\mathbb{R}}h
, and
C=C_{U}\cap{\mathfrak{q}}
.
Let
\eta\in\mathcal{H}^{-\infty}
be fixed by
U(H)
and
J=U(\tau_{h}^{G})

such that
{\sf H}_{{\mathbb{R}}\eta}(G)
is total in
\mathcal{H}
. Then

{\sf H}^{G/H}_{{\mathbb{R}}\eta}(W_{M}(h)_{eH})={\tt V}.

Proof.

In view of Corollary
74
,
it suffices to show that
S_{{\tt V},e}^{0}.eH=W_{M}(h)_{eH}
.
As
C_{U}
is pointed, the kernel of
U
is discrete.
Therefore (
85
) and

G^{h}_{e}\subseteq G^{h,\tau_{h}^{G}}\subseteq G_{\tt V}
imply

S_{{\tt V},e}=G^{h}_{e}\exp(C_{U}^{c}).

Now Proposition
61
(b) leads to

S_{{\tt V},e}^{0}.eH=(G^{h})_{e}.\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{eH}(C^{c,0})=W_{M}(h)_{eH}.

This completes the proof.
∎

8
Nets of standard subspaces
on compactly causal symmetric spaces

The main result of the preceding section (Theorem
75
) describes
how certain
antiunitary representations of
G_{\tau_{h}}
lead to interesting nets of
standard subspaces on compactly causal symmetric spaces.
These representations where supposed to have a positive cone
C_{U}

which is pointed and satisfies
{\mathfrak{g}}=C_{U}-C_{U}+{\mathbb{R}}h
,
and a cyclic distribution vector in

\mathcal{H}^{-\infty}
, fixed by
U(H)
and
J=U(\tau^{G}_{h})
.
In this section we construct such representations explicitly
in spaces of Hilbert–Schmidt operators
\mathcal{H}_{\rho}\subseteq B_{2}(\mathcal{K})
, where

(\rho,\mathcal{K})
is an antiunitary representation of
G_{\tau_{h}}
which is a
finite sum of irreducible representations. This is done in three steps:
First we recall from
[
Ne00
,
Ne19
]
some results on
the representations
(\rho,\mathcal{K})
of
G_{\tau_{h}}
,
then we use these representations to construct nets of standard subspaces
on the symmetric space of group type
G\cong(G\times G)/\Delta_{G}
,
and finally we use the twisted embedding

G\to G\times G,g\mapsto(g,\tau^{G}(g))
to obtain
pullback representations of
G_{\tau_{h}}
that can be used to obtain with
Theorem
75
nets of standard subspaces on
M=G/H
.

In this section we fix the following notation:
{\mathfrak{g}}
is a

semisimple
Lie algebra
and
C_{\mathfrak{g}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{g}}
is a pointed generating invariant cone.
Further,
G
is a connected Lie group with Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
,

\tau^{G}
an involutive automorphism of
G
, and
the corresponding automorphism

\tau=\mathop{\bf L{}}\nolimits(\tau^{G})
of
{\mathfrak{g}}
satisfies
-\tau(C_{\mathfrak{g}})=C_{\mathfrak{g}}
.
This implies that
C_{\mathfrak{q}}:=C_{\mathfrak{g}}\cap{\mathfrak{q}}
,
{\mathfrak{q}}={\mathfrak{g}}^{-\tau}
,
has interior points, so that
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C_{\mathfrak{q}})
is a compactly causal symmetric Lie algebra.
We also fix an Euler element
h\in{\mathfrak{h}}={\mathfrak{g}}^{\tau}

and assume that the Lie algebra involution

\tau_{h}=e^{\pi i\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits h}
integrates to an involutive automorphism

\tau_{h}^{G}
of
G
and that
-\tau_{h}(C_{\mathfrak{g}})=C_{\mathfrak{g}}
and

\tau_{h}^{G}(H)=H
.

8.1
C_{\mathfrak{g}}
-positive representations and
standard subspaces

To prepare our construction of nets of standard subspaces on
compactly causal symmetric spaces, we first collect some information on

C_{\mathfrak{g}}
-positive representations of
G
.
We thus obtain an interface to
[
Ne19
]
,
[
NÓØ21
]
and
[
NÓ21a
]
from which some
results and constructions will be used below.

Let
(\rho,\mathcal{K})
be an antiunitary
C_{\mathfrak{g}}
-positive representation
of
G_{\tau_{h}}:=G\rtimes\{\mathop{{\rm id}}\nolimits_{G},\tau_{h}^{G}\}
and write

J_{\mathcal{K}}:=\rho(\tau_{h}^{G})
for the corresponding conjugation on
\mathcal{K}
.
Then there exists a unique standard subspace
{\tt V}_{\mathcal{K}}\subseteq\mathcal{K}
with

J_{{\tt V}_{\mathcal{K}}}=J_{\mathcal{K}}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ and }\hskip 10.00002pt\Delta_{{\tt V}_{\mathcal{K}}}^{-it/2\pi}=\rho(\exp th)\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ for }\hskip 10.00002ptt\in{\mathbb{R}}.

Let

G_{{\tt V}_{\mathcal{K}}}=\{g\in G\colon\rho(g){\tt V}_{\mathcal{K}}={\tt V}\}.

Assume that
\rho
has a discrete kernel. Then
the derived representation
{\tt d}\rho
is injective, so that

\rho(G_{{\tt V}_{\mathcal{K}}})
commutes with
{\tt d}\rho(h)
and thus

G_{{\tt V}_{\mathcal{K}}}\subseteq G^{h}
. More specifically,

G_{{\tt V}_{\mathcal{K}}}=\{g\in G^{h}\colon\rho(g)J_{\mathcal{K}}\rho(g)^{-1}=J_{\mathcal{K}}\}=\{g\in G^{h}\colon g\tau_{h}^{G}(g)^{-1}\in\ker(\rho)\}.

(87)

This is an open subgroup of
G^{h}
that only depends on the
kernel of
\rho
.

Furthermore, the discreteness of the kernel of
\rho
implies
that its positive cone

C_{\rho}\subseteq{\mathfrak{g}}
is pointed. It is also generating because it contains

C_{\mathfrak{g}}
. Now
[
Ne19
, Thms. 2.16, 3.4]
imply that

S_{{\tt V}_{\mathcal{K}}}:=\{g\in G\colon\rho(g){\tt V}_{\mathcal{K}}\subseteq{\tt V}_{\mathcal{K}}\}=G_{{\tt V}_{\mathcal{K}}}\exp(C_{\rho}^{c}),

(88)

where

C_{\rho}^{c}:=C_{\rho,+}+C_{\rho,-}\hskip 10.00002pt\text{with}\hskip 10.00002ptC_{\rho,\pm}:=\pm C_{\rho}\cap{\mathfrak{g}}_{\pm 1}(h).

Note that
C_{\rho}^{c}
is a
G_{{\tt V}_{\mathcal{K}}}
-invariant hyperbolic
convex cone in
{\mathfrak{g}}^{-\tau_{h}}
.
Since
C_{\rho}^{c}
coincides with
C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{c}
by
Corollary
210
, and

-\tau_{h}(C_{\rho})=C_{\rho}
, the interior of this cone
intersects
{\mathfrak{g}}^{-\tau_{h}}
, so that

C_{\rho}^{c}
generates
{\mathfrak{g}}^{-\tau_{h}}
. Therefore

S_{{\tt V}_{\mathcal{K}}}=G_{{\tt V}_{\mathcal{K}}}\exp(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{c}),

(89)

and this semigroup only depends on
\rho
through its unit group
G_{{\tt V}_{\mathcal{K}}}
.
We conclude that the semigroup

S_{V_{\mathcal{K}}}
only depends on the representation

\rho
through the discrete subgroup
\ker(\rho)
.

For a more detailed discussion of irreducible antiunitary representations
of
G_{\tau_{h}}
, we refer to Proposition 4.6 and Remark 4.10 in

[
NÓ21a
]
. They restrict to representations of
G
which are
either irreducible or a direct sum of two inequivalent representations
exchanged by twisting with
\tau_{h}^{G}
.

For
1\leq p<\infty
we denote by
B_{p}(\mathcal{H})
the space of linear operators with finite
p
-Schatten norm:

B_{p}(\mathcal{H})=\{T\in B(\mathcal{H})\colon\|T\|_{p}=\mathop{{\rm tr}}\nolimits(|T_{p}|)^{1/p}<\infty\}.

Then
B_{1}(\mathcal{H})
is the space of trace class operators and
B_{2}(\mathcal{H})
is the space of Hilbert–Schmidt operators.

Proposition 81
.

There exists an injective antiunitary representation

(\rho,\mathcal{K})
of
G_{\tau_{h}}
such that

(a)

C_{\rho}\supseteq C_{\mathfrak{g}}

(b)

\rho
is a finite direct sum
of irreducible representations.

(c)

\rho
is a trace-class representation,
i.e.,
\rho(C^{\infty}_{c}(G))\subseteq B_{1}(\mathcal{H})
.

Proof.

From
[
Ne00
, Thm. XI.5.2]
we know that
there exist finitely many irreducible representations

(\pi_{j},\mathcal{K}_{j})_{j\in F}
of
G
with
C_{\pi_{j}}\supseteq C_{\mathfrak{g}}
and

\bigcap_{j\in F}\ker(\pi_{j})=\{e\}
.
Then
\pi:=\oplus_{j\in F}\pi_{j}
is injective and

\rho:=\pi\oplus(\pi^{*}\circ\tau_{h}^{G})

extends to an injective antiunitary representation of
G_{\tau_{h}}
. As

-\tau_{h}(C_{\mathfrak{g}})=C_{\mathfrak{g}}
, we also have
C_{\mathfrak{g}}\subseteq C_{\rho}
.
This proves (a) and (b).
That
\rho
is a trace class representation follows from
the fact that all representations
\pi_{j}

are trace class (
[
Ne00
, Thm. X.4.10]
).
∎

8.2
The group case: biinvariant nets on Lie groups

On
G
we obtain by the assignment

g\mapsto V_{+}(g):=g.C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{0}\subseteq T_{g}(G)\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ for }\hskip 10.00002ptg\in G,

a biinvariant cone field, turning
G
into a compactly causal
symmetric space on which the group
G\times G
acts by left and
right translations. Its infinitesimal
data is represented by the compactly causal symmetric Lie algebra

({\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}})
of group type, where

\tau(x,y)=(y,x)\hskip 10.00002pt\text{and}\hskip 10.00002ptC=\{(y,-y)\colon y\in C_{\mathfrak{g}}\}\subset({\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}})^{-\tau}

(90)

We also fix an Euler element
h\in\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}})
.

We want to apply
[
NÓ21a
]
to representations of
G\times G
,
so we assume that
{\mathfrak{g}}
is
semisimple
.
6
6

6

For generalization of some constructions in
[
NÓ21a
]

to non-reductive Lie algebras, we refer to
[
Oeh21
]
.

To this end, we consider in
{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}
the
invariant cone

C_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}}:=C_{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus-C_{\mathfrak{g}}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ which satisfies }\hskip 10.00002pt-\tau(C_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}})=C_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}}.

Remark 82
.

(Irreducible representations)

As
C_{\mathfrak{g}}
-positive representations of
G

are type I (
[
Ne00
, Thm. X.6.21]
),
irreducible
C_{\mathfrak{g}}
-positive representations of
G\times G
have the form

\pi=\rho_{1}\boxtimes\rho_{2}^{*},\hskip 10.00002pt\pi(g_{1},g_{2})=\rho_{1}(g_{1})\otimes\rho_{2}^{*}(g_{2})

where
(\rho_{1},\mathcal{K}_{1})
and
(\rho_{2},\mathcal{K}_{2})

are irreducible
C_{\mathfrak{g}}
-positive representations of
G
.

For the existence of an antiunitary extension to

(G\times G)_{\tau_{h}\times\tau_{h}}=(G\times G)\rtimes\{\mathop{{\rm id}}\nolimits,\tau_{h}^{G}\times\tau_{h}^{G}\},

we need that

\pi^{*}\cong\pi\circ(\tau_{h}^{G}\times\tau_{h}^{G})=(\rho_{1}\circ\tau_{h}^{G})\boxtimes(\rho_{2}\circ\tau_{h}^{G})^{*},

(91)

and this is equivalent to

\rho_{j}^{*}\cong\rho_{j}\circ\tau_{h}^{G}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ for }\hskip 10.00002ptj=1,2.

(92)

If these two conditions are satisfied, then
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}
extend
to antiunitary representations of
G_{\tau_{h}}
and
\pi
extends
to
(G\times G)_{\tau_{h}\times\tau_{h}}
by

\pi(\tau_{h}^{G}\times\tau_{h}^{G}):=\rho_{1}(\tau_{h}^{G})\otimes\rho_{2}(\tau_{h}^{G}).

If (
92
) is not satisfied, then
the canonical antiunitary extension of the representation

\pi\oplus(\pi^{*}\circ(\tau_{h}^{G}\times\tau_{h}^{G}))

is irreducible,
although its restriction to
G\times G
decomposes into two
inequivalent irreducible constituents.

Let
(\rho_{j},\mathcal{K}_{j})
,
j=1,2
, be two antiunitary

C_{\mathfrak{g}}
-positive representations of
G_{\tau_{h}}
which need not
be irreducible (cf. Subsection
8.1
).
Then

\mathcal{H}:=B_{2}(\mathcal{K}_{2},\mathcal{K}_{1})\cong\mathcal{K}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{K}_{2}^{*}

carries
the unitary representation
(\mathcal{H},\pi)
of
G\times G
defined by

\pi(g_{1},g_{2})A=\rho_{1}(g_{1})A\rho_{2}(g_{2})^{-1},

and we extend this representation to an antiunitary
representation of
(G\times G)_{\tau_{h}\times\tau_{h}}
by

\pi(\tau_{h}^{G}\times\tau_{h}^{G}):=J,\hskip 10.00002ptJ(A):=J_{\mathcal{K}_{1}}AJ_{\mathcal{K}_{2}},\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ where }\hskip 10.00002ptJ_{\mathcal{K}_{j}}:=\rho_{j}(\tau_{h}^{G}).

Lemma 83
.

Let
{\tt V}_{j}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{j}
,
j=1,2
, be standard subspaces
with the modular objects
(\Delta_{j},J_{j})
,
j=1,2
where

\Delta_{j}=\Delta_{{\tt V}_{j}},J_{j}=J_{{\tt V}_{j}}
. Then

{\tt V}_{1}\otimes{\tt V}_{2}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2}

is the standard subspace
{\tt V}
corresponding to the modular group defined by

\Delta^{it}=\Delta_{1}^{it}\otimes\Delta_{2}^{it}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ for }\hskip 10.00002ptt\in{\mathbb{R}},\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ and the conjugation }\hskip 10.00002ptJ=J_{1}\otimes J_{2}.

(93)

Proof.

The tensor product
{\sf H}:={\tt V}_{1}\otimes{\tt V}_{2}
is a closed
real subspace of
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{2}
which we want to compare
with the standard subspace
{\tt V}:=\mathop{{\rm Fix}}\nolimits(J\Delta^{1/2})
defined by
the pair
(\Delta,J)
from (
93
).
For
\xi_{1}\in{\tt V}_{1}
and
\xi_{2}\in{\tt V}_{2}
, we have

\Delta_{1}^{1/2}\xi_{1}=J_{1}\xi_{1}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ and }\hskip 10.00002pt\Delta_{2}^{1/2}\xi_{2}=J_{2}\xi_{2}.

For
\xi:=\xi_{1}\otimes\xi_{2}
the orbit map

{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathcal{H},\hskip 10.00002ptt\mapsto\Delta^{-it/2\pi}\xi=\Delta_{1}^{-it/2\pi}\xi_{1}\otimes\Delta_{2}^{-it/2\pi}\xi_{2}

extends holomorphically to the closure of the strip

\mathcal{S}_{\pi}\subseteq{\mathbb{C}}
by

z\mapsto\Delta_{1}^{-iz/2\pi}\xi_{1}\otimes\Delta_{2}^{-iz/2\pi}\xi_{2}

(cf.
[
NÓØ21
, Prop. 2.1]
). This implies that

\Delta^{1/2}\xi=\Delta_{1}^{1/2}\xi_{1}\otimes\Delta_{2}^{1/2}\xi_{2}=J_{1}\xi_{1}\otimes J_{2}\xi_{2}=J\xi.

Therefore
\xi\in{\tt V}
(
[
NÓØ21
, Prop. 2.1]
). This argument shows that

{\sf H}\subseteq{\tt V}
. As
{\sf H}
is generating in
\mathcal{H}

and invariant under the modular group

\Delta^{-it/2\pi}=\Delta_{1}^{-it/2\pi}\otimes\Delta_{2}^{-it/2\pi}
,

[
Lo08
, Cor. 2.1.8]
implies that
{\sf H}={\tt V}
.
∎

Corollary 84
.

Write
\mathcal{K}_{2}^{\rm op}\cong\mathcal{K}_{2}^{*}
for
\mathcal{K}_{2}
, endowed
with the opposite complex structure.
Then the standard subspace
{\tt V}\subseteq\mathcal{K}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{K}_{2}^{\rm op}

specified by the Lie algebra element
(h,h)\in{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}
and
J

is the tensor product
{\tt V}={\tt V}_{1}\otimes{\tt V}_{2}^{\prime}
,
constructed from the corresponding standard subspaces
{\tt V}_{j}\subseteq\mathcal{K}_{j}
.

Proof.

In view of the preceding lemma, we only have to observe
that, changing the complex structure on
\mathcal{K}_{2}
, corresponds to replacing
the modular operator
\Delta_{{\tt V}_{2}}=e^{2\pi i\partial\rho_{2}(h)}
by its inverse.
∎

We now assume that

\rho:=\rho_{1}=\rho_{2}
is a sum of
finitely many irreducible

C_{\mathfrak{g}}
-positive antiunitary representations of
G_{\tau_{h}}
.
Then

\pi(g_{1},g_{2})A=\rho(g_{1})A\rho(g_{2})^{-1}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ and }\hskip 10.00002ptJ(A)=J_{\mathcal{K}}AJ_{\mathcal{K}}.

By
[
Ne00
, Thm. X.4.10]

\rho
is a trace class representation, i.e.,

\rho(C^{\infty}_{c}(G))\subseteq B_{1}(\mathcal{H})
.
This implies that the integrated representation
defines a continuous linear map
\rho\colon C_{c}^{\infty}(G)\to B_{1}(\mathcal{K})

(
[
DNSZ16
, Thm. 1.3]
).

Lemma 85
.

The following assertions hold:

(a)

For the left multiplication
representation of
G
on
B_{2}(\mathcal{K})
,

B_{2}(\mathcal{K})^{\infty}=\{A\in B(\mathcal{K})\colon A\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathcal{K}^{\infty}\}\subseteq B_{1}(\mathcal{K})

(94)

and the inclusion
B_{2}(\mathcal{K})^{\infty}\to B_{1}(\mathcal{K})
