is continuous with respect to the norm topology on
B_{1}(\mathcal{K})

and the natural Fréchet topology on
B_{2}(\mathcal{K})^{\infty}
.

(b)

We have a
(G\times G)
-equivariant map

\rho^{*}\colon B(\mathcal{K})\to C^{-\infty}(G),\hskip 10.00002pt\rho^{*}(A)(\xi):=\mathop{{\rm tr}}\nolimits(\rho(\xi)^{*}A),\hskip 10.00002pt\xi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G).

(c)

The space of smooth vectors for the
representation
\pi
of
G\times G
on
B_{2}(\mathcal{K})
is

B_{2}(\mathcal{K})^{\infty,\pi}=\{A\in B(\mathcal{K})\colon A\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathcal{K}^{\infty},A^{*}\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathcal{K}^{\infty}\}.

(d)

The map

B(\mathcal{K})\to B_{2}(\mathcal{K})^{-\infty,\pi},A\mapsto\eta_{A}
,
with
\eta_{A}(B)=\mathop{{\rm tr}}\nolimits(B^{*}A)
is injective
and equivariant with respect to the representation
of
(G\times G)_{\tau_{h}\times\tau_{h}}
on
B(\mathcal{H})
, given by

(g_{1},g_{2}).A:=\rho(g_{1})A\rho(g_{2})^{-1}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ for }\hskip 10.00002ptg_{1},g_{2}\in G\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ and }\hskip 10.00002pt(\tau_{h}^{G}\times\tau_{h}^{G}).A=J_{\mathcal{K}}AJ_{\mathcal{K}}.

Note that (a) implies in particular that
each bounded operator on
\mathcal{K}
defines a distribution
vector for the left multiplication representation on
B_{2}(\mathcal{K})
,
hence also for the representation
\pi
of
G\times G
.
The smooth vectors for
G\times G

coincide with the algebra of so-called
Schwartz operators
,
i.e., operators which remain bounded when composed on the left and
the right with elements of the enveloping algebra acting on smooth vectors
(cf.
[
DNSZ16
]
,
[
KKW16
]
).

Proof.

(a) The first equality in (
94
)
follows from
[
DNSZ16
, Prop. 1.7]
.

Let
A\in B_{2}(\mathcal{K})
be a smooth vector for the
left multiplication representation of
G
.
By the Dixmier–Malliavin Theorem,
A
is a finite sum
of operators of the form
\rho(\xi_{j})A_{j}
,
A_{j}\in B_{2}(\mathcal{K})
,

\xi_{j}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G)
, and these are trace class because

(\rho,\mathcal{K})
is a trace class representation.

For the last assertion, we have to show that the inclusion map

\Gamma\colon B_{2}(\mathcal{K})^{\infty}\to B_{1}(\mathcal{K})

is continuous. This is a linear map from a Fréchet space to a Banach space.
Therefore it suffices to write it as a pointwise limit of a
sequence
(\Gamma_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
of continuous linear maps
(
[
DNSZ16
, Lemma 1.2]
).
So let
\xi_{n}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G)
be a
\delta
-sequence
and
\Gamma_{n}(B):=\rho(\xi_{n})B
.
These maps are continuous from
B_{2}(\mathcal{K})\to B_{1}(\mathcal{K})
,
hence in particular from
B_{2}(\mathcal{K})^{\infty}\to B_{1}(\mathcal{K})
.
Since the left multiplication representation of
G
on
the Banach space
B_{1}(\mathcal{K})
is continuous,

\|\Gamma_{n}(B)-B\|_{1}\to 0
holds for every
B\in B_{1}(\mathcal{H})\supseteq B_{2}(\mathcal{H})^{\infty}
.

(b) From the continuous linear map

\rho\colon C^{\infty}_{c}(G)\to B_{1}(\mathcal{K})
, we obtain the map
\rho^{*}

by taking adjoints. The equivariance for the action of

G\times G
on both sides follows from

\displaystyle\rho^{*}\big(\rho(g_{1})A\rho(g_{2})^{-1}\big)(\xi)

\displaystyle=\mathop{{\rm tr}}\nolimits\big(\rho(\xi)^{*}\rho(g_{1})A\rho(g_{2})^{-1}\big)=\mathop{{\rm tr}}\nolimits\big(\big(\rho(g_{1})^{-1}\rho(\xi)\rho(g_{2})\big)^{*}A\big)

\displaystyle=((g_{1},g_{2}).\rho^{*}(A))(\xi).

(c) follows from
[
DNSZ16
, Cor. 1.8]
.

(d) That every functional
\eta_{A}
defines a distribution
vector follows from (a). For the injectivity of the assignment,
we first observe that
all rank one-operators
P_{\xi,\eta}=|\xi\rangle\langle\eta|
,
defined by smooth vectors
\xi,\eta\in\mathcal{K}^{\infty}
,
are contained in
B_{2}(\mathcal{K})^{\infty}
.
For these we have

\eta_{A}(P_{\xi,\eta})=\mathop{{\rm tr}}\nolimits(P_{\xi,\eta}^{*}A)=\mathop{{\rm tr}}\nolimits(P_{\eta,\xi}A)=\langle\xi,A\eta\rangle,

and since
\mathcal{K}^{\infty}
is dense in
\mathcal{K}
, any bounded operator
A
is determined
by
\eta_{A}
.

For the equivariance, we calculate

\eta_{A}(\pi(g_{1},g_{2})^{-1}B)=\mathop{{\rm tr}}\nolimits((\rho(g_{1})^{*}B\rho(g_{2}))^{*}A)=\mathop{{\rm tr}}\nolimits(\rho(g_{2})^{*}B^{*}\rho(g_{1})A)=\eta_{(g_{1},g_{2}).A}(B).

Further

\eta_{J_{\mathcal{K}}AJ_{\mathcal{K}}}(B)=\mathop{{\rm tr}}\nolimits(B^{*}J_{\mathcal{K}}AJ_{\mathcal{K}})=\overline{\mathop{{\rm tr}}\nolimits(J_{\mathcal{K}}B^{*}J_{\mathcal{K}}A)}=\overline{\mathop{{\rm tr}}\nolimits((J_{\mathcal{K}}BJ_{\mathcal{K}})^{*}A)}=\pi^{-\infty}(\tau_{h}^{G}\times\tau_{h}^{G})(\eta_{A})(B).\qed

We want exhibit concrete contexts, where the assumptions of
Theorem
75
are satisfied. This will be achieved in Theorem
87

and the ground is prepared by the following proposition.
Recall that we assume in this section that
{\mathfrak{g}}
is semisimple.
In the following proposition this is crucial to use
Corollary
210
in the proof (d).

Proposition 86
.

Assume that
\ker\rho
is discrete. Let

\mathcal{H}_{\rho}\subseteq\mathcal{H}=B_{2}(\mathcal{K})
denote the closed subspace generated by
the image
\rho(C^{\infty}_{c}(G))
of the integrated representation of the
convolution algebra of test functions and denote the corresponding
subrepresentation of
\pi
by
\pi_{\rho}
. Then the following
assertions hold:

(a)

\eta^{0}:=\mathop{{\rm tr}}\nolimits|_{\mathcal{H}_{\rho}^{\infty}}\in\mathcal{H}_{\rho}^{-\infty}

is a cyclic distribution vector invariant under the diagonal subgroup
\Delta_{G}=\{(g,g)\colon g\in G\}
and
J(A)=J_{\mathcal{K}}AJ_{\mathcal{K}}
.

(b)

\mathcal{H}_{\rho}
is
J
-invariant and

{\tt E}:={\mathbb{R}}\eta^{0}\subseteq(\mathcal{H}^{-\infty}_{\rho})_{{\rm ext},J}
.

(c)

\ker(\pi_{\rho})
is discrete.

(d)

Let
{\tt V}_{\rho}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{\rho}
be the standard subspace
with the modular data

J_{{\tt V}_{\rho}}=J|_{\mathcal{H}_{\rho}}
and

\Delta_{{\tt V}_{\rho}}=e^{2\pi i\cdot\partial\pi(h,h)}
.
Then

S_{{\tt V}_{\rho}}=\{g\in G\times G\colon\pi_{\rho}(g){\tt V}_{\rho}\subseteq{\tt V}_{\rho}\}=(G\times G)_{{\tt V}_{\rho}}\exp(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{c}\oplus-C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{c}).

Proof.

(a), (b) By Lemma
85
(d), the trace
\eta_{\mathbf{1}}=\mathop{{\rm tr}}\nolimits\in\mathcal{H}^{-\infty}=B_{2}(\mathcal{K})^{-\infty}
is
invariant under
J
and the diagonal subgroup
\Delta_{G}
.
In particular, it is fixed under the modular group

\exp({\mathbb{R}}(h,h))
, which implies (b).

If we consider
\mathbf{1}\in B(\mathcal{H})
as a distribution vector, we have
for
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G)
the relation

\displaystyle\pi^{-\infty}(\xi_{1}\otimes\xi_{2})\mathbf{1}

\displaystyle=\rho(\xi_{1})\int_{G}\xi_{2}(g)\rho(g^{-1})\,dg=\rho(\xi_{1})\int_{G}\xi_{2}(g^{-1})\rho(g)\,dg

\displaystyle=\rho(\xi_{1})\rho(\xi_{2}^{\vee})=\rho(\xi_{1}*\xi_{2}^{\vee}).

(95)

As tensor products of test functions on
G
span a dense subspace
of
C^{\infty}_{c}(G\times G)
,
this calculation shows that the closed subspace of
\mathcal{H}

generated by
\pi^{-\infty}(C^{\infty}_{c}(G\times G)){\tt E}
coincides with

\mathcal{H}_{\rho}
. This subspace is also
J
-invariant because

\displaystyle J\rho(\xi)J

\displaystyle=\int_{G}\overline{\xi(g_{1},g_{2})}\pi(\tau_{h}^{G}(g_{1}),\tau_{h}^{G}(g_{2}))\,dg_{1}\,dg_{2}=\int_{G}\overline{\xi(\tau_{h}^{G}(g_{1}),\tau_{h}^{G}(g_{2}))}\pi(g_{1},g_{2})\,dg_{1}\,dg_{2}.

(c) As
\mathcal{H}_{\rho}
is generated by
{\tt E}
, the kernel of
\pi_{\rho}
is
the largest normal subgroup of
G\times G
acting trivially on
{\tt E}
.
A pair
(g_{1},g_{2})\in G\times G
fixes
\mathbf{1}\in B(\mathcal{K})
if and only if

\rho(g_{1}g_{2}^{-1})=\mathbf{1}
, i.e.,
g_{1}g_{2}^{-1}\in\ker(\rho)
.
If
(g_{1},g_{2})\in\ker(\pi_{\rho})
, then we have for all
a,b\in G
the relation

ag_{1}a^{-1}bg_{2}^{-1}b^{-1}\in\ker(\rho),

and since
G
is connected and
\ker(\rho)
discrete, it follows that

g_{1},g_{2}\in Z(G)
. This shows that

\ker(\pi_{\rho})=\{(g_{1},g_{2})\in Z(G)\times Z(G)\colon g_{1}g_{2}^{-1}\in\ker(\rho)\}=\Delta_{Z(G)}\cdot(\ker(\rho)\times\{e\}),

(96)

which is a discrete subgroup of
G\times G
.

(d) As the kernel of
\pi_{\rho}
is discrete by (c), the description
of the semigroup
S_{{\tt V}_{\rho}}
follows from

[
Ne19
, Thms. 2.16, 3.4]
.
Note that the discreteness of
\ker(\pi_{\rho})
implies that

C_{\pi_{\rho}}^{c}=C_{{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}}}^{c}=C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{c}\oplus-C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{c}

(Corollary
210
).
∎

The following theorem shows that, for symmetric spaces of group
type, there exist biinvariant nets of real subspaces assigning
to the wedge domain
W_{G}(h)_{e}=G^{h}_{e}\exp(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{c,0})
the standard subspace
associated to the pair
(h,\tau_{h}^{G})
by the BGL construction
(Subsection
7.2
).

Theorem 87
.

Let
(\rho,\mathcal{K})
be a
C_{\mathfrak{g}}
-positive antiunitary trace class representation
of
G_{\tau_{h}}
with discrete kernel
and consider the representation
(\pi_{\rho},\mathcal{H}_{\rho})

of
G\times G
on the closed subspace

\mathcal{H}_{\rho}:=\overline{\rho(C^{\infty}_{c}(G))}\subseteq B_{2}(\mathcal{K}),\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ given by }\hskip 10.00002pt\pi_{\rho}(g_{1},g_{2})A=\rho(g_{1})A\rho(g_{2})^{-1},

extended to an antiunitary representation of
(G\times G)_{\tau_{h}\times\tau_{h}}

by

J(A):=\pi_{\rho}(\tau_{h}^{G}\times\tau_{h}^{G})(A):=\rho(\tau_{h}^{G})A\rho(\tau_{h}^{G}).

For
{\tt E}:={\mathbb{R}}\eta^{0}
,
\eta^{0}(A):=\mathop{{\rm tr}}\nolimits(A)
, and

q\colon G\times G\to G,q(g_{1},g_{2}):=g_{1}g_{2}^{-1}
,

{\sf H}^{G}_{\tt E}(\mathcal{O}):={\sf H}_{\tt E}^{G\times G}(q^{-1}(\mathcal{O}))=\overline{\rho(C^{\infty}_{c}(q^{-1}(\mathcal{O}),{\mathbb{R}}))}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{\rho}

(97)

defines a
(G\times G)
-covariant net of closed real subspaces in
\mathcal{H}_{\rho}
on

G\cong(G\times G)/\Delta_{G}
. The wedge domain

W_{G}(h)_{e}=G^{h}_{e}\exp(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{c,0})

satisfies

{\sf H}^{G}_{\tt E}(W_{G}(h)_{e})={\tt V}_{\rho}=\overline{\mathop{{\rm span}}\nolimits_{\mathbb{R}}\rho(C^{\infty}_{c}(W_{G}(h)_{e},{\mathbb{R}}))},

(98)

where
{\tt V}_{\rho}
is the standard subspace corresponding to the pair

(e^{2\pi i\cdot\partial\pi_{\rho}(h,h)},J)
of modular data.

Proof.

By Proposition
86
(c), the kernel
of
\pi_{\rho}
is discrete. Therefore the
invariance of
\eta^{0}
under the modular group and
J
and
Theorem
72
(b) imply that

{\tt V}_{\rho}={\sf H}^{G\times G}_{\tt E}(S_{{\tt V}_{\rho}}^{0}):=\overline{\pi_{\rho}^{-\infty}(C^{\infty}_{c}(S_{{\tt V}_{\rho}}^{0},{\mathbb{R}}))\eta^{0}}

holds for the open subsemigroup

S_{{\tt V}_{\rho}}^{0}=(G\times G)_{{\tt V}_{\rho}}\exp(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{c}\oplus-C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{c}),\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ where }\hskip 10.00002ptC_{\mathfrak{g}}^{c}=C_{{\mathfrak{g}},+}-C_{{\mathfrak{g}},-}

(Proposition
86
(d)).
With Corollary
73
, we even obtain

{\tt V}_{\rho}={\sf H}_{\tt E}(S_{{\tt V}_{\rho},e}^{0})\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ with }\hskip 10.00002ptS_{{\tt V}_{\rho},e}=(G^{h}_{e}\times G^{h}_{e})\exp(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{c}\oplus-C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{c})=S(C_{\mathfrak{g}},h)_{e}\times S(C_{\mathfrak{g}},h)_{e}^{-1},

(99)

where

S(C_{\mathfrak{g}},h)^{0}_{e}=S(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{0},h)_{e}=W_{G}(h)_{e}

(Theorem
52
).
With

\pi_{\rho}^{-\infty}(S(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{0},h)\times S(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{0},-h))\eta^{0}=\rho(S(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{0},h)),

we further derive from (
99
)

{\tt V}_{\rho}=\overline{\rho(C^{\infty}_{c}(S(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{0},h),{\mathbb{R}}))}.

(100)

As
\eta^{0}
is
\Delta_{G}
-invariant (Proposition
86
),
[
NÓ21a
, Lemma 2.11(a)]
shows that the net defined in
(
97
) satisfies

{\tt V}_{\rho}={\sf H}_{\tt E}^{G\times G}(S_{{\tt V}_{\rho},e}^{0})={\sf H}_{\tt E}^{G\times G}(S_{{\tt V}_{\rho},e}^{0}\Delta_{G}).

(101)

Now
q^{-1}(W_{G}(h)_{e})=(W_{G}(h)_{e}\times W_{G}(h)_{e}^{-1})\Delta_{G}=S_{{\tt V}_{\rho},e}^{0}\Delta_{G}

leads to

\displaystyle{\sf H}^{G}_{\tt E}(W_{G}(h)_{e})

\displaystyle={\sf H}_{\tt E}^{G\times G}(q^{-1}(W_{G}(h)_{e}))={\sf H}_{\tt E}^{G\times G}\big(S_{{\tt V}_{\rho},e}^{0}\Delta_{G}\big)\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt=}\limits^{\eqref{eq:42}}}}\ {\tt V}_{\rho}.\qed

Remark 88
.

For the stabilizer group of
{\tt V}_{\rho}
, we have

(G\times G)_{{\tt V}_{\rho}}=\{(g_{1},g_{2})\in G^{h}\times G^{h}\colon\pi_{\rho}(g_{1},g_{2})J=J\pi_{\rho}(g_{1},g_{2})\}\supseteq G_{{\tt V}_{\mathcal{K}}}\times G_{{\tt V}_{\mathcal{K}}}.

By (
96
),
the relation
\pi_{\rho}(g_{1},g_{2})J=J\pi_{\rho}(g_{1},g_{2})

is equivalent to

(g_{1}\tau_{h}^{G}(g_{1})^{-1},g_{2}\tau_{h}^{G}(g_{2})^{-1})\in\ker(\pi_{\rho})=\Delta_{Z(G)}(\ker(\rho)\times\{e\})\subseteq Z(G)\times Z(G).

(102)

From Remark
310
we know that

\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(G)^{h}=\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(G^{h})\subseteq\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(G)^{\tau_{h}}

is of index two if
{\mathfrak{g}}
is simple
(otherwise of index
2^{k}
, where
k
is the number of simple
hermitian ideals of
{\mathfrak{g}}
). Equation (
102
) implies in particular that

g_{1},g_{2}\in\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits^{-1}(\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(G)^{\tau_{h}})=\{g\in G\colon g\tau_{h}^{G}(g)^{-1}\in Z(G)\}.

Lemma 89
.

\mathcal{H}_{\rho}=B_{2}(\mathcal{K})
if and only if
(\rho,\mathcal{K})
is an
irreducible representation of
G
.

Proof.

If
\mathcal{H}_{\rho}=B_{2}(\mathcal{K})
, then
\rho(C^{\infty}_{c}(G))
is dense
in
B_{2}(\mathcal{K})
, so that the von Neumann algebra generated by this subspace
is
B(\mathcal{K})
, which by Schur’s Lemma implies that
(\rho,\mathcal{K})
is irreducible.

If, conversely,
(\rho,\mathcal{K})
is irreducible, then

\rho(C^{\infty}_{c}(G))\subseteq B_{1}(\mathcal{K})\subseteq B_{2}(\mathcal{K})

is weakly dense in
B(\mathcal{K})
,
i.e., its annihilator in
B_{1}(\mathcal{K})
is trivial.
Therefore the orthogonal space
\rho(C^{\infty}_{c}(G))^{\bot}\subseteq B_{2}(\mathcal{K})

has trivial intersection with
B_{1}(\mathcal{K})
. As it is invariant
under multiplication with
\rho(C^{\infty}_{c}(G))
, which consists of trace class
operators, this can only happen if
\rho(C^{\infty}_{c}(G))^{\bot}=\{0\}
,
i.e., if
\mathcal{H}=B_{2}(\mathcal{K})
.
∎

Remark 810
.

(a) If
\rho
is reducible
and extends to an irreducible antiunitary representation
of
G_{\tau_{h}}
, then
G
preserves a decomposition

\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{K}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{K}_{2}
and
\rho(C_{c}^{\infty}(G))

generates
B(\mathcal{K}_{1})\oplus B(\mathcal{K}_{2})
. Further

J=\rho(\tau_{h}^{G})
is a conjugation with

J\mathcal{K}_{1}=\mathcal{K}_{2}
. Accordingly,
\mathcal{H}_{\rho}\subseteq B_{2}(\mathcal{K})

is isomorphic to
B_{2}(\mathcal{K}_{1})\oplus B_{2}(\mathcal{K}_{2})
(diagonal matrices).

(b) The trace class representation
(\rho,\mathcal{K})

of
G
on
\mathcal{K}
decomposes with finite multiplicities as a direct sum

(\rho,\mathcal{K})\cong\sum_{j\in J}(\rho_{j},\mathcal{K}_{j})^{\oplus m_{j}}.

Identifying
B(\mathcal{K}_{j})
with diagonal operators in

M_{m_{j}}(B(\mathcal{K}_{j}))\cong B(\mathcal{K}_{j}^{\oplus m_{j}})
, we then have

\rho(C^{\infty}_{c}(G))\subseteq\widehat{\bigoplus}_{j\in J}B_{2}(\mathcal{K}_{j})\cong\mathcal{H}_{\rho}.

8.3
The general case: Nets on compactly causal symmetric spaces

Let
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C)
be a semisimple compactly causal symmetric Lie algebra
for which
{\mathfrak{h}}={\mathfrak{g}}^{\tau}
contains no non-zero ideal.
We recall from (
14
) the
canonical embedding

\iota\colon{\mathfrak{g}}\to{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}},\hskip 10.00002pt\iota(x):=(x,\tau(x))\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ for }\hskip 10.00002ptx\in{\mathfrak{g}}

(103)

into a symmetric Lie algebra of group type.
The Extension Theorem
24

implies that the elliptic cone
C\subseteq{\mathfrak{q}}
extends to a
-\tau
-invariant
cone
C_{\mathfrak{g}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{g}}
,
so that

\widetilde{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}:=\{(y,-y)\colon y\in C_{\mathfrak{g}}\}

leads to the causal symmetric Lie algebra

({\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{g}},\tau_{\rm flip},\widetilde{C}_{\mathfrak{g}})
of group type.
On the level of global symmetric spaces

M=G/H
,
H\subseteq G^{\tau^{G}}
open, the infinitesimal embedding

\iota
corresponds to the quadratic representation

Q\colon M\to M_{G}=G^{\sharp}_{e}\subseteq G,\hskip 10.00002ptgH\mapsto gg^{\sharp},

which is a covering morphism of symmetric spaces.

The following theorem is an important tool to
analyze smooth vectors, resp., distribution vectors for subgroups.

Theorem 811
.

(Zellner’s Smooth Vector Theorem;
[
NSZ17
, Thm. 3.1]
)

If
(\pi,\mathcal{H})
is a unitary representation of a Lie group
and
x_{0}\in C_{\pi}^{0}
in the interior of its positive cone, then the inclusion

\mathcal{H}^{\infty}\to\mathcal{H}^{\infty}(\partial\pi(x_{0}))
is an isomorphism
of Fréchet spaces. In particular every smooth vector for the single operator

\partial\pi(x_{0})
is smooth for
G
.

We now consider a
C_{\mathfrak{g}}
-positive antiunitary trace class representation

(\rho,\mathcal{K})
of
G_{\tau_{h}}
with discrete kernel
and use Theorem
87
to obtain a
representation
(\pi_{\rho},\mathcal{H}_{\rho})
of
(G\times G)_{\tau_{h}\times\tau_{h}}

on
\mathcal{H}_{\rho}\subseteq B_{2}(\mathcal{K})
by

\pi_{\rho}(g_{1},g_{2})A=\rho(g_{1})A\rho(g_{2})^{-1}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ and }\hskip 10.00002pt\pi_{\rho}(\tau_{h}\times\tau_{h})A=J_{\mathcal{K}}AJ_{\mathcal{K}}.

We introduce the morphism of graded Lie groups

\iota_{G}\colon G_{\tau_{h}}\to(G\times G)_{\tau_{h}\times\tau_{h}},\hskip 20.00003ptg\mapsto(g,\tau^{G}(g)),\hskip 20.00003pt\tau_{h}^{G}\mapsto\tau_{h}^{G}\times\tau_{h}^{G},

corresponding to the inclusion
\iota:{\mathfrak{g}}\to{\mathfrak{g}}\times{\mathfrak{g}}
from (
103
).
We thus obtain the antiunitary representation of
G_{\tau_{h}}
, defined by

U:=\pi_{\rho}\circ\iota_{G},\hskip 20.00003ptU(g)A=\rho(g)A\rho(g)^{\sharp}

(104)

Theorem 812
.

The representations
U
and
\pi_{\rho}
on
\mathcal{H}_{\rho}
have the same
smooth vectors.
Let

{\tt E}={\mathbb{R}}\eta^{0}\subseteq\mathcal{H}^{-\infty}(\pi_{\rho})=\mathcal{H}^{-\infty}(U)\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ with }\hskip 10.00002pt\eta_{0}=\mathop{{\rm tr}}\nolimits

be
as in
Proposition
86
.
If
q_{M}\colon G\to M,g\mapsto gH
denotes the canonical projection, then

{\sf H}_{\tt E}^{M}(\mathcal{O}):={\sf H}_{\tt E}^{G,U}(q_{M}^{-1}(\mathcal{O})):=\overline{U^{-\infty}(C^{\infty}_{c}(q_{M}^{-1}(\mathcal{O}),{\mathbb{R}})){\tt E}}

(105)

defines a
G
-covariant net of closed real subspaces of
\mathcal{H}_{\rho}
on
the symmetric space
M=G/H
which satisfies

{\tt V}_{(h,\tau_{h}^{G},U)}={\sf H}^{M}_{\tt E}(W_{M}(h)_{eH}).

(106)

Proof.

For any

x_{0}\in C^{0}\subseteq{\mathfrak{q}}
we have

\iota(x_{0})=(x_{0},-x_{0})\in\widetilde{C}_{\mathfrak{g}}^{0}\subseteq C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{0}\oplus-C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{0}

(see (
90
)).
Therefore Zellner’s Theorem
811
implies that
smooth vectors of the single operator

\partial U(x_{0})=\partial\pi_{\rho}(x_{0},-x_{0})
are
smooth for
G\times G
. This shows that

\mathcal{H}^{\infty}(\pi_{\rho})=\mathcal{H}^{\infty}(U)\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ and therefore }\hskip 10.00002pt\mathcal{H}^{-\infty}(\pi_{\rho})=\mathcal{H}^{-\infty}(U).

(107)

We conclude in particular that

{\tt E}\subseteq\mathcal{H}^{-\infty}(U)
, so that
(
105
) defines a
G
-covariant net
of closed real subspaces of
\mathcal{H}_{\rho}
on the symmetric space
M=G/H
.

The positive cone

C_{U}
of
U
contains
\iota^{-1}(C_{\pi_{\rho}})\supseteq\iota^{-1}(C_{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus-C_{\mathfrak{g}})
,
hence is a closed generating
invariant cone whose intersection with
{\mathfrak{q}}
contains
C
.
As
\iota(h)=(h,h)
, the standard subspace
{\tt V}_{\rho}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{\rho}

from Theorem
87

coincides with the standard subspace
{\tt V}_{U}:={\tt V}_{(h,\tau_{h}^{G},U)}

associated to the pair
(h,\tau_{h}^{G})
and the
representation
U
of
G
by the BGL construction.

The discreteness of the kernel of
\pi_{\rho}
(Proposition
86
)
implies that

U
has discrete kernel. Therefore
[
Ne19
, Thms. 2.16, 3.4]
implies that

S_{{\tt V}_{\rho}}=(G^{h})_{{\tt V}_{\rho}}\exp(C_{U}^{c}).

(108)

We claim that

C_{U}^{c}=C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{c}.

(109)

In fact, we have

C_{U}\cap{\mathfrak{q}}=\{x\in{\mathfrak{q}}\colon(x,-x)\in C_{\pi_{\rho}}\}\supseteq C_{\mathfrak{g}}\cap{\mathfrak{q}},

so that
C_{\mathfrak{g}}
and
C_{U}
are invariant cones in
{\mathfrak{g}}
with
intersecting interiors, and this implies that

C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{c}=C_{U}^{c}
by Corollary
210
.
With (
108
) and (
109
) we now obtain

q_{M}(S_{{\tt V}_{\rho,e}}^{0})=G^{h}_{e}.\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{c,0}\cap{\mathfrak{q}})=W_{M}(h)_{eH},

and thus by (
98
) in Theorem
87

\displaystyle{\tt V}_{\rho}

\displaystyle={\sf H}^{G,U}_{\tt E}(S_{{\tt V}_{\rho},e}^{0})={\sf H}^{G,U}_{\tt E}(S_{{\tt V}_{\rho},e}^{0}H)={\sf H}^{G,U}_{\tt E}(q_{M}^{-1}(q_{M}(S_{{\tt V}_{\rho},e}^{0})))

\displaystyle={\sf H}^{G,U}_{\tt E}(q_{M}^{-1}(W_{M}(h)_{eH}))={\sf H}^{M}_{\tt E}(W_{M}(h)_{eH}).\qed

Remark 813
.

(The kernel of
U
)
(a) For a representation
(\rho,\mathcal{K})
of
G
,
the representation
\pi_{\rho}
on
\mathcal{H}_{\rho}\subseteq B_{2}(\mathcal{K})

has the property that
\Delta_{Z(G)}
acts trivially.
In fact, the subspace
\mathcal{H}_{\rho}
is generated by

\rho(C^{\infty}_{c}(G,{\mathbb{R}}))\subseteq B_{2}(\mathcal{K})
which commutes with

\rho(Z(G))
. We therefore have
for
A\in\mathcal{H}_{\rho}
and
z\in Z(G)

\pi_{\rho}(z,z)A=\rho(z)A\rho(z)^{-1}=A.

For the representation
U(g):=\pi_{\rho}(g,\tau^{G}(g))
of
G
,
we obtain for
z\in Z(G)
that

U(z)A=\rho(z)A\rho(z^{\sharp})=\rho(zz^{\sharp})A,

so that
zz^{\sharp}\in\ker(\rho)
is equivalent to
z\in\ker(U)
.
We conclude that

\ker U=\{z\in Z(G)\colon zz^{\sharp}\in\ker\rho\}.

(110)

(b) If
\ker(\rho)=Z(G)
(Proposition
81
),
the representation
(U,\mathcal{H}_{\rho})
factors through
G/Z(G)
.
We then have

G_{\tt V}=\{g\in G^{h}\colon gg^{\sharp}\in\ker(U)\}=\{g\in G^{h}\colon gg^{\sharp}\in Z(G)\}=\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits^{-1}(\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(G)^{\tau}).

(c) If
\rho
is faithful (Proposition
81
),
then

\ker U=\{z\in Z(G)\colon zz^{\sharp}=e\}=Z(G)^{\tau^{G}}

and thus

\displaystyle G_{\tt V}

\displaystyle=\{g\in G^{h}\colon gg^{\sharp}\in\ker(U)\}=\{g\in G^{h}\colon gg^{\sharp}\in Z(G)^{\tau^{G}}\}

\displaystyle=\{g\in G^{h}\colon gg^{\sharp}\in Z(G),(gg^{\sharp})^{2}=e\}.

(111)

9
The wedge space as an ordered symmetric
space

In this section we return to a geometric topic.
We show that, under rather natural assumptions,
the wedge space
\mathcal{W}(M,h)
, consisting of all
G
-translates
of the connected component
W_{M}(h)_{eH}
of the wedge domain in
M
,
carries the structure of an ordered symmetric space.
We start in Subsection
9.1
with the order
structure and the determination of the stabilizer group of

W(M,h)_{eH}
. This turns out to be rather hard to do directly,
so we use the representation theoretic results
from Subsection
8.3
on the correspondence between wedge domains
and standard subspaces to obtain all required information.
The symmetric space structure is discussed in Subsection
9.2
.
This result establishes a bridge to the abstract wedge spaces
introduced in
[
MN21
]
.

9.1
The order structure

Let
(G,\tau^{G})
be a connected symmetric Lie group corresponding
to the modular compactly causal symmetric Lie algebra

({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C,h)
, let
H\subseteq G^{\tau^{G}}
be an open subgroup,

M=G/H
the corresponding compactly causal symmetric space
and

W:=W_{M}(h)_{eH}\subseteq M

the connected component of the
wedge domain specified by
h
corresponding to the base point
eH
.
As in Theorem
65
, we assume that

C=C_{\mathfrak{g}}\cap{\mathfrak{q}}
for a pointed invariant cone
C_{\mathfrak{g}}\subseteq{\mathfrak{g}}
with

-\tau(C_{\mathfrak{g}})=C_{\mathfrak{g}}
.

We further assume the context of
Theorem
812
, where

(U,\mathcal{H})
is an antiunitary representation of
G_{\tau_{h}}
with discrete kernel
and there exists a distribution vector
\eta^{0}
with

{\sf H}^{M}_{{\mathbb{R}}\eta^{0}}(W)={\tt V}:=\mathop{{\rm Fix}}\nolimits(U(\tau^{G}_{h})e^{\pi i\cdot\partial U(h)}).

The
wedge space

\mathcal{W}(M,h):=\{gW\colon g\in G\}

is the
G
-orbit of
W=W_{M}(h)_{eH}
in the set of subsets of
M
.
The order on the wedge space is determined by the subsemigroup

S_{W}:=\{g\in G\colon gW\subseteq W\}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ with }\hskip 10.00002ptS_{W}\cap S_{W}^{-1}=G_{W}.

(112)

Below we abbreviate
G_{e}^{h}:=(G^{h})_{e}
.

Theorem 91
.

The compression semigroup of
W
is

S_{W}=G_{W}\exp(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{c})\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ with }\hskip 10.00002ptG_{W}=G^{h}_{e}H^{h}.

Furthermore,
G_{W}
is open in
G^{h}
.

Proof.

By definition,

W=W_{M}(h)_{eH}=G^{h}_{e}.\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits(C_{+}^{0}-C_{-}^{0})=S(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{0},h)_{e}.eH

is the orbit of the base point under the interior of the semigroup

S(C_{\mathfrak{g}},h)_{e}=G^{h}_{e}\exp(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{c})

(cf. Proposition
61
(b)).
This shows that

S(C_{\mathfrak{g}},h)_{e}\subseteq S_{W},

(113)

and in particular
G^{h}_{e}\subseteq G_{W}
.
For
s\in S_{W}
, the inclusion
sW\subseteq W
implies

U(s){\tt V}=U(s){\sf H}_{\tt E}^{M}(W)={\sf H}_{\tt E}^{M}(sW)\subseteq{\sf H}_{\tt E}^{M}(W)={\tt V},

so that
S_{W}\subseteq S_{\tt V}.

We thus obtain with (
113
)

G^{h}_{e}\exp(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{c})\subseteq S_{W}\subseteq S_{\tt V}\ {\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\subseteq}\limits^{\eqref{eq:sc1}}}}\ G^{h}\exp(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{c}).

(114)

It follows in particular that

G^{h}_{e}\subseteq G_{W}\subseteq G^{h}
. This implies in particular that
G_{W}
is
open in
G^{h}
. As

G^{h}_{e}.eH=M^{\alpha}_{eH}
by Proposition
34

and this submanifold equals
M^{\alpha}\cap\overline{W}
,
it follows that

G_{W}=\{g\in G^{h}\colon gM^{\alpha}_{eH}=M^{\alpha}_{eH}\}=G^{h}_{e}(G^{h}\cap H)=G^{h}_{e}H^{h}.

By (
114
),
\exp(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{c})\subseteq S_{W}
, so that

G_{W}\exp(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{c})\subseteq S_{W}
. For the converse, let

s=g\exp x\in S_{W}\subseteq G^{h}\exp(C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{c})
.
Then

s.eH=g\exp(x).eH\in gW_{M}(h)_{eH}\subseteq W_{M}(h)

implies
g\in G_{W}
.
∎

The preceding proof makes heavy use of representation
theoretic results to determine
S_{W}
. It would be nice to have a more
direct geometric proof, but we presently do not see how to do that.

9.2
The symmetric space structure

We now turn to the structure of a symmetric space on
the wedge space
\mathcal{W}(M,h)
.

Proposition 92
.

Let
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau)
be a semisimple
symmetric Lie algebra,
G
a connected Lie group with
Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
,
\tau^{G}
an involutive automorphisms of
G

integrating
\tau
and
H\subseteq G
a closed subgroup
satisfying

\mathop{\bf L{}}\nolimits(H)={\mathfrak{h}}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ and }\hskip 10.00002pt\tau^{G}(H)=H.

Then
M=G/H
carries the structure of a symmetric space.

Proof.

In view of Remark
213
(b),
we may factor the subgroup
H\cap Z(G)
which acts trivially on
M
and
thus assume that

\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits
is injective on
H
. Then Remark
213
(d)
implies that
H\subseteq G^{\tau^{G}}
, and since

\mathop{\bf L{}}\nolimits(H)={\mathfrak{h}}={\mathfrak{g}}^{\tau}=\mathop{\bf L{}}\nolimits(G^{\tau^{G}})
, the subgroup
H
is open in
G^{\tau^{G}}
.
Therefore
M=G/H
is a symmetric space.
∎

Proposition 93
.

(The wedge space as a symmetric space)

Let
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C,h)
be a non-compactly causal semisimple
symmetric Lie algebra,
G
a connected Lie group with Lie algebra

{\mathfrak{g}}
and assume that

•

\tau
integrates to an involutive automorphism

\tau_{h}^{G}
of
G
,

•

H\subseteq G^{\tau^{G}}
is an open subgroup,

•

\tau_{h}=e^{\pi i\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits h}
integrates to an involutive automorphism

\tau_{h}^{G}
of
G
,

•

\tau_{h}^{G}(H)=H
, i.e.,

\tau_{h}
induces an involution
\tau_{h}^{M}
on

M:=G/H
.

Then the wedge space

\mathcal{W}(G,h)=G.W\cong G/G_{W}

is a symmetric space.

Proof.

Recall that
G_{W}=G^{h}_{e}H^{h}\subseteq G^{h}

(Theorem
91
).
First we observe that

\tau^{G}_{h}(G^{h})=G^{h}
follows from
\tau(h)=h
.
With
\tau_{h}^{G}(H)=H
, we therefore get

\tau^{G}_{h}(G_{W})=\tau^{G}_{h}(G^{h}_{e}H^{h})=G^{h}_{e}H^{h}=G_{W}.

Hence the assertion follows from Proposition
92
.
∎

Remark 94
.

In the context of Subsection
9.1
, we
have four subgroups with the Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}_{0}(h)=\ker(\mathop{{\rm ad}}\nolimits h)
:

G^{h}_{e}\subseteq G_{W}\subseteq G_{\tt V}\subseteq G^{h}.

Accordingly, we have a sequence of coverings of homogeneous spaces

G/G^{h}_{e}\to\mathcal{W}(M,h)\cong G/G_{W}\to U(G){\tt V}\cong G/G_{\tt V}\to\mathcal{O}_{h}=\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(G)h\cong G/G^{h}.

Here
\mathcal{O}_{h}\cong\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(G).h
always is a symmetric space because

\tau_{h}
defines an involution on
\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(G)
and

\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(G)^{h}\subseteq\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(G)^{\tau_{h}}
is an open subgroup.

Likewise,
G/G_{\tt V}\cong\mathcal{O}_{\tt V}:=U(G){\tt V}

carries a natural symmetric space structure.
The point reflection in the base point
{\tt V}\in\mathcal{O}_{\tt V}

is given by
{\tt W}\mapsto J_{\tt V}{\tt W}^{\prime}
.
We refer to
[
Ne19
]
for more
on the geometry of the space
\mathop{{\rm Stand}}\nolimits(\mathcal{H})
of standard subspaces
of
\mathcal{H}
.

Remark 95
.

(a) The three conditions in Proposition
93

are in particular satisfied for
spaces of group type
M=G\cong(G\times G)/\Delta_{G}
, where

\tau^{G\times G}(g_{1},g_{2})=(g_{2},g_{1})

and
\tau^{G}_{h}
exists on
G
, so that
\tau^{G\times G}_{h}=\tau^{G}_{h}\times\tau^{G}_{h}
.

(b) For Cayley type spaces we have

\tau=\tau_{h}
, so that

G_{W}=G^{h}_{e}H^{h}\subseteq G^{\tau^{G}_{h}}
follows
from
H\subseteq G^{\tau^{G}_{h}}
.

(c) If
Z(G)=\{e\}
and
G\cong\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(G)
, then

M_{G}\cong\{\varphi\in\mathop{{\rm Aut}}\nolimits(G)_{e}\colon\tau\varphi\tau=\varphi^{-1}\},\hskip 10.00002pt\tau_{h}^{G}(\varphi)=\tau_{h}\varphi\tau_{h},\hskip 20.00003pt\tau^{G}(\varphi)=\tau\varphi\tau.

Further,
G_{W}\subseteq G^{h}\subseteq G^{\tau^{G}_{h}}

(Remark
310
), so that
\mathcal{W}(M_{G},h)
is a symmetric space.

(d) For
M=M_{G}
we have
H=G^{\tau^{G}}
, which is
\tau_{h}^{G}
-invariant.
If
G
is simply connected, then
H=G^{\tau^{G}}
is connected, so that we have
in particular
M=G/H\cong M_{G}
.

(e) If the representation
U
in Theorem
812
is faithful, then

G_{W}\subseteq G_{\tt V}G^{h,\tau_{h}^{G}}\subseteq G^{\tau_{h}^{G}},

which obviously implies the invariance of
G_{W}
under
\tau^{G}_{h}
.

10
Open problems

In this section we briefly discuss some interesting open problems that we plan to
address in the future.

Problem 101
.

For a compactly causal symmetric space
M=G/H
with the
infinitesimal data
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C,h)
,
characterize the antiunitary representations

(U,\mathcal{H})
of
G
for which there exists a real subspace
{\tt E}\subseteq\mathcal{H}^{-\infty}

satisfying

{\sf H}_{\tt E}^{G/H}(W_{M}(h)_{eH})={\tt V}_{(h,\tau^{G},U)}.

We conjecture that
C_{\mathfrak{g}}
-positivity should be enough, but this is not easy
to see.

Problem 102
.

The symmetric spaces
\mathcal{W}(M,h)
of wedge domains
in
M
are particular examples of non-compactly causal
parahermitian symmetric spaces. The corresponding causal
symmetric Lie algebra is

({\mathfrak{g}},\tau_{h},C_{\mathfrak{g}}^{c})
(Theorem
91
).
Up to coverings, we thus obtain interesting realizations of
non-compactly causal symmetric spaces of Cayley type.

However, there are much more parahermitian symmetric spaces
(cf.
[
Kan00
]
,
[
MN21
]
),
and we expect that they can likewise be realized as wedge spaces

\mathcal{W}(M,h)
of a non-compactly causal symmetric spaces
M=G/H

(see
[
NÓ21b
]
for details and results in this direction).
If
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau,C)
is non-compactly causal and
{\mathfrak{g}}
is simple,

h\in\mathcal{E}({\mathfrak{g}})
, then
({\mathfrak{g}},\tau_{h})
can be any simple parahermitian
symmetric Lie algebra by
[
Ó91
, Lem. 2.6]
.
In this case
G/G^{h}_{e}
carries no natural order structure, which is also
reflected in the geometry of
\mathcal{W}(M,h)
, respectively
the fact that the inclusion order on this set is trivial.

For example, if
M=G_{\mathbb{C}}/G
is irreducible
non-compactly causal of complex type,
corresponding to
({\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbb{C}},\tau,iC_{\mathfrak{g}},h)
, where
{\mathfrak{g}}
is simple hermitian
(not necessarily of tube type), and
h
a causal Euler element,
then
G_{\mathbb{C}}^{h}\cong K_{\mathbb{C}}
is a connected subgroup, and

\mathcal{W}(G_{\mathbb{C}}/G,h)\cong G_{\mathbb{C}}/K_{\mathbb{C}}

is the complexification of the Riemannian symmetric space
G/K
.

11
Anti-de Sitter space
\mathop{{\rm AdS}}\nolimits^{d}

In this section we discuss the
d
-dimensional anti-de Sitter space

\mathop{{\rm AdS}}\nolimits^{d}
as an example of a compactly causal symmetric space.
As we verify all assertions for this example directly, it can be read
independently of the other sections. It illustrates our results for this
concrete example and it is a guiding example for the general
theory. From the perspective of physics it is of particular importance
because anti-de Sitter spaces are precisely the
irreducible

compactly causal symmetric spaces which are Lorentzian. There are more
reducible examples, even compact ones, such as the conformal compactification
of Minkowski space, considered as a symmetric space of the compact group

\mathop{{\rm SO}}\nolimits_{2}({\mathbb{R}})\times\mathop{{\rm SO}}\nolimits_{d}({\mathbb{R}})
.

We endow
V:={\mathbb{R}}^{d+1}
with the symmetric bilinear form

\beta(x,y):=x_{0}y_{0}+x_{1}y_{1}-x_{2}y_{2}-\cdots-x_{d}y_{d}.

We consider the connected group
G:=\mathop{{\rm SO}}\nolimits_{2,d-1}({\mathbb{R}})_{e}
and consider
the
anti-de Sitter space

M:=\mathop{{\rm AdS}}\nolimits^{d}:=\{x\in V\colon\beta(x,x)=1\}=G.e_{0}\cong G/G^{e_{0}}

and note that

H:=G^{e_{0}}\cong\mathop{{\rm SO}}\nolimits_{1,d-1}^{\uparrow}({\mathbb{R}})

(115)

is connected.
By
[
NÓ21b
, Prop. B.3]
, the
exponential function of the symmetric space
\mathop{{\rm AdS}}\nolimits^{d}
is given by

\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{p}(v)=C(\beta(v,v))p+S(\beta(v,v))v,

(116)

where the
functions
C,S\colon{\mathbb{C}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
are defined by

C(z):=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{(2k)!}z^{k}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ and }\hskip 10.00002ptS(z):=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{k}}{(2k+1)!}z^{k}.

(117)

Then
C(z^{2})=\cos(z)
and
S(z^{2})=\frac{\sin z}{z}
.
It follows in particular that
\beta
-positive vectors
v\in T_{p}(M)

(timelike vectors)
generate closed geodesics.

The domain

V_{>0}:=\{v\in V\colon\beta(v,v)>0\}

intersects
T_{e_{0}}(\mathop{{\rm AdS}}\nolimits^{d})\cong e_{0}^{\bot}
in a double light cone,
and we write

V_{+}(e_{0}):=\{x\in T_{e_{0}}(\mathop{{\rm AdS}}\nolimits^{d})=e_{0}^{\bot}\colon\beta(x,x)>0,\beta(x,e_{1})>0\}

for the “positive” cone containing
e_{1}
. As this cone is

G^{e_{0}}
-invariant by (
115
), we obtain a
causal structure
on
M
by

V_{+}(g.e_{0}):=g.V_{+}(e_{0})\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ for }\hskip 10.00002ptg\in G.

The tangent bundle of
M
is

T(M)=\{(x,y)\in V\times V\colon\beta(x,x)=1,\beta(x,y)=0\}.

As
G
preserves the causal orientation on
M
, we call
a pair
(x,y)\in T(M)

•

positive
if
G.(x,y)\cap V_{+}(e_{0})\not=\emptyset
,
and

•

negative
if
G.(x,y)\cap-V_{+}(e_{0})\not=\emptyset
.

Then

T(M)_{\pm}=\bigcup_{m\in M}\pm V_{+}(m)

is the open subset of positive/negative vectors in the tangent bundle.
The reflection
r_{01}(x)=(-x_{0},-x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{d})
is
contained in
G
, so that

V_{+}(-e_{0})=r_{01}(V_{+}(e_{0}))=-V_{+}(e_{0}).

(118)

We consider the boost vector field
X_{h}(v)=hv
, where

h\in\mathop{{\mathfrak{so}}}\nolimits_{2,d-1}({\mathbb{R}})
is defined by

hx=(0,x_{2},x_{1},0,\ldots,0).

It generates the flow

\alpha_{t}(x)=e^{th}x=(x_{0},\cosh t\cdot x_{1}+\sinh t\cdot x_{2},\cosh t\cdot x_{2}+\sinh t\cdot x_{1},x_{3},\ldots,x_{d}).

It also leads to the involutions

\tau_{h}(x)=e^{\pi ih}x=(x_{0},-x_{1},-x_{2},x_{3},\ldots,x_{d})\mbox{ on }{\mathbb{R}}^{d+1}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ and }\hskip 10.00002pt\tau^{G}_{h}(g)=\tau_{h}g\tau_{h}\mbox{ on }G,

and the Wick rotation

\kappa_{h}(x)=e^{-\frac{\pi i}{2}h}x=(x_{0},-ix_{2},-ix_{1},x_{3},\ldots,x_{d})

with
\tau_{h}=\kappa_{h}^{2}
.
Note that
\tau_{h}\not\in G
by (
115
). This element
reverses the causal structure on
\mathop{{\rm AdS}}\nolimits^{d}
.

11.1
Subgroup data

We now describe the groups
G^{\tau_{h}^{g}}
and
G^{h}
in more detail.
The subgroup
G^{h}
consists of all elements
g
in
G
such that
gh=hg
.
Hence
G^{h}
can also be described as the group of all elements
g\in G
leaving all

h
-eigenspaces invariant:

G^{h}=\{g\in G\colon g(e_{1}\pm e_{2})\in{\mathbb{R}}(e_{1}\pm e_{2}),g\{e_{1},e_{2}\}^{\bot}=\{e_{1},e_{2}\}^{\bot}\}.

Writing
V_{h}:=hV={\mathbb{R}}e_{1}\oplus{\mathbb{R}}e_{2}\cong{\mathbb{R}}^{1,1}
, we have

\displaystyle G^{\tau_{h}}

\displaystyle=\{g\in G\colon gV_{h}=V_{h}\}=G\cap(\mathop{\rm O{}}\nolimits_{1,1}({\mathbb{R}})\times\mathop{\rm O{}}\nolimits_{1,d-2}({\mathbb{R}}))

\displaystyle\supseteq G^{h}\supseteq G^{h}_{e}=\mathop{{\rm SO}}\nolimits_{1,1}^{\uparrow}({\mathbb{R}})\times\mathop{{\rm SO}}\nolimits_{1,d-2}^{\uparrow}({\mathbb{R}}).

A maximal compact subgroup
K
of
G
is

K=G\cap\mathop{\rm O{}}\nolimits_{d+1}({\mathbb{R}})\cong\mathop{{\rm SO}}\nolimits_{2}({\mathbb{R}})\times\mathop{{\rm SO}}\nolimits_{d-1}({\mathbb{R}}).

As

K^{\tau_{h}}=\{\pm\mathbf{1}\}\times{\rm S}(\mathop{\rm O{}}\nolimits_{1}({\mathbb{R}})\times\mathop{\rm O{}}\nolimits_{d-2}({\mathbb{R}}))\cong\{\pm\mathbf{1}\}\times\mathop{\rm O{}}\nolimits_{d-2}({\mathbb{R}})

has two connected component, polar decomposition implies

\pi_{0}(G^{\tau_{h}})\cong\begin{cases}{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\times{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}&\text{ for }d>2\\
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}&\text{ for }d=2.\end{cases}

(119)

The group
G^{\tau_{h}}
acts on the Minkowski plane
V_{h}\cong{\mathbb{R}}^{1,1}
by a
homomorphism

\gamma\colon G^{\tau_{h}}\to\mathop{\rm O{}}\nolimits_{1,1}({\mathbb{R}})

which is surjective for
d>2
, but not for
d=2
. We have

G^{\tau_{h}}=G^{h}\cup r_{01}G^{h},\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ where }\hskip 10.00002ptr_{01}=\mathop{{\rm diag}}\nolimits(-\mathbf{1}_{2},\mathbf{1}_{d-1}).

We also note that

\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(G^{\tau_{h}})h=\{\pm h\}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ and }\hskip 10.00002ptG^{\tau_{h}}\setminus G^{h}=\{g\in G\colon\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)h=-h\}.

It follows in particular that
G^{h}
is connected if

d=2
and has two connected components if
d>2
:

G^{h}=G^{h}_{e}\cup rG^{h}_{e},\hskip 10.00002pt\text{where }r=\mathop{{\rm diag}}\nolimits(-\mathbf{1}_{4},\mathbf{1}_{d-3})

(120)

The subgroup
H=G^{e_{0}}\cong\mathop{{\rm SO}}\nolimits_{1,d-1}^{\uparrow}({\mathbb{R}})
satisfies

H^{\tau_{h}}={\rm S}(\mathop{\rm O{}}\nolimits^{\uparrow}_{1,1}({\mathbb{R}})\times\mathop{\rm O{}}\nolimits_{d-2}({\mathbb{R}})),

which has two connected components for
d>2
and one for
d=2
.

The submanifold of
\alpha
-fixed points in
M
is

M^{\alpha}=\{(x_{0},0,0,x_{3},\ldots,x_{d})\colon x_{0}^{2}-x_{3}^{2}-\cdots-x_{d}^{2}=1\}\cong{\mathbb{H}}^{d-2}\cup-{\mathbb{H}}^{d-2}.

Proposition
34
implies that
G^{h}_{e}.(\pm{\mathbb{H}}^{d-2})=\pm{\mathbb{H}}^{d-2}
.
The element
r\in G^{h}
from (
120
) satisfies
r(e_{0})=-e_{0}
,
so that
G^{h}
acts transitively on
M^{\alpha}
.

11.2
The tube domain

The tube domain

\mathcal{T}_{V}:=V+iV_{>0}\subseteq V_{\mathbb{C}}

is connected, but its intersection with

M_{\mathbb{C}}:=\{z=x+iy\colon\beta(x,x)-\beta(y,y)=1,\beta(x,y)=0\}

decomposes into two connected components. In fact,

z\in M_{\mathbb{C}}\cap\mathcal{T}_{V}
implies

\beta(x,x)=1+\beta(y,y)>1,

so that

\Gamma(z):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\beta(x,x)}}(x,y)\in T(M),

and the map
\Gamma:M_{\mathbb{C}}\cap\mathcal{T}_{V}\to T(M)

is
G
-equivariant. The tangent vectors

\Gamma(z)=(\widetilde{x},\widetilde{y})
are
\beta
-positive
with
\beta(\widetilde{y},\widetilde{y})<1
. If, conversely,

(\widetilde{x},\widetilde{y})\in T(M)
satisfies

0<\beta(\widetilde{y},\widetilde{y})<1
, then

c:=\beta(\widetilde{x},\widetilde{x})-\beta(\widetilde{y},\widetilde{y})=1-\beta(\widetilde{y},\widetilde{y})>0,

so that
z:=c^{-1/2}(\widetilde{x}+i\widetilde{y})\in M_{\mathbb{C}}\cap\mathcal{T}_{V}
.
This shows that
\Gamma
is a diffeomorphism

\Gamma\colon M_{\mathbb{C}}\cap\mathcal{T}_{V}\to\{(x,y)\in T(M)\colon 0<\beta(y,y)<1\}.

In particular,
M_{\mathbb{C}}\cap\mathcal{T}_{V}
has two
G
-invariant
connected components

\mathcal{T}_{M}^{\pm}:=\Gamma^{-1}(T(M)_{\pm}),

(121)

which are called the
tube domains of
M
.

The following lemma describes how the positive tube domain

\mathcal{T}_{M}^{+}
can be parametrized by the exponential function of
M_{\mathbb{C}}
.

Lemma 111
.

We have

\mathcal{T}_{M}^{+}=G.\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{e_{0}}(iV_{+}(e_{0}))=\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits(iT(M)_{+}),

(122)

and the fixed point set of the antilinear extension
\overline{\tau}_{h}
of
\tau_{h}

in the complex manifold
\mathcal{T}_{M}^{+}
is

(\mathcal{T}_{M}^{+})^{\overline{\tau}_{h}}=G^{\tau_{h}}.\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{e_{0}}(iV_{+}(e_{0})^{-\tau_{h}})=G^{\tau_{h}}.\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{e_{0}}(i{\mathbb{R}}_{+}e_{1}).

Proof.

The second equality in (
122
)
follows from the
G
-equivariance of
the exponential function of
M_{\mathbb{C}}
.
For the first equality in (
122
),
we start with the observation that
both sides are
G
-invariant.

“
\supseteq
”: We have to show that any
x\in V_{+}(e_{0})
satisfies

z:=\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{e_{0}}(ix)\in\mathcal{T}_{M}^{+}
.
Since the orbit of
x
under

G^{e_{0}}
contains an element in
{\mathbb{R}}_{+}e_{1}
, we may assume that

x=x_{1}e_{1}
with
x_{1}>0
. Then

z=\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{e_{0}}(ix)=\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{e_{0}}(ix_{1}e_{1})=C(-x_{1}^{2})e_{0}+S(-x_{1}^{2})x_{1}ie_{1}=\cosh(x_{1})e_{0}+\sinh(x_{1})ie_{1}

and
x_{1}>0
imply

\Gamma(z)=(e_{0},\tanh(x_{1})e_{1})\in T(M)_{+},

hence that

z\in\mathcal{T}_{M}^{+}
.

“
\subseteq
”: Conversely, let
z=x+iy\in\mathcal{T}_{M}^{+}
.
Acting with
G
, we may thus assume that

y=y_{1}e_{1}
with
y_{1}>0
. Then
x\in y^{\bot}=e_{1}^{\bot}
follows from

\mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits\beta(z,z)=0
, so that (acting with
G^{e_{1}}
) we
may further assume that
x=x_{0}e_{0}
for some
x_{0}\not=0
.
Now
z=x_{0}e_{0}+iy_{1}e_{1}\in\mathop{{\rm AdS}}\nolimits^{d}_{\mathbb{C}}
implies that

1=\beta(z,z)=x_{0}^{2}-y_{1}^{2}.

Since

\Gamma(z)=\frac{1}{|x_{0}|}(x_{0}e_{0},y_{1}e_{1})\in T(M)_{+},

and
y_{1}>0
, we also have
x_{0}>0
.
Hence there exists a
t>0
with

y_{1}=\sinh t
and
x_{0}=\cosh t
. We then have

\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{e_{0}}(ite_{1})=\cosh(t)e_{0}+\sinh(t)ie_{1}=x_{0}e_{1}+y_{0}ie_{1}=z.

This proves (
122
).

For the second assertion, we start from

\mathcal{T}_{M}^{+}=G.\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{e_{0}}(iV_{+}(e_{0})),
which immediately shows that

(\mathcal{T}_{M}^{+})^{\overline{\tau}_{h}}\supseteq G^{\tau_{h}}.\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{e_{0}}(iV_{+}(e_{0})^{-\tau_{h}}),

where

V_{+}(e_{0})^{-\tau_{h}}=V_{+}(e_{0})\cap({\mathbb{R}}e_{1}+{\mathbb{R}}e_{2})=\{x_{1}e_{1}+x_{2}e_{2}\colon x_{1}>0,x_{1}>|x_{2}|\}.

We now show that we actually have equality.
So let
z=x+iy\in(\mathcal{T}_{M}^{+})^{\overline{\tau}_{h}}
.
Then

y=y_{1}e_{1}+y_{2}e_{2}\in V^{-\tau_{h}}
is
\beta
-positive, i.e.,

y_{1}^{2}>y_{2}^{2}
. Acting with

G^{h}
, we may thus assume that

x=x_{0}e_{0}
and
y=y_{1}e_{1}
.
From
1=\beta(z,z)=x_{0}^{2}-y_{1}^{2}
and

z=x+iy=x_{0}e_{0}+iy_{1}e_{1}\in\mathcal{T}_{M}^{+}
it now follows that

x_{0}y_{1}>0
.
Acting with the rotation
r_{01}(x)=(-x_{0},-x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{d})
,
which is contained in
G^{\tau_{h}}
, we may further assume that
x_{0}>0
, so that

y_{1}>0
holds as well.
Hence there exists a
t>0
with

y_{0}=\sinh(t)
and
x_{0}=\cosh(t)
. As above,
we now obtain

\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{e_{0}}(ite_{1})=\cosh(t)e_{0}+\sinh(t)ie_{1}=z.

This implies the second assertion.
∎

11.3
The wedge domains

For the closed convex
H
-invariant cone

C:=\overline{V_{+}(e_{0})}\subseteq T_{e_{0}}(M)\cong{\mathfrak{q}}
, we obtain

C_{+}=[0,\infty)(e_{2}+e_{1})\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ and }\hskip 10.00002ptC_{-}=[0,\infty)(e_{2}-e_{1}).

Lemma 112
.

We have

W_{M}(h)_{e_{0}}=G^{h}_{e}.\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{e_{0}}(C_{+}^{0}+C_{-}^{0})\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ and }\hskip 10.00002ptW_{M}(h)_{-e_{0}}=G^{h}_{e}.\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{-e_{0}}(-C_{+}^{0}-C_{-}^{0}).

Proof.

First we recall that

W_{M}(h)_{e_{0}}=G^{h}_{e}.\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{e_{0}}(C_{+}^{0}+C_{-}^{0})=G^{h}_{e}.\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{e_{0}}({\mathbb{R}}_{+}(e_{1}+e_{2})-{\mathbb{R}}_{+}(e_{1}-e_{2})).

For
g=r_{01}=\mathop{{\rm diag}}\nolimits(-1,-1,1,\ldots,1)\in G
we have

\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)h=-h
,
ge_{0}=-e_{0}
and

gC_{+}=C_{-}
. With Remark
47
we further obtain

\displaystyle W_{M}(h)_{-e_{0}}

\displaystyle=W_{M}(h)_{g.e_{0}}=g.W_{M}(-h)_{e_{0}}=gG^{h}_{e}.\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{e_{0}}(-(C_{+}^{0}+C_{-}^{0}))

\displaystyle=G^{h}_{e}.g\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{e_{0}}(-(C_{+}^{0}+C_{-}^{0}))=G^{h}_{e}.\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{-e_{0}}(-g.(C_{+}^{0}+C_{-}^{0}))

\displaystyle=G^{h}_{e}.\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{-e_{0}}(-(C_{+}^{0}+C_{-}^{0})).\qed

Lemma 113
.

The positivity domain

W_{M}^{+}(h)=\{x\in M\colon X_{h}(x)\in V_{+}(x)\}

of the vector field
X_{h}
satisfies

W_{M}^{+}(h)=G^{h}.\{x_{0}e_{0}+x_{2}e_{2}\in M\colon x_{0}x_{2}>0\}.

(123)

Proof.

If
X_{h}(x)\in V_{+}(x)
, then

x_{2}^{2}>x_{1}^{2}
implies that

x_{0}^{2}=1+\underbrace{x_{2}^{2}-x_{1}^{2}}_{>0}+x_{3}^{2}+\cdots+x_{d}^{2}>1+x_{3}^{2}+\cdots+x_{d}^{2}>0.

Acting with the centralizer
G^{h}
, which contains the group

\mathop{{\rm SO}}\nolimits_{1,d-2}^{\uparrow}({\mathbb{R}})
, acting on

\ker(h)=\mathop{{\rm span}}\nolimits\{e_{0},e_{3},\ldots,e_{d}\},

we may assume that
x_{3}=\cdots=x_{d}=0
, so that

x=(x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},0,\ldots,0)
is an element of
the
2
-dimensional anti-de Sitter space
\mathop{{\rm AdS}}\nolimits^{2}
.

7
7

7

As a
manifold
\mathop{{\rm AdS}}\nolimits^{2}
identifies naturally with
\mathop{{\rm dS}}\nolimits^{2}
, but the causal
structure is different.

Next we observe that the modular flow
\alpha_{t}
moves
any element
x
with
x_{2}^{2}>x_{1}^{2}
to an element with
x_{1}=0
.
For
x=(x_{0},0,x_{2},0,\ldots,0)\in\mathop{{\rm AdS}}\nolimits^{d}
we have

x_{0}^{2}>x_{2}^{2}
.
Now
X_{h}(x)=x_{2}e_{1}\in V_{+}(x)
is equivalent to

x_{0}x_{2}>0
(see (
118
)).
As
W_{M}^{+}(h)
is invariant under
G^{h}\supseteq\exp({\mathbb{R}}h)
, the assertion
follows.
∎

Lemma 114
.

The subset

W_{M}^{\rm KMS}(h):=\{x\in M\colon(\forall t\in(0,\pi))\ \alpha_{it}(x)\in\mathcal{T}_{M}^{+}\}

coincides with
W_{M}^{+}(h)
.

Proof.

For
x\in W_{M}^{\rm KMS}(h)
and
0<t<\pi
we have

\alpha_{it}(x)=(x_{0},\cos t\cdot x_{1}+\sin t\cdot ix_{2},\cos t\cdot x_{2}+\sin t\cdot ix_{1},x_{3},\ldots,x_{d}),

so that

\mathop{{\rm Im}}\nolimits(\alpha_{it}(x))=\sin t\cdot(0,x_{2},x_{1},0,\ldots,0).

This element is
\beta
-positive for every
t\in(0,\pi)

if and only if
x_{2}^{2}>x_{1}^{2}
.
Using the
G^{h}
-invariance of both sides,
we see as in the proof of Lemma
113

that we may assume that
x=(x_{0},0,x_{2},0,\ldots,0)
,
so that
x_{0}^{2}-x_{2}^{2}=1
and
x_{2}\not=0
. Then

z_{t}:=x_{t}+iy_{t}:=\alpha_{it}(x)=(x_{0},\sin t\cdot ix_{2},\cos t\cdot x_{2},0,\ldots,0)=x_{0}e_{0}+\cos t\cdot x_{2}e_{2}+\sin t\cdot x_{2}ie_{1}

is
G
-conjugate to

\mathop{{\rm sgn}}\nolimits(x_{0})\sqrt{x_{0}^{2}-\cos^{2}(t)x_{2}^{2}}\cdot e_{0}+\sin t\cdot ix_{2}e_{1}.

Hence
\Gamma(z_{t})\in T(M)_{+}
for
0<t<\pi
is equivalent to

x_{0}x_{2}>0
. In view of (
123
), this is equivalent to

x\in W_{M}^{+}(h)
. We conclude that

W_{M}^{+}(h)=W_{M}^{\rm KMS}(h).

∎

Lemma 115
.

W_{M}^{\rm KMS}(h)=\kappa_{h}\big((\mathcal{T}_{M}^{+})^{\overline{\tau}_{h}}\big).

Proof.

For
m\in W_{M}^{\rm KMS}(h)
we have

\overline{\tau}_{h}(\alpha_{\frac{\pi i}{2}}(m))=\alpha_{-\frac{\pi i}{2}}(\tau_{h}(m))=\alpha_{-\frac{\pi i}{2}}\alpha_{\pi i}(m)=\alpha_{\frac{\pi i}{2}}(m),

so that

\alpha_{\frac{\pi i}{2}}(m)\in(\mathcal{T}_{M}^{+})^{\overline{\tau}_{h}},\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ and thus }\hskip 10.00002ptW_{M}^{\rm KMS}(h)\subseteq\kappa_{h}\big((\mathcal{T}_{M}^{+})^{\overline{\tau}_{h}}\big).

To show that we actually have equality,
let
z=x+iy\in(\mathcal{T}_{M}^{+})^{\overline{\tau}_{h}}
.
Then

y=y_{1}e_{1}+y_{2}e_{2}\in V^{-\tau_{h}}
is
\beta
-positive, i.e.,

y_{1}^{2}>y_{2}^{2}
. Further,
\beta(x,x)>\beta(y,y)>0
.
Acting with

G^{h}
, we may thus assume that

x=x_{0}e_{0}
and
y=y_{1}e_{1}
.
From
1=\beta(z,z)=x_{0}^{2}-y_{1}^{2}
and

z=x+iy=x_{0}e_{0}+iy_{1}e_{1}\in\mathcal{T}_{M}^{+}
it now follows that

x_{0}y_{1}>0
. So

\kappa_{h}(z)=(x_{0},0,y_{1},0,\ldots,0)\in W_{M}^{+}(h)

follows from
x_{0}y_{1}>0
and (
123
).
This proves the assertion.
∎

The following theorem represents four different characterizations of the
wedge domain in
M
.

Theorem 116
.

For anti-de Sitter space
M=\mathop{{\rm AdS}}\nolimits^{d}
, we have the equalities

W_{M}^{+}(h)=W_{M}^{\rm KMS}(h)=\kappa_{h}\big((\mathcal{T}_{M}^{+})^{\overline{\tau}_{h}}\big)=W_{M}(h).

Proof.

The proof follows from the preceding five lemmas.
It only remains to show that

\kappa_{h}((\mathcal{T}_{M}^{+})^{\overline{\tau}_{h}}_{e_{0}})=W_{M}(h)_{e_{0}}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ and }\hskip 10.00002pt\kappa_{h}((\mathcal{T}_{M}^{+})^{\overline{\tau}_{h}}_{-e_{0}})=W_{M}(h)_{-e_{0}}.

The component

(\mathcal{T}_{M}^{+})^{\overline{\tau}_{h}}_{e_{0}}=G^{h}_{e}.\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{e_{0}}(iV_{+}(e_{0})^{-\tau_{h}})=G^{h}_{e}.\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{e_{0}}(i(C_{+}^{0}-C_{-}^{0}))

is mapped by
\kappa_{h}
to

G^{h}_{e}.\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{e_{0}}(C_{+}^{0}+C_{-}^{0})=W_{M}(h)_{e_{0}}.

For
g=(-1,-1,1,\ldots,1)
we have
g.e_{0}=-e_{0}
,

\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)h=-h
and
\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)C^{c}=C^{c}
. Now

(\mathcal{T}_{M}^{+})^{\overline{\tau}_{h}}_{-e_{0}}=G^{h}_{e}g_{0}.\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{e_{0}}(iV_{+}(e_{0})^{-\tau_{h}})

is mapped by
\kappa_{h}
to

\displaystyle G^{h}_{e}\kappa_{h}(g_{0}.\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{e_{0}}(iV_{+}(e_{0})^{-\tau_{h}}))

\displaystyle=G^{h}_{e}g_{0}\kappa_{h}^{-1}(\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{e_{0}}(iV_{+}(e_{0})^{-\tau_{h}}))

\displaystyle=G^{h}_{e}g_{0}\mathop{{\rm Exp}}\nolimits_{e_{0}}(-C_{+}^{0}-C_{-}^{0})=W_{M}(h)_{-e_{0}},

where the last equality follows from Lemma
112
.
This completes the proof.
∎

Appendix A
Some facts on symmetric Lie groups

In this section we collect some basic facts on
symmetric subgroups of a Lie group
G
and on Olshanski semigroups.

Lemma A1
.

Let
(G,\tau)
be a symmetric Lie group.
Then
G^{\sharp}:=\{g\in G\colon g^{\sharp}=g\}

is a submanifold of
G
containing
e
.
Its identity component is

G^{\sharp}_{e}=\{gg^{\sharp}\colon g\in G_{e}\}\cong G_{e}/(G_{e})^{\tau}.

For
g_{0}\in G^{\sharp}
and the involutive
automorphism
\sigma(g):=g_{0}\tau(g)g_{0}^{-1}
, the connected component
of
G^{\sharp}
containing
g_{0}
is

G^{\sharp}_{g_{0}}=\{gg_{0}g^{\sharp}\colon g\in G_{e}\}=\{g\sigma(g)^{-1}g_{0}\colon g\in G_{e}\}.

Proof.

In the group
G_{\tau}:=G\rtimes\{\mathop{{\rm id}}\nolimits_{G},\tau\}

the involutions form a submanifold
\mathop{{\rm Inv}}\nolimits(G_{\tau})

and
G_{e}
acts transitively on its connected components by conjugation
(
[
GN
, Ex. 4.6.12]
).
From

\mathop{{\rm Inv}}\nolimits(G_{\tau})\cap(G\times\{\tau\})=G^{\sharp}\rtimes\{\tau\},

we thus derive that
G^{\sharp}
is a submanifold of
G
and that

G_{e}.\tau=\{gg^{\sharp}\colon g\in G_{e}\}\times\{\tau\}

is a connected component of
\mathop{{\rm Inv}}\nolimits(G_{\tau})
.
Therefore
G^{\sharp}_{e}
is the
G_{e}
-orbit of
e
with
respect to the action given by
g.x:=gxg^{\sharp}
.
The same argument yields the description of
G^{\sharp}_{g_{0}}
.
∎

The following lemma could also be drawn from
[
KNÓ97
, §IV]
, but
for the sake of completeness we provide the complete arguments.

Lemma A2
.

Let
(G,\sigma)
be a connected symmetric Lie group,
let
L\subseteq G^{\sigma}
be an open subgroup, and let

C\subseteq{\mathfrak{g}}^{-\sigma}
be an open
\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(L)
-invariant hyperbolic
convex cone. Suppose further that
\exp_{G}
is injective on

{\mathfrak{z}}({\mathfrak{g}})\cap{\mathfrak{q}}
, so that we can form the real Olshanski semigroup

S:=L\exp(C)\subseteq G

for which the polar map
L\times C\to S,(g,x)\mapsto g\exp x
is a
homeomorphism.
Let
\tau
be an involutive automorphism of
G
commuting with
\sigma

such that
-\tau(C)=C
and put
g^{\sharp}:=\tau(g)^{-1}
.
Then the following assertions hold:

(1)

S
is
\sharp
-invariant and the subset

S^{\sharp}:=\{s\in S\colon s^{\sharp}=s\}
is invariant under the action of
S
on
itself by
s.t:=sts^{\sharp}
.

(2)

The projection

p\colon S\to L,p(g\exp x):=g

satisfies

p(s^{\sharp})=p(s)^{\sharp}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ and }\hskip 10.00002ptp(g.s)=gp(s)g^{\sharp}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ for }\hskip 10.00002pts\in S,g\in L.

(124)

(3)

The connected component
S^{\sharp}_{e}

of
S^{\sharp}
containing
\exp(C^{-\tau})
coincides
with

L_{e}.\exp(C^{-\tau})=\{g\exp(x)g^{\sharp}\colon g\in L_{e},x\in C^{-\tau}\}

and the map

L_{e}\times_{(L_{e})^{\tau}}C^{-\tau}\to S^{\sharp}_{e},\hskip 10.00002pt[g,x]\mapsto g\exp(x)g^{\sharp}

(125)

is a diffeomorphism.

(4)

S^{\sharp}_{e}=\{ss^{\sharp}\colon s\in S_{e}\}
.

(5)

For each connected component
\mathcal{C}
of
S^{\sharp}
there exists an
element
\ell_{0}\in L^{\sharp}\cap\mathcal{C}
such that the involution

\gamma(g):=\ell_{0}^{-1}\tau(g)\ell_{0}
of
G
satisfies

(a)

\gamma
commutes with
\sigma
.

(b)

-\gamma(C)=C
.

(c)

The connected component
\mathcal{C}
is of the form

S^{\sharp}_{\ell_{0}}=\ell_{0}\{s\gamma(s)^{-1}\colon s\in S_{e}\}=\bigcup_{\ell_{1}\in L_{e}}\ell_{0}\ell_{1}\exp(C^{-\gamma})\ell_{0}^{-1}\ell_{1}^{\sharp}\ell_{0},

which is diffeomorphic to
L_{e}\times_{(L_{e})^{\gamma}}C^{-\gamma}.

If
{\mathfrak{g}}
is semisimple, then
{\mathfrak{z}}({\mathfrak{g}})=\{0\}
,
so that the above condition on the exponential function is automatically
satisfied. It is also satisfied if
G
is simply connected.

Proof.

For the existence of real Olshanski semigroups, we refer to

[
La94
, Thm. 3.1]
. To see that the assumptions of this theorem are satisfied,
note that, as
Z(G)
is a
\sigma
-invariant closed subgroup, so is

Z(G)^{-\sigma}_{e}=\exp({\mathfrak{z}}({\mathfrak{g}})\cap{\mathfrak{q}})
. Therefore the injectivity of

\exp
on
{\mathfrak{z}}({\mathfrak{g}})\cap{\mathfrak{q}}
implies in particular that, for every

x\in{\mathfrak{q}}\cap{\mathfrak{z}}({\mathfrak{g}})
, the one-parameter subgroup
\exp({\mathbb{R}}x)
is closed
and isomorphic to
{\mathbb{R}}
.

(1) follows immediately from
L^{\sharp}=L
and

(\exp x)^{\sharp}=\exp(-\tau(x))\in\exp(C)
for
x\in C
.

(2) From the bijective polar decomposition
(cf. Lawson’s Theorem
[
Ne00
, XI.1.7]
and
[
La94
]
),
we obtain the map
p\colon S\to L
and
(
124
) is easily verified.

(3) By (2), the subset
S^{\sharp}_{e}
is
L_{e}
-invariant and contains
\exp(C^{-\tau})
. From (
124
) we get
p(S^{\sharp})\subseteq L^{\sharp}
,
and by continuity
p(S^{\sharp}_{e})\subseteq L^{\sharp}_{e}
.
As

L^{\sharp}_{e}=\{gg^{\sharp}\colon g\in L_{e}\}\cong L_{e}/(L_{e})^{\tau}

by Lemma
A1
, the group
L_{e}
acts transitively on
L^{\sharp}_{e}
.
With (
124
) we conclude that

S^{\sharp}_{e}=L_{e}.p^{-1}(e),\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ where }\hskip 10.00002ptp^{-1}(e)=\exp(C^{-\tau}).

This proves the first part of (3). For the second part we observe that

g_{1}\exp(x_{1})g_{1}^{\sharp}=g_{2}\exp(x_{2})g_{2}^{\sharp}
is equivalent to

g_{2}^{-1}g_{1}\in L^{\tau}
(which implies

(g_{2}^{-1}g_{1})^{\sharp}=(g_{2}^{-1}g_{1})^{-1}
), and
\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g_{2}^{-1}g_{1})x_{1}=x_{2}
.
Therefore the map in (
125
) is a bijection and the
invertibility of its differential implies that
it is a diffeomorphism.

(4) Clearly
ss^{\sharp}\in S^{\sharp}_{e}
for every
s\in S_{e}
.
Hence it suffices to show that every
g\in(S^{\sharp})_{e}

is of the form
ss^{\sharp}
for some
s\in S_{e}
. Since both sides are

L_{e}
-invariant, we may assume that
g=\exp(2x)
for some
x\in C^{-\tau}
.
Then the assertion follows with
s:=\exp x
.

(5) Let
S^{\sharp}_{c}\subseteq S^{\sharp}
be a connected component
and
\ell_{0}\in p(S^{\sharp}_{c})
.
As
\ell_{0}\in L\subseteq G^{\sigma}
, the automorphism

\gamma
of
G
is an involution which commutes with
\sigma
.
Further
-\gamma(C)=C
follows from the fact
that
C
is
\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(\ell_{0})
-invariant. Therefore
\gamma
satisfies
both assumptions made for
\tau
. Next we observe that

\ell_{0}=\ell_{0}^{\sharp}
leads to

(\ell_{0}s)^{\sharp}=s^{\sharp}\ell_{0}^{\sharp}=\ell_{0}(\ell_{0}^{-1}s^{\sharp}\ell_{0})=\ell_{0}\gamma(s)^{-1}

implies that

(\ell_{0}S)^{\sharp}=\ell_{0}\{s\in S\colon\gamma(s)^{-1}=s\}.

The remaining assertions now follow from (3), applied to the involution

\gamma
instead of
\tau
.
∎

Remark A3
.

The preceding lemma extends to connected real Olshanski semigroups

\Gamma_{L}(C)=L\exp(C)

not contained in a group
G
(cf.
[
Ne00
, Def. XI.1.11]
).
Here
({\mathfrak{g}},\sigma)
is a symmetric Lie algebra,
L
is a connected Lie group
with Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{l}}={\mathfrak{g}}^{\sigma}

to which the adjoint action of
{\mathfrak{l}}
on
{\mathfrak{g}}^{-\sigma}
integrates,
and
C\subseteq{\mathfrak{g}}^{-\sigma}
is a pointed generating
\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(L)
-invariant
(weakly) hyperbolic cone.

If
G
is the simply connected Lie group with Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
,
these semigroups are constructed by first obtaining

\Gamma_{\widetilde{L}}(C)
, where
q_{L}\colon\widetilde{L}\to L
is the universal covering,
as a the simply connected covering of
\Gamma_{G^{\sigma}}(C)=G^{\sigma}\exp(C)\subseteq G
, and then factoring the discrete central
subgroup
\ker(q_{L})\cong\pi_{1}(L)\subseteq Z(\widetilde{L})

(see Definition
41
and
[
Ne00
, §XI.1]
for more details).

Appendix B
The topology on
\mathcal{H}^{\infty}
and the
space
\mathcal{H}^{-\infty}

Let
(U,\mathcal{H})
be a unitary representation of
G
.
A
smooth vector
is an element
\eta\in\mathcal{H}
for which the orbit map

U^{\eta}:G\to\mathcal{H},g\mapsto U(g)\eta

is smooth. We write
\mathcal{H}^{\infty}=\mathcal{H}^{\infty}(U)
for the space
of smooth vectors. It carries the
derived representation

{\tt d}U
of the Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}
given by

{\tt d}U(x)\eta=\lim_{t\to 0}\frac{U(\exp tx)\eta-\eta}{t}.

(126)

We extend this representation to a homomorphism

{\tt d}U\colon\mathcal{U}({\mathfrak{g}})\to\mathop{{\rm End}}\nolimits(\mathcal{H}^{\infty}),

where
\mathcal{U}({\mathfrak{g}})
is the complex enveloping algebra of
{\mathfrak{g}}
. This algebra
carries an involution
D\mapsto D^{*}
determined uniquely by

x^{*}=-x
for
x\in{\mathfrak{g}}
.
For
D\in\mathcal{U}({\mathfrak{g}})
, we obtain a seminorm on
\mathcal{H}^{\infty}
by

p_{D}(\eta)=\|{\tt d}U(D)\eta\|\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ for }\hskip 10.00002pt\eta\in\mathcal{H}^{\infty}.

These seminorms define a topology on
\mathcal{H}^{\infty}
which turn the injection

\eta\colon{\cal H}^{\infty}\to{\cal H}^{{\cal U}({\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbb{C}})},\hskip 10.00002pt\xi\mapsto({\tt d}U(D)\xi)_{D\in{\cal U}({\mathfrak{g}}_{\mathbb{C}})}

(127)

into a topological embedding, where the right hand side carries the product
topology (cf.
[
Mag92
, 3.19]
).
Then
\mathcal{H}^{\infty}
becomes a complete locally convex space
for which the linear operators
{\tt d}U(D)
,
D\in\mathcal{U}({\mathfrak{g}})
, are continuous.
Since
\mathcal{U}({\mathfrak{g}})
has a countable basis,
countably many such seminorms already determine the topology, so that

\mathcal{H}^{\infty}
is metrizable, hence a Fréchet space.
We also observe that
the inclusion
\mathcal{H}^{\infty}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}
is continuous.

The space
\mathcal{H}^{\infty}
of smooth vectors is
G
-invariant
and we denote the corresponding representation by
U^{\infty}
. We have
the intertwining relation

{\tt d}U(\mathop{{\rm Ad}}\nolimits(g)x)=U^{\infty}(g){\tt d}U(x)U^{\infty}(g)^{-1}\hskip 10.00002pt\mbox{ for }\hskip 10.00002ptg\in G,x\in{\mathfrak{g}}.

If
\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G)
and
\xi\in\mathcal{H}
then

U(\varphi)\xi=\int_{G}U(g)\varphi(g)\,dg\in\mathcal{H}^{\infty}
.
A sequence
(\varphi_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
in
C^{\infty}_{c}(G)
is called a

\delta
-sequence
if
\varphi_{n}\geq 0

and
\int_{G}\varphi_{n}(g)\,dg=1
for all
n\in{\mathbb{N}}

and for every
e
-neighborhood
V\subseteq G
, we have

\mathop{{\rm supp}}\nolimits(\varphi_{n})\subseteq V
if
n
is sufficiently large.
If
(\varphi_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
is a

\delta
-sequence, then
U(\varphi_{n})\xi\to\xi
, so that
\mathcal{H}^{\infty}

is dense in
\mathcal{H}
.

We write
\mathcal{H}^{-\infty}
for the space
of continuous anti-linear functionals on
\mathcal{H}^{\infty}
.
Its elements are called
distribution vectors
.
The group
G
,
\mathcal{U}({\mathfrak{g}})
and
C^{\infty}_{c}(G)
act on
\eta\in\mathcal{H}^{-\infty}
by

•

(U^{-\infty}(g)\eta)(\xi):=\eta(U(g^{-1})\xi)
,
g\in G,\xi\in\mathcal{H}^{\infty}
.

If
U\colon G\to\mathop{{\rm AU}}\nolimits(\mathcal{H})
is an antiunitary
representation and
U(g)
is antiunitary, then we have to modify
this definition slightly by

(U^{-\infty}(g)\eta)(\xi):=\overline{\eta(U(g^{-1})\xi)}
.

•

({\tt d}U^{-\infty}(D)\eta)(\xi):=\eta({\tt d}U(D^{*})\xi)
,
D\in\mathcal{U}({\mathfrak{g}}),\xi\in\mathcal{H}^{\infty}
.

•

U^{-\infty}(\varphi)\eta=\eta\circ U^{\infty}(\varphi^{*})
,

\varphi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(G).

We have natural
G
-equivariant linear embeddings

\mathcal{H}^{\infty}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}\smash{\mathop{\hbox to60.0pt{\rightarrowfill}}\limits^{\xi\mapsto|\xi\rangle}}\mathcal{H}^{-\infty}.

(128)

For each
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G)
, the map

U(\varphi)\colon\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}^{\infty}

is continuous, so that its adjoint defines a weak-
*
-continuous map

U^{-\infty}(\varphi^{*})\colon\mathcal{H}^{-\infty}\to\mathcal{H}
. We actually have

U^{-\infty}(\varphi)\mathcal{H}^{-\infty}\subseteq\mathcal{H}^{\infty}

as a consequence of the
Dixmier–Malliavin Theorem
[
DM78
, Thm. 3.1]
,
which asserts that every
\varphi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G)
is a sum of functions
of the form
\varphi_{1}*\varphi_{2}
with
\varphi_{j}\in C^{\infty}_{c}(G)
.
